the conditions of life of the ordinary people
in a small region of the Mughal Empire from
about the accession of Akbar (1556) to the
collapse of Mjughal power (1739). The study
brings out ho\v the Mughal administration

functioned on the ground, how peasant
communities were organized and religious men
conducted th|ir worldly affairs. We are even
able to meet ^ome lowly men and women as
distinct individuals.

Some interesting facts also emerge: the early
dates from which Akbar’s grants to temples
began; the extensive survey of temples in Braj
heartland that Akbar ordered in 1598; and,
late in Aurangzeb’s reign (1704), the official
levy of Re. 1 per annum on each village in the
Braj region to be collected by the Chaitanya

matter should find much of interest in
the reconstructed history of three villages
separately studied (Vrindavan, Radhakund
and Rajpur). At the end, there are biographies
of Gosains of the Chaitanya sect mainly
containing details of their worldly concerns.

The book useis a mass of privately
preserved documents in Persian and Braj,
along with historical texts, travel accounts,

                              Preface



An account of how this volume came to be planned and then written,
largely on the basis of material collected by the co-author, the late
Dr Tarapada Mukherjee, is set out in some detail in the Introduction,
and so on it nothing need be said here. What needs to be recorded is
my sense of gratitude for the very kind approval given to the enterprise
by Dr (Mrs) Emma Mukherjee, herself a distinguished physician, when,
after her husband’s passing away, I explained the project to her in 1991.
Unfortunately, she too did not live to see the completion of work on
this volume. I am most grateful to the two daughters of Dr Tarapada
Mukherjee, Mrs Mimi Ray and Mrs Rimi Mukherjee Mehra, for having
so readily agreed to the publication of this joint work, when I approached
them for their consent.
   I recall with much gratitude the kindness of Shri Shrivats Goswami,
a leading divine of Vrindavan, who personally brought a heavy load of
photographs of the documents sent to me from London by Dr Tarapada
Mukherjee, that constitute the basic material for this book.
   In preparing the final text of the volume, I have been greatly aided by
the suggestions and comments of Professor Sayera I. Habib who has gone
through all the chapters of this volume.
   Faiz Habib, a skilled cartographer, has drawn the four maps. Chapter 2
originated as a paper co-authored by him.
   Professor Shireen Moosvi has organized the entire processing of the
text under the auspices of the Aligarh Historians Society (AHS), of which
she is the Secretary. I am most grateful for the many suggestions that she
has made while going through the processed text.
   Professor S.A. Nadeem Rezavi, Chairman, Department of History,
has organized the scanning of the entire set of documents, photographs
of which had been supplied to me by Dr Mukherjee. I am particularly
satisfied that they are now preserved and available for use by bona-fide
researchers.
xii                               Preface

    The text of the volume has been successively processed over the years
under the auspices of the AHS, by Mr Muneeruddin Khan, Ms Nazma
Khan, Mr Zeeshan Khan and Mr Saif Ali Khan, to all of whom sincere
thanks are due.
    I am very happy that Mr B.N. Varma, an old friend, agreed on behalf
of Primus Books to publish this book. Mr Benedict Joseph O’Hagan has
won my gratitude not only by his diligent copy-editing but also for giving
way whenever I preferred my English to King’s English. Now that the
book is in the readers hands, it would be too bold for me to claim that
it anywhere approaches ‘definitive history’ which Dr Tarapada Mukherjee
once hoped the rich information contained in the documents he had
collected could lead to. But if the reader is able to find some information
of a kind not usually to be encountered in conventional histories, I for one
would be satisfied.

Aligarh                                                       Irfan Habib
November 2019
                         Introduction
                               Irfan Habib




It was largely owing to the vision and effort of the co-author of the present
volume, the late Dr Tarapada Mukherjee (1928-90), that an exceptionally
large amount of very valuable documentary material became available to
the historians of Mughal India. Tarapada Mukherjee’s interest in Bengali
literature and its history made him choose Brajbuli poetry of the Bengali
Vaishnavites for the subject of his doctoral research; and his interests
widened from what the Vaishnavites wrote in Bengali and Sanskrit to the
authors themselves, notably the gosvdmls (usually spelt ‘goswdmis) of the
Chaitanya sect at Vrindavan. This ultimately led to a dedicated effort on
his part to collect all the material that he could track down which might
illumine for us the lives and activities of these Bengali divines in a region
so distant from Bengal.


The Documents

So in 1978 Dr Mukherjee, a member of the faculty of the School of
Oriental and African Studies (SOAS), University of London, organized,
under auspices of the SOAS and with the support of the Vrindavan
Research Institute, a campaign to photograph all the old documents
that could be found in possession of the Chaitanya Goswamis (‘Gosa’ins’
in Braj) and their temples. The archives of the Govind-dev temples of
Vrindavan and Jaipur and the Radhakund temple were photographed,
as were the holdings of the Madan Mohan and Radha Damodar temples
deposited in the Vrindavan Research Institute (VRI), Vrindavan. Since
many documents had been submitted to courts in the course of litigation,
Dr Mukherjee’s team obtained permission to photograph documents
2                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 kept in case files stored in the strong room of the Mathura District court.
 (Unfortunately documents so retrieved are not identified as such in the
 photographs given to me).1 An important addition to the collection came
 in the late 1980s when the Institute of Vaishnav Studies, USA, provided
 him with a thick file of xeroxed copies of documents obtained from the
 Gopinath temples of Vrindavan and Jaipur.
    It was my good fortune to receive a letter from Dr Tarapada Mukherjee,
 dated 20 October 1986, in which he asked if we could collaborate on
 the Persian portion of the collection. So commenced our joint effort.
 I received, through his kindness and the aid of Shri Shrivats Goswami
 of Vrindavan, photographs and xerox copies of a large number of
Persian (including bilingual) documents; and Professor J.C. Wright, Dr
Mukherjee’s collaborator in London, very kindly furnished me with his
decipherments and translations of over thirty Braj documents.
    To this material I was able to add, with the assistance of Mr Zakir
Husain of the National Archives, copies of documents that the National
Archives of India, New Delhi, had acquired from sources in Vrindavan.
Later on I had occasion to consult and transcribe (from xerox copies
furnished to me) some of the National Archives’ further acquisitions
from Vrindavan.
    Dr Mukherjee and I published three joint papers in 1987, 1988, and
 1989-90, dealing with Mughal imperial grants to temples and priests,
from the time of Akbar to that of Shahjahan, and with the evidence that
the Vrindavan documents yielded about the nature of land rights in the
area during the reign of Akbar. These papers form Chapters 3, 4 and 6
of this volume.
    Struck by cancer which he faced with incredible bravery, Tarapada
Mukherjee passed away on 7 July 1990, His death left me alone to
continue with the analysis of the material I had been provided with.
There is no doubt that I have taken far too long a time (nearly three
decades) to come out with a volume that can claim to fulfil at least part of
Tarapada Mukherjee’s ambitious plan. My plea in my own defence is that
for this work to be completed, I needed to decipher, transcribe, arrange,
understand, and analyse a very large amount of material, mostly in the
Persian cursive script; and further that with Dr Mukherjee passing away,
I no longer had the advantage of his aid and guidance in the pursuit of
our joint enterprise.


   1 In a letter dated 18 January 1989, Dr Tarapada Mukherjee informed me that
photographs of all the documents in temple collections as well as the Mathura
Court’s strong room were deposited in the VRI as well as the library of SOAS,
London.
                               Introduction                              3

   A reader is bound to expect that there would be full and adequate
indications of sources in the volume that has come out of this effort.
References to printed texts and holdings of the National Archives have
posed no problems. But many of the photographs of documents Dr
Mukherjee sent me give no indication of the collection that they came
from. Often, when the collection is indicated, no number is given to locate
the document within that collection. Some photographs bear numbers;
others do not, and sometimes the numbers marked on the obverse and
reverse vary. It seemed to me that in such a situation the best way of
securing wider academic access to this collection was to transcribe the
documents on separate sheets and arrange them chronologically, so that
the text of a document can be traced simply by reference to its date. This
work has been completed, and from the photographs and xeroxed copies
received by me from Dr Tarapada Mukherjee all documents assignable
to the period down to about 1760 have been transcribed and arranged in
rough chronological order. The photographs and xerox copies collected
by Dr Tarapada Mukherjee have now been scanned at the Department of
History, Aligarh Muslim University, the collection being appropriately
named after him. Hopefully, my transcripts will also be scanned or
xeroxed and, like the photographs of the originals, be made available to
researchers. In such expectation, the Vrindavan documents are cited by
their transcript numbers in Chapter 5, which happens to be the piece
written last for this volume.


Their Value

It may, perhaps, not be out of place here to offer an assessment of the
historical value of the Vrindavan documents. There, of course, exist large
collections of official Mughal-period documents such as at Hyderabad
(the Telangana State Archives), and in the Inayat Jang collection at the
National Archives, New Delhi. We, then, have the rich material in the
Rajasthan State Archives, Bikaner, and the private family collections
obtained by the U.P. State Archives, Allahabad/Lucknow. Other State
Archives too are known to be rich in Mughal-period material. But in
terms of the variety of transactions with which it deals, the Vrindavan
collection, in its several repositories, might yet be unique. Contrary to
some assumptions in recent historical writing about the limitations of
power of the pre-colonial state, the Vrindavan Gosa’ins seem to have
found in the Mughal state an effective protector of their property rights
in both town and country, which made them anxious to get the terms
of their pettiest transactions recorded in Persian. There were parallel
4                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

authorities (the jagirdar, the imperial officials and the qdzi, for instance)
to whom appeals could be made, a multiplicity that set limits to local
despotism. Even in the religious sphere there was much greater ease
in ordinary life than what the official annals of Aurangzeb’s time, for
example, might suggest to have been the case. This does not exculpate
the Mughal Empire from being an apparatus of fiscal exploitation, but it
does weaken many other accusations against it, such as those of despotic
tyranny, and of lacking regulatory constraints.
   Another virtue of these documents is that through them we are enabled
to meet a number of individual men (and at least one woman, Krishna
Priya), mostly Gosa ins, whose biographies we can partly reconstruct (see
Chapter 10 of this volume). We even meet some peasants as historical
persons, whose genealogy, age at a particular time, aspects of appearance
and, sometimes, field-holdings we can learn about. Other classes are also
not left unrepresented, from merchants and bankers making gifts to the
gosa’ins to a poor woman Isarwi, seeking recompense from a gosd’in for
the destruction of her father’s hut. There is much to interest us too as
far as relations between Hindus and Muslims are concerned. We can
even seek evidence for the extent of ravages by smallpox from the huliyas
(descriptions of physical appearances) of a large number of individuals
prepared for legal purposes. We can also picture what some townships
or villages were like, such as Vrindavan, Radhakund and Rajpur, and
construct a history, even if fragmentary, of their inhabitants.
   As texts not prepared for public viewing, the limitations of our
material must also be recognized. Only such documents were deliberately
preserved as could influence recognition of the preservers’ ownership
rights and privileges or monetary and proprietary claims. Documents
outside this range are often chance survivals. Yet, with these undoubted
limitations the Vrindavan documents remain unique, for ordinary people
enter the record here to a far greater extent than in any other collection
of Mughal-period documents so far known to us. For all this, should we
not be ever grateful to the Gosa ins who preserved these papers and to the
late Dr Tarapada Mukherjee for so devotedly bringing them together?


Plan of the Volume

When Dr Tarapada Mukherjee and I began our collaborative effort, we had
in mind a rough plan to proceed with our studies, both thematically and
chronologically, and simultaneously to prepare calendars of documents
                               Introduction                               5

relevant to each theme and period that we happened to select. Three long
papers (Chapters 3, 4, and 6 in this volume) were jointly written by us
along these lines.
   After his passing away, one ambitious element of the plan, viz., the
preparation of calendars of the documents, had to be abandoned, for
the reason of the sheer length of time the work would have taken.
Translations and calendars already jointly prepared by us have, however,
been retained (in Chapters 3, 4, and 6).
   Though the writing (and revision) of the different parts of this
volume has been spread over thirty years, it hopefully fulfils much of
the rest of our original plan. Chapter 1 is designed to offer the reader
a general view of Braj Bhum in Mughal times by making use of both
the Vrindavan documents and other sources, Chapter 2 being its more
technical supplement. The next three chapters (3 to 5) concern the
Mughal administration in Braj Bhum and its conduct during the period
as it appears in our documents. The subsequent four chapters (6 to 9)
deal with the peasantry and agrarian society, one (Chapter 6) dealing
specifically with land rights as revealed by documents of Akbar’s reign
and the other three (Chapters 7, 8 and 9) being concerned with peasants
and other classes in three (originally) rural settlements, viz., Vrindavan,
Arith and Rajpur. The concluding section comprises the last chapter
(10), which contains biographical notices of the Gosa’ins themselves as
reconstructed from documents of the period, c.1550 to c.1750. Since such
biographical particulars are essential for understanding the contexts of
many of our documents, I have adopted a liberal, rather than restrictive
definition of ‘Gosa in’ in this chapter, practically accepting every one as
such who chose to adopt this designation.
   While I hope I have at least partly fulfilled the design that Dr Tarapada
Mukherjee had in mind, there are still problems with this volume which
with his keen eyes he would have surely spotted. One arises from the fact
that each of the ten papers that now appear as chapters were originally
written as practically independent pieces. This meant that when brought
together for publication in their original form, the reader, while going
through them, would have encountered a great deal of repetition. For
example, the collections of Vrindavan documents were described at
the beginning of every paper. Such repetition has now been avoided by
transferring the description of the sources to this Introduction. Less
easy has it been to deal with such transactions and events as have been
touched upon in more than one chapter because of their relevance in
the respective places. To the extent possible I have sought to reduce
6                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

the repetitive element by deleting some parts of the original papers and
shortening descriptions where they seemed too extensive; but a reader
may still feel that more could have been done in this respect, and that
Chapters 1 and 2, for example, should have been fully assimilated. I felt,
however, that, while desirable, an attempt to rewrite the text on such a
scale would have set back the volume’s publication for a long time and
would not by itself have added anything further to knowledge.
   A final word here. The entire text, including the three papers that
Dr Tarapada Mukherjee and I jointly wrote, has undergone considerable
revision, addition, and correction, as further reading of the material
yielded more facts and clarified certain matters, while fresh scrutiny of
Survey maps improved place-identifications. Additions also had to be
made to the calendars of documents attached to Chapters 4 and 6. Thus
to a great extent the volumes different chapters should be taken not as
mere reproductions of the original papers, but as their new versions.

Forms of Names and Transliteration

Since the bulk of our documents are in Persian, one must recognize that
the Persian script has certain problems in reproducing Indian names.
First, there are no letters in Persian to represent Nagari letters d, the
hard r, and the harsh       and, second, short vowels (a, e, i, u) following
upon consonants are not indicated in normal Persian writing, while dai,
de and di are all written the same way. Further, g’ (as in ‘gun’) is not
often distinguished from ‘k’; and j’ often does duty for ‘ch’ as well. It is,
therefore, reasonable for one to prefer the form of a name as given in Braj
documents over the form found in Persian writing, so long as the name
is not derived from Arabic or Persian. No effort has been made in this
volume to restore or use the Sanskrit forms of personal names. So we have
read Seo Ram or Shiv Ram for the same person, both forms being found
in our Persian and Braj documents, but not Shiva Rama, or Siva Rama,
the usual transliteration of the Sanskrit form of that name; and so, to take
another instance, we have Bijai Singh, not Vijaya Simha.
   As far as transliteration in general is concerned, the Braj words and
names are rendered largely in accordance with the system adopted by
Platts, except that we have ‘sE and 'dJ for Platts ‘s’ and ‘sh’. For Persian (and
Arabic) the system of transliteration used in Steingass’s Persian-English
Dictionary has been followed, with some simplifications. To represent the
Arabic-Persian letter waw, we have used ‘v’ and ‘w’ indifferently. Thus
we have both the spellings vakil and wakil to represent the same Persian/
Arabic word.
    We have followed present-day official spellings of place names in the
case ofwell-known cities and towns, e.g. Agra, Delhi, Mathura, Vrindavan,
usually without applying diacritical marks. Where the spellings in our
documents are different from these, we use the earlier forms only when
quoting or summarizing those documents, e.g. Bindraban (often ‘corrected’
by us to ‘Brindaban’) for Vrindavan; and in such cases diacritical marks are
8                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

applied. We tend to prefer the earlier forms, where different from present
ones, e.g. ‘Arith’ over Radhakund (which too is used especially when
found alone in documents).
   For rivers the present-day official forms are used: thus we have in our
own text Yamuna, not Jamna, the name still in popular use.
   Some slight variations from the mode of transliteration here outlined
occur in Chapter 10, for which see the initial section of that chapter.


Dates

The dates in the Persian documents are given in Hijri and Ilahi eras. The
latter was officially followed from the late years of Akbar to 1637-38. It
was a solar era, with numbered years of each reign, beginning with the
first nauroz after each accession. Shahjahan shifted to the Hijri calendar
in 1637, but henceforth Hijri dates also began to be recalled in terms of
regnal years, each set of regnal years beginning with the first day of the
month specific to each reign. Our conversion of Hijri into Christian era
dates (Gregorian) is based on H.G. Cattenoz, Tables de Concordance des
eres Chre'tienne et Hegirienne, Paris, 1954. It should be borne in mind that
dates so obtained could vary from the actual dates, by one or two days,
since in India local moon sightings were used to set the beginning of each
month. Similarly, in our conversion of Ilahi into Christian era dates a
margin of error of + 1 day needs to be allowed, since, though it was based
on a solar calendar like the Christian era, the initial day of the Ilahi year,
namely, the Nauroz, did not necessarily fall on 21 March every year as we
have assumed. Finally, we have usually followed Professor J.C. Wright in
his conversion of Vikrama-Samvat dates found in Braj documents into
Christian era dates.


Short Glossary of Terms relating to Persian Documents

The following terms occur in the text of this volume more than once, and
it may be convenient for the reader to have the explanation of each such
term furnished here.

Bai'nama            Sale-deed.
Chakndma            Document demarcating limits of agricultural holding
                    or area of land-grant.
                              Introduction                               9

Dastak             Order granting permission or protection, e.g. road­
                   pass.
Farmdn             Imperial rescript, bearing the Emperor’s seal above the
                   text on the right side.
Hasbul hukm        Order usually issued by a minister on the Emperor’s
                   instructions.
Hiba-ndma          Deed of hiba or gift requiring immediate transfer of
                   gifted assets to designated beneficiary.
Mahzar             Statement of facts in relation to any matter or event,
                   usually signed or endorsed by a number of persons.
Nishan             Order issued by a prince of the imperial family.
Parwdna,           Order by an official usually issued to a subordinate or
 parwancha         set of subordinates.
Risdla             Report, communication.
                   Receipt, bond. Note: Acknowledgements of debt as
                   well as transferable bills of exchange (hundis) could be
                   deemed tamassuks.
Tashiha            Certificate verifying a grant or concession.
Tughra             Highly stylized writing, used to form a square or
                   rectangle, containing the name and title of the
                   emperor, set above the main text of a farmdn.
Zimn               Text on the back of a farmdn or other official order,
                   containing the "file’ on the matter at issue, consisting
                   of texts of earlier documents, if any, additional
                   particulars, remarks of officials and, placed usually at
                   the top, a record of the final processing of the case.

BRAJ BHUM
                              CHAPTER 1



           Braj Bhum in Mughal Times
                                 Irfan Habib




The Name and Limits of the Region

When ‘Vraja’, to give to ‘Braj’ its Sanskrit form, came into use as the name
of the territory around Mathura, containing the sites of Lord Krishna’s
youthful adventures, is difficult to establish. It does not seem to have been
used in ancient Indian texts as a territorial name, its proper sense being
that of cattle-shed, herd, flock, etc.1 A popular verse in the local dialect,
quoted by H.M. Elliot in 1844, speaks of‘Braj chaurasi, as if there were
eighty-four villages in the Braj territory.12 In the Vrindavan documents,
the first reference to it as a territory appears to occur in a Braj deed of vs
1651/ad 1594, recording the sale of a piece of land in Vrindavan by the
village headmen (panch), who are all said to be ‘Brajbasi’, natives of‘Braj’
(Wright 6); and later in vs 1697/ad 1640 when Ranajit and six others

    1 See Monier Monier-Williams, Sanskrit-English Dictionary, 2nd edn.,
Oxford, 1899, p. 1042 (s.v. vraja).
    2 For the traditional perceptions of Braj territory see H.M. Elliot, Memoirs
- • - of the Races of the North-Western Provinces, revised and edited by John
Beames, London, 1869, vol. II, pp. 57-8 n., and F.S. Growse, Mathura: a District
Memoir, orig. pub. 1882, repr., New Delhi, 1979, pp. 80-91. The popular belief,
as Elliot noted in the present case, was that chaurasi signified the inclusion in
Braj territory of all places within a radius of 84 kos from Mathura. But this is not
the usual sense attached to the term chaurasi, which simply means a group of 84
villages (see ibid., vol. II, pp. 47-55). Braj may properly be defined in religious
terms as the territory containing the main spots of the Krishna lore, for which
sec A.W, Entwistle, Braj: Centre of Krishna Pilgrimage, Groningen, 1987.
14                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

of the same township, selling land to a gosai (goswdmi), call themselves
‘samast Birajbdsi, indicating thereby that all of them belong to Biraj or
Braj (Wright 8). However, there is little indication in our documents
about the extent of the territory going by the name Braj until the name
Braj Bhum, ‘Braj land’, makes its appearance in 1704, in a parwana of
Mukhtar Khan, Governor of the province of Agra, where a list of parganas
or sub-districts constituting it is also furnished: thirteen on the right side
of the Jamna (Yamuna) and five on the left.34
   Except for Anupnagar (mod. Anupshahr), all the parganas in this
‘official’ list lie within the region of the Braj dialect. The name ‘Braj’,
adopted by linguists for the dialect is, however, recent. Banarsldas, who
wrote his Ardh-kathanak (1641) in Braj, designates the language he was
writing in as either des-bhasbd or just bhdshdj whereas in the Tubfatu’l
Hind, written in the late years of Aurangzebs reign, it is simply called
bhdkha (‘kh’ being the usual transcription in Persian of the hard Sanskrit
‘sh’).5 (For the language employed in documents presumably intended to
be read out to ordinary rural folk, see the closing portion of Chapter 7,
‘The Peasants of Vrindavan’).
   It is clear, then, that ‘Braj’ as the name for the territory around
Mathura had a religious, not a linguistic, connotation, being the area in
which pilgrimages were to be made to places that were related in tradition
to episodes in the Krishna legend. Since traditions, as well as names,
disappear, revive, or are invented, the late dates of the use of Braj in a
territorial sense raises the question whether such use of it was not a gift
of Shri Chaitanya and his followers. One recalls that the poetry about
Krishna and Radha that the Chaitanya sect inspired in Bengal in the
latter part of the sixteenth century, and which later was composed in


    3 Vrindavan Docs. (G.D.): copy of original with attestation by the qazi whose
seal bears the year ah 1121/ad 1709. The same list of parganas is given in a
statement on behalf of the zaminddrs, etc., of these parganas, made after 1710
(also in G.D. Coll.).
    4 Banarsldas, Ardhakathanak, text printed by Mukund Lath, in Appendix II
to his English translation of the work, Haifa Tale, Jaipur, 1981, pp. 223-75. Lath
uniformly renders the words des-bhasba and bhdsha as ‘Hindi’ in his translation.
    ' See, for example, Mirza Khan (Jan?), Tubfatu’l Hind (c.1707), Preface. I have

Department of History, AMU, Acc. No. 67, of this valuable work on Indian arts,
manners, and language, with particular reference to what today would be called
the Braj region. I have not been able to use the printed text, ed. Nurul Hasan
Ansari, Tehran, 1968.
map   1        BRAJ BHUM, 1704

a form of Bengali verse called Brajbuli, ‘the Braj speech’.6 In his order
(parwdna), Mukhtar Khan, Governor of Agra, noted particularly that
Rup and Haridas (early disciples of Shri Chaitanya), who had established
themselves at Bindraban (the usual local form of the name Vrindavan),
which was then ‘all jungle and uninhabited’, had enjoined their followers
and successors ‘to worship Divinity, to travel within Braj Bhum and not
to pursue mendicancy and residence anywhere outside Braj Bhum’.7 This
also bears out the special position of sanctity that Braj Bhum held for
the Chaitanya goswamis (‘gosa’ins’ or ‘gusa’ins’ in Vrindavan documents),
so that their pilgrimage circuits defined its limits, largely confining it to
the eighteen parganas, listed by Mukhtar Khan, all but two of which lay
within the northern parts of the Agra suba (province).
   The eighteen parganas listed by Mukhtar Khan as belonging to Braj
Bhum were, west of the Yamuna: (1) Mathura, (2) Sahar, (3) Mangotla
(Maghora), (4) 01, (5) Ao, (6) Pahari, (7) Kaman, (8) Khoh or Koh Mujahid,
(9) Noh, (10) Khohri, (11) Hodal, (12) Palwal, and (13) Faridabad; and
east of Yamuna: (14) Mahaban (including tappa Mat), (15) Sa'dabad, (16)
Jalesar, (17) Koi (Aligarh), and (18) Anupnagar (Anupshahr)8*11 (see Map 1).

    Since sarkdr Delhi belonged to suba Dehli, it is not clear what authority
Mukhtar Khan, as Governor of Agra, had to include two of its parganas, Palwal
and Faridabad, within Braj Bhum.
16                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

The same list is repeated verbatim in a statement made in 1710 or soon
thereafter by zamlndars, local officials and headmen of the same parganas
in which they too refer to the Chaitanya mendicants who, ‘settled in
Bindraban [Vrindavan], engage in the worship of the Lord and wander
within Braj Bhum’ (GD, unnumbered).
    In this chapter we will broadly follow the limits set by the parganas
listed in the earlier two documents, and exclude other areas of the Mughal
province of Agra (including Agra itself) that might belong to the Braj
zone linguistically, but do not fall within the territory of that name as
delimited by the Mughal administration.


Agriculture

Of this area, as of other parts of the Upper Gangetic Basin, our major
source of geographical and economic information from Mughal times is
the Ain-iAkbart, compiled by Abu’l Fazl, c. 1595. From the official statistics
for each pargana or subdistrict that it offers, we obtain precise figures for
‘measured land’ (drazi). These represent the extent of ‘gross cultivation’
(where double-cropped land is counted twice) but also included land held
to be available for cultivation (including fields currently unsown). With
British-period statistics, comparison has to be made in large blocks since
Mughal boundaries diverged considerably from British ones, and only large
blocks would sufficiently reduce the possible margins of error. Fortunately

    As for Khohri at No. 10 in the list of parganas of Braj Bhum, it must be
Khohri Rana, a pargana in sarkar Alwar in the Am-iAkbans tables (I, p. 452),
and not Hasanpur Kori, also listed among parganas of that sarkar. This is made
certain by the separate entries for Khohri and Hasanpur Khohri under sarkar
Alwar in Br. Lib. Add. 6586, f. 91a. In Irfan Habib, Atlas of the Mughal Empire,
Sheet 6A, Khohri Rana is shown as situated near the north-western corner of
the sarkar of Alwar, placing it very far from the extreme western limits of Braj
Bhum. The correct position seems to be represented rather by the village Khori,
placed in the quarter-inch map at 27° 35’ N., 76° 59’ E., i.e. near the eastern
limits of sarkar Alwar, immediately adjacent to Braj Bhum.
    Incidentally, the first mention of Mathura as sarkar headquarters (involving
its own transfer from sarkar Agra and merger with the previous sarkar of Sahar)
occurs in a Vrindavan document of 1696, while till 1695 Mathura is described as
a pargana within sarkar Agra (Akbarabad).
    One may note in passing that the placing of Noh (No. 9) to the west of the
Yamuna implies that the river Yamuna had then taken a course to the east of Noh
Jhil, a fair possibility judging from the still existing flood channels in the area.
                          Braj Bhum in Mughal Times                              17

for us, Shireen Moosvi has carried out the necessary calculations. The
part of Braj Bhum that lay to the east of the Yamuna River falls within
her ‘Middle Doab’ block, and the part west of that river partly within her
‘Agra block. She has found that in the Middle Doab, Gross Cultivation
in 1595 was about 74.64 per cent of what it was in 1909-10, but in Agra
it was as high as 98.29 per cent. These percentages are among the highest
(that for the Agra block is, indeed, the highest) in Uttar Pradesh as a
whole, where overall the gross cultivation in 1595 was just 52.84 per cent
of what it was in 1909-10? (But see our fresh scrutiny of the evidence in
Chapter 2 of this volume).
    It may thus be supposed that, relatively speaking, Braj Bhum in Mughal
 times was not a region with extensive wastes and forest, being different
 in this regard from many other parts of the country. Such a situation
was also implied by the Dutch factor, Pelsaert, who in speaking of the
 territory around Agra in 1626, wrote that ‘trees are plentiful around the
 city but very scarce in the open country; even four or five trees usually
 mark the site of a village’.9
                             1011
    There is no evidence of any canal passing through the Braj Bhum
 territory, and cultivation must, therefore, have largely depended upon
 precipitation and wells. It is doubtful if the Persian wheel (the geared
 saqiya) had come into use in the area. It is true that a Braj document of
 1558 refers to a renta (a possible corruption of robot) while giving details
 of demarcation of land sold at Vrindavan, then still a small village. But
 renta here probably refers to a species of tree of this name, viz., rithd in
 Hindi.11
    From the AJn-i Akbans tables of dasturs or tax-rates on individual
 crops, we obtain lists of crops cultivated in different tracts. The parganas
 in Braj Bhum were distributed among the dastur~circ\es of Agra, Bayana,
 Sahar, Pahari, Thana Farida, and Koi. Of rabi crops as many as nineteen
 were raised in the Agra circle; and all of these, except Kabuli gram, were
 cultivated in the other circles, except for Koi (modern Aligarh) where


    9 Shireen Moosvi, The Economy of the Mughal Empire, c.1595: A Statistical

the rate for fenugreek too is not entered. In the kharif harvests, the Agra

kori millet in addition; Bayana has this last crop but lacks china and bdjra.
Koi and Thana Farida have all the kharif crops that are recorded for Agra
except that both lack rapeseed, but Thana Farida does have kori. It is
possible that being a low-rated crop, like arhar’, kori was simply not
deemed worth taxing in certain parganas)1 Despite the few variations, the
number of crops raised by the peasants is certainly impressive.
    We have no means of knowing the extent of land occupied by each
of the numerous crops. Probably, the inferior foodgrains dominated
the cropping. The thick paunda sugarcane and the ordinary variety of
sugarcane, as well as indigo, appear on the rated crop lists of all the dastur-
circles of the region. But the famous Bayana indigo tract lay well to the
south of Braj Bhum. The only locality within Braj Bhum which produced
indigo for long-distance trade was that around Koi (Aligarh) across the
Yamuna. According to Pelsaert, writing in 1626, it was mainly bought by
Armenian, Lahore and Kabuli’ merchants; and the annual yield was about
1,000 bales or about 100,320 kg.1213 The figure, however, is probably for the
whole Kol-Khurja tract. It may be presumed that proximity to Agra, the
Mughal capital city with a population of about half a million or more in
its days of glory,14 should have encouraged the growth of higher-quality
crops for the market that the city furnished.


Rural Society

The fact that the tax-rates per unit of area (bigha-i ildhi) for each crop
given in the Ajn-i Akbari are all in terms of money shows that money
relations widely prevailed in the villages. In 1704, when villages in the
eighteen parganas of Braj Bhum were required to provide charity payments
to gosd’ins (goswamis) of Rup s line, through an order of the Governor
of Agra, the levy was again in terms of money, namely, one rupee per


   12 The dastur rates by circles for ceuba Agra will be found in the A’ln-i Akbari,

village per year, half a rupee to be paid at each of the two harvests.1516
Vrindavan documents consist, in a fairly large part, of deeds recording
sales of agricultural land, and these again show prices being paid in money.
It is only in an early deed of land-sale in Vrindavan, recorded in 1558 in
Braj, that the sellers, who claim to be thepancb (peasant-headmen) of the
village, receive Rs.60 in cash (rok) and Rs. 10 in garments {pahardvam).'"'
Such part-payments in kind (small as these were) disappear entirely from
subsequent records of sales.
    Our documents reveal to us a well differentiated village society, created
partly by the way the caste system imposed deprivation on many, and
partly by the way taxation and money circulation introduced considerable
variations among peasants with regard to the size of resources and area
of land cultivated by them. We get a glimpse of the deprivation when
we come across a simple entry in a document of 1692, prepared for the
purpose of laying down limits of a land-grant, held in seven plots, in the
village of Rajpur (near Vrindavan). In specifying the boundaries of each
plot, the fields (kisht) of individual persons (e.g. the field of Raghunath
the Brahman or of Tubi Singh, Gangaram, etc.) are carefully mentioned,
but for defining the northern boundary of the seventh plot, we have the
simple entry kisht-i Chamardn-i mauza Rajpur, ‘the field of the Chamars
of village Rajpur*. The entry implies that a plot of village land was left to
be cultivated by the ‘untouchable* Chamars of the village, presumably in
return for the labour services (including the removal of dead animals) they
were subject to. In the present case either the co-sharers in that field were
too numerous, or thought to be of too inferior a station, to allow their
being individually named.
    Our documents seldom mention the caste of peasants who appear as
headmen or sellers. But in a sale deed of land dated 1594 in a village
near Vrindavan the two peasants who sold it are called Gaurua;17 as are

the three brothers who sell the land, with muqaddami (headmans rights

There being no other peasant caste mentioned in documents relating to
the locality of Vrindavan, it may be assumed that Gauruas formed the
dominant peasant caste of the locality. Gauruas seem to have formed the
dominant peasant caste as well in Arith (Radhakund), the other Braj
village with which many of our documents are concerned (see Chapter 8).
In the nineteenth century the Gauruas were held to form an ‘inferior’ class
of Rajputs, who, as was the case with many peasant castes at the time,
maintained the custom of levirate or widow remarriage,20
    The Vrindavan documents are important in showing that the panch of
the village were really a group of dominant peasants, styled muqaddams
in the Persian documents, being generally empowered to sell away, or
lease out, vacant land of the village. It also seems that the position of
a panch was partly hereditary, possibly by some form of primogeniture.
Since these matters have been discussed elsewhere, it may not perhaps be
useful to claim space for them here as well.21
    What we may consider a little more closely is the religiously composite
nature of the panch. In 1594 thirteen panch of village Dosaich, and
hamlets Nagu and Bhanan, situated within Vrindavan, sold a plot of land
to Raja Man Singh of Amber, the deed for which exists in both Persian
and Braj versions. Of the thirteen joint sellers, three bear Muslim names,
viz., Nizam, Humaun/Humyan, and Nasso (Nasir).22 In another sale-deed
of 1601, of the seven peasants named as sellers (styled zammdars here,
not headmen), two (Junaid and Najm) are Muslims.23 The names of the
fathers of the sellers in both these documents are not given; but the case
of‘All Khan in a subsequent generation suggests that the Muslims were

ones, Bari Khan must have been a convert, a change that did not still
affect his position in the village. A late document (of 1717) suggests that
the peasants at Arith too were Gauruas, for the two peasant sellers of land
here are described as belonging to this caste.26 Caste affiliation, in both
Vrindavan and Arith, thus seems to have remained unaffected by change
of religion and continued to be the main factor in determining status in
village society.
    The Vrindavan documents tell us little about the class that stood
above the peasants, the zammdars, though the term begins to appear in
seventeenth-century documents beginning with the one of 1601 referred
to earlier. One man who appears in a document of 1558 could have well
belonged to this class: ‘The thdkur of Sei, All Khan, chaudhurij as he is
described in the Braj version, helped Jiv Goswami obtain a plot of land
in Vrindavan. In the Persian version of the same document prepared in
1569 he is simply styled ‘the muqaddam (headman)’ of the same village of
Sei.27 Possibly in that village he did not share the headmen’s rights with
anyone else.

of zamlndars of each pargana, along with the numbers of their armed

    In Table 1.1 the absence of any data for zamindar castes and their
retainers in the pargana of Mathura (which contained Vrindavan) seems
surprising. Perhaps, the pargana was held to be mainly urban with few
villages. In general, the peasant castes, viz., Jats, Meos, Ahirs and Gujars,
find better representation among zamindars recorded under the parganas
of sarkdr Sahar (Nos. 11-15), that is, in the areas further west from the
Yamuna, than in the core Braj areas where the upper castes, Rajputs,
                           Braj Bhum in Mughal Times                            23

Brahmans, Shaikhzadas, etc., appear to dominate. The names of some
castes are not easy to decipher. It is possible that the Jat uprising of the
seventeenth and eighteenth centuries substantively altered the situation:
as Elliot’s map of ‘Zamindari Possession’ in 1844 shows, two large blocks
of Jat zamindars had by now been created near Mathura on both sides
of the river Yamuna.28 See Chapter 2 for more on zamindar castes and
communities.
    If the Jat example shows how successful insurgents could turn into
zamindars, the Mughal administration could also plant its own men and
make them zamindars (i.e. recognize them as holders of zamindari rights
and privileges) in order to strengthen or, indeed, create local support
for its authority. Thus Hasan ‘All Khan, appointed faujdar of Mathura
in December 1669, established Shah Muhammad Nawaz, Saidam
Baloch, Sahikh Razlu’ddm, Lal Muhammad, and .Nazar Muhammad as
permanent zamindars of particular tracts in his jurisdiction.29 In fact, a
parwana whose text survives shows how this was managed. This official
document recites that Hasan ‘Ali Khan, as faujdar of the chakla (territorial
division) of‘Islamabad, that is Mathura’, recommended to the Emperor
that one Qasim, son ofDaulat, who had been assigned in jagir 25 villages
in pargana Sahar, ‘a haven of insurgents’, be recognised as zamindar of the
same villages, so as to be held responsible for the collection and payment
of revenue in the time of the succeeding jagirdars of the area, after the
transfer of his ownjagir. The parwana says that the proposal was approved
by the Emperor and a farman to this effect was being issued.30
   Before we leave agrarian society, a word about cattle and their owners.
We have noted that by Moosvi’s estimation the extent of Gross Cultivation
in 1595 in Middle Doab was less than 75 per cent of what it was in 1909-

Towns

The major town of Braj Bhum was Mathura, with a history dating back
to Mauryan times, if not earlier. In 1595 it was the headquarters of a
pargana, being placed within the sarkdr of Agra. It was only as late as
 1695-6 that it was separated from Agra and made the headquarters of
a sarkdr, under the official name of Islamabad, with the parganas of the
erstwhile sarkdr of Sahar brought under it.38 Mathura also came to possess
a rupee mint during the late years of Aurangzebs reign, though its output
seems to have remained small.39
   If one wishes to study how a town or township could arise and grow in
Mughal times, then it is not Mathura, but Vrindavan, over 10 kilometres
away, on which our documents give us invaluable information. Within
the pargana of Mathura, on the same side of the Yamuna, was the village
of Dosaich. In Dosaich’s vicinity, Shri Chaitanya is said to have located
the site of Radha’s garden, the Vrindavana of holy legend.40 As we noted
earlier, the Governor of Agra issued an official order in 1704 crediting Rup
and Haridas, who were Chaitanyas disciples, with having established their
residence in Bindraban (so spelt) when it was ‘all jungle and uninhabited*.41
Thereafter, the succeeding Goswamls and their followers began buying
land in village Dosaich and its surrounding hamlets, numerous records of
which are preserved for us either in the original or early copies in Persian
as well as Braj.42 Alongside this process of land acquisition there was a

spate of temple construction, which began to put even Mathura into the

its environs undertaken in 1598 with a view to establishing which temples
deserved imperial grants of land. The work was carried out by Abu 1 Fazl
with the help of four (named) pandits, and their recommendations, as
approved by Emperor Akbar, were set forth in a singular Jarman issued on
11 September 1598. Of the 35 temples now recommended for assistance
only 6 were situated in Mathura, compared to 18 in village Dosaich and its
vicinity (i.e. Vrindavan). Pargana Sahar accounted for a further 8, pargana
Mangotla, 8, and pargana Hodal, l.43
    Settlements grew around the temples at Vrindavan, and agricultural
land was progressively converted into habitational area. This process we
can see not only in sale-deeds, but also in the rising prices of land under
cultivation (ziraatl, zarj) in the vicinity of the township. Table 1.2
shows prices in Rupees per bigha at which waste and cultivated land was
purchased at and around Vrindavan in various years (transactions of less
than one bigha of land have been ignored).
    One would expect prices to vary with the precise situation of the land
sold, which may explain the very high price (relative to the previous years)
paid in two transactions, one in 1595 and the other in 1715. But generally,
a rise in land prices as the township grew seems well established from the
table.
    This growth meant that buyers of land plots needed to have increasingly
larger financial resources. Jahangir, who visited the temple-town in 1619,
said the temples were built in Akbar’s time by Rajput nobles after their
own style with decorated exteriors.44 The ruler of Amber Raja Man
Singhs patronage of the Govind-dev temple and its priests, continued by
his successors, is otherwise well established.45 Then, there were imperial
Mughal land-grants conferred under Akbar and Jahangir and maintained
under their successors.46 The Vrindavan documents definitively show that

    From these various sources of income much prosperity came the way
 of the Goswamis. When in 1710 Gobind Charan, head of the line of Rup
 Goswami, wished to transfer all his properties and effects to his designated
successor, his nephew and adopted son, Jagannath, an impressive list of
his possessions was drawn up. Besides a large store of ornaments, utensils
and cloth, he possessed 5 cows, 10 buffaloes, 15 bullocks, a horse and a
camel, one carriage, and one cart. He had a haveli and ten kunjs (retreats
or cottages set amidst groves) in Kaman, with two others in places nearby,
along with a garden with two wells; one haveli (house) and two shops in
Mathura; three havelis, eleven retreats (kunj), four gardens, four plots of
land, including one which was under cultivation, at Vrindavan; two tanks
and three retreats at Arith (Radhakund); a garden with haveli and well at
Barsana; three bavells in three other villages; one haveli at Amber, another
nearby, where he also possessed a garden with four wells; and, finally, a
garden at Sanganer about 15 km. SSW of Amber.50 There are names of
persons entered under various houses and retreats, some of whom could
have been his tenants. That the Goswamis often let out houses to rent is
shown by the actual judgement of the qdzi of Mathura, given in 1675 in
the plaint of Gobind Charan’s predecessor, Seoram Gosa’in, that he had
let out a haveli of his at Mathura to one Arjun, who now refused to pay
him rent.51 The Goswamis thus must have constantly been adding further
income from their investments in land and buildings.
   While these worldly activities of the Goswamis were doubtless a factor
in the growth of the township of Vrindavan, the basic source of its growth
remained the places character as a religious centre of the Chaitanya sect. In
time, however, followers of other sects appeared, with whom the relations
of the Chaitanya sect were by no means always cordial. In Mathura in 1654,
Gauranga Bangall, who owed allegiance to "Kishan Chaitan’ (Chaitanya),
complained to the local Mughal official that a Radha Ballabhl named
Damodar Das had seized a tree under which Lord Chaitanya used to sit.
A compromise was thereupon affected.52 By 1718, according to Mahyar
Khan’s parwana, there were now at Vrindavan besides the ‘Gaudiyas’ or

   What then, did this township look like? Unfortunately, the historian
Bhimsen, who visited it in 1697-8 and was greatly impressed by it, left no
further description of the town.54 Slightly less brief are the notes 1‘timad
‘All Khan left of the two visits he paid to Vrindavan as a tourist in 1718.
He remarked on its large Brahman population and admired the gardens
that the Hindus had laid out in the town, along what ‘in Hindvi* was
called Kunj-Gall (‘grove-lane’)- He adds that the town was infested by
red-[faced] monkeys, whom visitors used to feed with gram?5 Finally,

bazaar: it has twenty or fifteen shops of grain-sellers, betel-leaf and flower sellers,
etc. Mostly it is the Hindu mendicants of much attainment who live in the
retreats of Brindaban. A few Muslim faqirs live in cells. It is said that it. was the
place where Krishna had his sports. Men and women of that place are very
handsome. Whoever observes them will have his carnal love or spiritual devotion
greatly intensified according to his capacity.

   He goes on to speak enthusiastically of the concourse of young men and
women in the evenings, indulging in music, laughter, and games with each
other, and returning to their homes after nightfall with lighted candles.

of a large quarter of Vrindavan close to the Yamuna’s banks.

Merchants and Moneylending

 One class on which our documents shed very little light is that of the
 merchants. However, it was from their ranks that some substantive gifts
were received by the Gosa’ins, from which one may infer the degree
 of prosperity of the class. Kanho, wife of Kaitam, mahajan (banker),
 donated a haveli and a shop in Toda Bhiwan (Bhim) to the Govind-dev
 temple in 1711.57 In 1719 Manoharl, wife of Lakhu, Khandelwal Banya
 (baqqat), and her daughter made a gift of a large pucca house at Kaman
 to the Govind-dev temple and its chief priest, Jagannath (NAI-2691/10).
 In 1725, Raja Ram, sdhukdr (banker) from Bengal, gifted two kunjs in
Vrindavan to Ram Jiwan Gosa’in of the Gopinath temple (IVS 205). In
 1760, Bishan Kunwar, widow of Bengali Rai Agarwal, mahajan, resident
of Delhi, gifted a brick-and-stone haveli, wooden-roofed, with a grove and
pucca well, aL Vrindavan, to Ram Saran, priest of the Govind-dev temple
 (NAI-2671/26).
    We do not learn much about commercial activities from our records. The
sole document relating to the hundis, or bills of exchange, is a statement
of 1680 by one Sherdil, mansabdar (rank-holding officer) to the effect that
he had held a hundi for Rs.43Vi, drawn by Bans! and Manohardas Sahu,
which he had now sold to Bansidhar Jhokan in Mathura (IVS 174).
    Those with financial resources also engaged in a number ofmoneylending
activities that belie assumptions like those of Vera Anstey that in pre­
colonial times 'the power to borrow was strictly limited by the political,
economic and legal lack of security, and land could be hardly considered to
offer security for a loan’.58 In 1728 Kishan Charan, adhikdri of the Madan
Mohan temple, advanced a loan of Rs.30 to two muqaddams and two
ordinary peasants against the security (gahnat) of three bighas of dressed
fields (kyart pdtrivdrl), surrounded by agricultural land, presumably in



gyan-gudrt). It could not refer to the evening assemblage but was the name of
the ground where the people assembled. The name is, in fact, borne by a large
quarter of the town of Vrindavan by the side of the river Yamuna, not far from
the Govind-dev temple (see Survey of India’s Guide Map of Mathura and
Vrindavan; also Growse, op. cit., p. 271). This is where the concourse presumably
used to be held when Bilgrami visited the town.
   57 Cf Mirza Raja Bijai Singhs parwdna in M. Horstmann, In Favour of
Govind-dev Ji, pp. 208-11, facsimile, p. 329.
   58 Vera Anstey, The Economic Development ofIndia, 4th edn., London, 1952,

Vrindavan (Wright 22),59 The interest charged was to amount to a taka to
a rupee (taka rupiya ko bydj), presumably per month, a rate we cannot now
establish because we do not know how may takas (copper coins) went to
a rupee at the time. Two successive Braj documents, one of 9 June 1743
(Wright 31) and the other of a month or more later (Wright 32) show
that for receiving Rs. 132 from Ram Kishor, adhikari of Madan Mohan,
six named jimidars (zamtnddrs) mortgaged their garden, held to be worth
Rs.137, which was handed over to the lender when the latter amount was
not paid back by the borrowers.6061 This would imply a rate of interest of
3.79 per cent per month (i.e. 45.45 per cent a year at the simple rate) based
on the value assigned to the mortgaged garden.
    There are three documents which concern loans advanced against the
security of urban property. All three come from the Gopinath temple
archives. In one (IVS 173), dated 18 January 1720, one Gujar Mai of the
S rim al caste, resident of Delhi, mortgaged two shops in Agra that he
owned against a loan of Rs.30 from one Moti Lal s/o Manak Chand,
the rent to be collected by the lender in lieu of interest. In a document
of 1756-7 (probably 12 April 1757) one Udai Ram Das6' took a loan of
Rs.3,000 from Rao Rup Ram at the monthly rate of Re.0.75 (12 annas)
per cent per month (i.e. 9 per cent per annum at the simple rate) on the
security of two rectangular kunjs in Vrindavan (IVS 148A-B). Though
the bond comes from the Gopinath temple archives, it is not clear how
any priest of that temple was involved in the transaction. The rate of
interest seems moderate, but possibly the lender also claimed the rent due
from the mortgaged property until the amount lent was returned. Finally,
we have a late document of 13 September 1772, in which one Sobha
Chand, Brahman, of Gujarat, records his acknowledgement of a loan of
Rs.25 from Gosa in Bindraban Das (adhikari?) of the Gopinath temple,
mortgaging his house (haveli) in the Mandvl Rani quarter of Mathura
with a fixed interest rate of 2 per cent per month (i.e. 24 per cent per
annum at the simple rate), which he was to pay monthly until he also
repaid the principal (IVS 197).

   As these details show, moneylending was carried out not only by
professional bankers and usurers but also by other rich men. This seems
to bear out what Francisco Pelsaert noted in 1626 in respect of Agra, next
door to Mathura:

In Agra the men who are richest live mainly by money-lending, a practice which
is not discreditable to Hindus, but only to Moslems (though they do it commonly
enough).62



Administration

 Most of the Persian documents in the Vrindavan collections are related
 directly and indirectly to the conduct of the Mughal administration, a fact
 which has led Shireen Moosvi to argue in favour of the depth of control
 that the Mughal state apparatus possessed even in rural localities.63 In any
 case, we have here names of various officials by which we can establish
 who was serving in what position at what time, especially at Mathura,
 headquarters of the pargana, within whose limits Vrindavan was situated.
    The most powerful officer the Gosa’ins or Goswamis ofVrindavan usually
 dealt with was the noble in whose jagir Mathura lay at that particular time.
 These jagjrdars and their subordinates were birds of passage, so to speak,
 since their jdgirs were held on a temporary basis only. The names of eleven
jagirdars of Mathura, successively holding the assignment, during the reign
 of Shahjahan (1628-58) appear in the Vrindavan documents, giving an
 average term of 2.7 years for each.64 The jagirdar, besides his entitlement
 to collect the land tax and other imposts, was usually responsible for
 settling local complaints and fulfilling some police obligations. We may as
 an illustration take the proceedings in a dispute over buildings and land in
 Vrindavan between Gobind Charan Gosa in and Kishan Priya, the widow
 of Braj Kunwar Gosa’in. In 1705 Mukhtar Khan, who along with holding
 the office of Governor of Agra was apparently also thejagjrdar of Mathura,
 issued a parwana to his agent at Mathura, Mir Madan, in favour of Gobind
 Charan’s claims (G.D. Persian). At the same time, Raja Jai Singh of Amber

(who held the position of patron of the Govind-dev temple) sent parwdnas
to officials at Mathura, also urging Gobind Charan’s claims. These officials
were the faujdar (commandant) Mirza Bahram Beg; Sayyid Sultan, the
waqa i nigar (news reporter, an appointee of the Bakhshi-u’l Mamalik,
an imperial minister); and Mir Madan, the jdgirddrs agent. The task of
implementing these orders fell to thefaujdar, who sent a mounted soldier
and four footmen to enforce them. When owing to Kishan Priyas spirited
defiance they failed to achieve their purpose, Gobind Charan’s partisans
insisted that the true state of affairs be put into the waqd’T (report of the
waqd’i-nigar}y 'so that there may be no need for further enquiry’.65 The
dispute, as one may expect, lingered on for a long time, though ultimately
Gobind Charan had some success.66
     Another official with whom the Goswamis had much to do was the qdzi
of Mathura (as was so at other pargana headquarters,, such as Sahar). First,
he did duty as a registrar, so that unless a text of agreement, sale, gift, etc.,
 or a copy thereof, was authenticated by the qdzi, it could not be considered
a reliable piece of evidence. For this reason, many papers written in Braj
were subsequently reinforced with Persian versions and in that form
 authenticated by the qazi, as we can see in the case of a number of sale
 deeds in the Vrindavan Collections. Second, the qdzi also decided disputes
 over property, for which purpose the Goswamis had regular recourse to
 his court at Mathura. We have already referred to the qdzi Muhammad
 ‘Arif’s judgement in favour of Seo Ram Gusa’in against his tenant
 Arjun in 1675. An interesting judgement was delivered by his successor
 Qutbuddm, on 28 February 1706, upholding the claims of Gobind Charan
 Gusa’in to a haveli and two shops in Mathura which he had received by
 hiba (gift) from Kishan Charan, as against the claims of the officials of the
 Baitu’l Mdl, or Public Treasury, who were appropriating the rents from
 the three premises on the ground apparently that Kishan Charan had died
  intestate. In his decision the qdzi had the support of Izid Bakhsh ‘Rasa,
 then the head of the Bayutat (Property and Household) Department of
  the Province of Agra, who issued a parwana in favour of Gobind Charan
  on 2 February 1706.67 Below the formal text of his parwana Izid Bakhsh

 wrote in his own hand: ‘Let the officials act in accordance with what
 has been written. Let them so behave that no harm is inflicted on Gods
 people. Let them strictly obey this’.68 It is possible to see in this episode
 two contrasting aspects of Mughal administration, namely, arbitrariness
 and some spirit of compassion.
     It is possible that as a group well supported by imperial grants69 and
 patronized by the Amber rulers, so uniformly loyal to the Mughals, the
 Vrindavan Goswamis should have been seeing more of the better side of
 the Mughal administration. When Aurangzeb had the temple of Keshav
 Rai at Mathura destroyed in January 1670, this caused much alarm at
 Vrindavan and Seo Ram Gusain departed from the town, carrying away
 the idols installed at the Govind-dev temple, which now left Vrindavan for
 ever.70 Yet our documents do not even distantly refer to any destruction or
 desecration affecting any of the major temples at Vrindavan. On the other
hand, in May 1671 during the middle of this period of alarm, Hasan ‘All
Khan, faujdar of Mathura, issued a parwdna directing that the 135 bighas
 of land held by the late Raja Jai Singh of Amber on behalf of the Govind-
dev temple, etc., under imperial Jarmans, should be maintained as per
status quo and no interference therein should occur.71 In fact, as we noted
earlier, all the previous grants to Vrindavan temples and priests continued
to be maintained and confirmed during the reign of Aurangzeb. Seo Ram
himself returned in 1675 and set about looking after his properties in
Mathura and Vrindavan.72
    One needs, however, to remind oneself here that any measure of
relief from the administration did not usually come free of charge. It is
not a matter on which one would expect formal documents to be frank
and forthcoming. Nonetheless, in a draft text for endorsement by local
notables, c.1718, Gosain Gopi Raman of Vrindavan lets it out that much
‘substantial money’ (zar-i-kulli) had to be spent to win over the amin
(finance comptroller) of a princess and to get the Imperial court to favour
his cause, so as to protect his rights against the pretensions of one Kunj
Das (RD-30). In January 1725, Gopt Raman himself gave a qdzi-certified
promissory note to one Qazi Nur Muhammad for payment of Rs.30
towards his services in obtaining official confirmation of a land-grant of

50 bighas (VRI, Sr. No. 53, Acc. No. 9). One can speculate as to how much

    One fresh source of trouble for the Goswamis was undoubtedly the
Jat uprising of the early 1690s. In 1692, a new demarcation document
(chaknama) had to be drawn up for an imperial grant held by Gosa in
Das, because of his claim that ‘the cbakndma of the grant had been lost
among the goods plundered during the times of disturbance raised by the
accursed Jats’.73 Krishan Charan, the successor of Seo Ram, whom we met
earlier, narrated in 1694 that he had to leave behind his properties and
‘flee to the Rajput country owing to the disturbance raised by the Jats’.74
However, except for this period of rebellion and unrest in the early 1690s,
life in Vrindavan, and apparently Braj Bhum as a whole, seems to have
followed a course of sedate calm till well into the reign of Muhammad
Shah (1719-48) J5


People

Ordinary people appear in our documents usually only when they had
something to do with the Gosa’ins’ land transactions. Peasants appear as
sellers of land, and we can glimpse something of the working of the village
‘community’ from the information provided by sale deeds. It is left to
other materials to provide us with some notion of the burdens the peasants
bore, the primary one being that of land-revenue. The A’in-i Akbarts tax­
schedules (dasturs) for the Braj Bhum territory, as for other areas, show
a very high level of tax burden considering the prices prevailing at the
time: the clear aim was the appropriation of the maximum share possible
of the peasants surplus.76 This could not take place without considerable
repression. In December 1631, Mundy visited Koi, the headquarters of
one of the Doab parganas which Mukhtar Khan included within Braj
Bhum in his parwana of 1704. ‘Here’, says Mundy, ‘nowe are in this Castle
about 200 of them [peasants] prisoners because they cannot pay the Tax
imposed on them, which heretofore was paid when their Corne was sold;

a substantial addtion to rural and urban taxation. Its imposition was
decreed by Aurargzeb in 1679, and was later abolished by Farrukhsiyar
in 1713 through a simple order.78 One is left to wonder whether this
total silence on jisya is due to the fact that the Vrindavan gosa ins, being
technically mendfcants (fuqara), were exempt from that onerous tax.
   Fiscal oppression apart, that daily life was hard for the poor was noted
by the observant franc is co Pelsaert, writing about Agra and its surrounding
region in or about 1626. He offers this description of the life of‘workmen’
at Agra: they hac 'kbichri, made of green pulse mixed with rice, cooked
with water’ as thdr food. Their huts were ‘built of mud with thatched
roofs’. Of furniture there was ‘little or none except some earthenware pots’
and two beds for the man and wife, the scanty bed-cloths leaving them
exposed to bitter cold in winter. Some relief from cold was secured only
from cow dung fires.79 This could serve as a description of the conditions
of poor people throughout Braj Bhum.
    Setting aside .amines, any of which (such as the one in 1554-6)
could have carriel away large parts of the population,80 there was the
scourge of diseast. Out of the 25 villagers whose personal descriptions
are recorded in tie extant papers authenticated by qdzis of Mathura and
Sahar during the period 1653-1717, as many as ten bore the marks of
smallpox, a propcrtion of 40 per cent.81 If one takes into account all the
persons whose appearance is recorded in huliyas in documents whether of
qazis courts or others, belonging to Vrindavan (mostly), Mathura, and
Arith, between 1453 and 1737, after adjusting the figure for the number
of persons whose huliya is recorded more than once, as many as 43 out of
96, or 44.8 per ceit, are reported to have borne the marks of smallpox. It
is surely curious that the number of those affected by smallpox seems to
have grown over time. Among the 48 persons whose huliyas were recorded
during the period 1653-1717, the number bearing marks of smallpox is
only 15, but amo^g the next 48 physical descriptions recorded between

1722 and 1737 the number rises to 28, nearly double. Does this mean that
smallpox was on the increase in the area in the late seventeenth century?
Or merely that pockmarks were not observed carefully enough earlier?
Were we to accept the latter explanation, this would mean that over the
total period as many as over 58 per cent of adults could have suffered
from an attack of smallpox at some point in their lives, though we still
do not know the extent of mortality the disease caused, which must have
been correspondingly high. Plague too visited the region in 1615-6, and
Jahangir has left us an account of the heavy mortality it caused in the area
around Agra.82
   From our documents we learn much about the extent of illiteracy. In
the gwzf-certified documents ordinary persons simply affix a mark. Not
a single peasant, the panch included, seems to have known how to write,
if we judge by their manifest failure to sign their names in the numerous
surviving deeds of land-sale or other legal documents. The only exception
appears to be offered by a bilingual document of 1654 where All Khan,
son of Angad, a peasant of Vrindavan, was able to write ‘All’ in Nagari,
followed by an abbreviation das (for dastkhat) also in Nagari (GD=Wright
12). He probably did not know how to write ‘Khan’ to complete his name!
The same conditions prevailed apparently among ordinary townsmen.
When in May 1705 a report was drawn up on the failure of a horseman and
four foot soldiers to evict Kishan Priya from the premises she occupied at
Vrindavan, and the five troopers had to endorse the report, Shaikh Farid,
the horseman, did not even know how to put the mark, and only Arjun
Singh, a companion of his, put a geometric figure as a mark apparently
on behalf of them both. Daddhram, one of the foot soldiers, put a cross.83
The other footmen probably could not even hold a pen to make a mark.
Not many, then, could really have attained literacy, let alone education,
under the banyan tree.


Relations among Communities

When we turn from what our evidence tells us about the misery of
disease and extent of illiteracy in Braj Bhum to the light it throws on
intercommunal relations, we at least encounter a far less depressing scene.
History undoubtedly records some acts of religious intolerance committed
in Mughal times; but it also presents a contrary picture. In the Vrindavan

  82 Tuzuk-i Jahangiri, op, cit., pp. 161-2.
  83 G.D. Coll. (Persian).
38                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

documents it is the contrary picture that is incontestably more visible.
    In contrast to Aurangzeb’s destruction of the great Keshav Rai temple
of Mathura in 1670, we have evidence of the generous grants to temples
and their priests in the Braj region under Akbar (Chapter 3), and his
successor Jahangir (Chapter 4), If Shahjahan was less generous over new
grants, the farman he issued in 1634 to protect the sounding of the gong
in the Madan Mohan temple, Vrindavan, is surely a memorable text: it
expressly describes the ritual performed in the temple as ‘worship of God’
('ibadat-i-Ilahl) (MM-61), a recognition inconceivable for a Muslim of
even moderately orthodox views to give voice to. It is also important to
remind ourselves that even under Aurangzeb, all the grants to temples
and priests at Vrindavan and elsewhere in the Braj region continued to be
honoured, as noted earlier (see also Chapter 5). The unprecedented official
provision of income for the Gosa’ins through a levy on all the villages of
the eighteen purganas of Braj Bhum from 1704 onwards, also belongs to a
late phase in the same reign.
    Later, when some time in or before 1726-7, Subal Das, chief priest of
the Madan Mohan temple at Vrindavan, was writing to ‘Inayatullah Khan,
now a minister, but earlier a secretary of Aurangzeb, to seek from him
confirmation of an imperial land-grant held by that temple, Subal Das
must have felt that the following words would strike the right chord with
that high official: ‘Whatever favour is shown to the temple (devala) comes,
after all, from God—-jazdk Allah (Arabic for “May God reward thee”)!’84
   A similar commingling of religious idioms is found in a rare private
letter in Persian that has also survived among the Vrindavan documents.
Written by an avowed devotee of Jiv Gosa’in and addressed to one Mehta
Sitaldas, in or before 1658, it has the Arabic invocation Allahu Akbar at
the top, while ‘Ram Ram’ is recited in salutation to the addressee in the
main text (RD-96).
   Jiv Goswami himself, the celebrated head of the Chaitanya sect of
Vrindavan, benefited from the assistance of‘All Khan, thakur and chaudhurl
of Sei, a village fairly distant from Vrindavan, who transferred to Jiv (in
1558) the land he had himself purchased at the latter place, without any
apparent gain to himself (see above and Chapter 6). The attitude of ease
of dealings among Hindus and Muslims at the time is also shown by the
fact that there are four Muslim signatures in Persian on the deed of gift
of a temple (devala) by Gopinath, wine-seller, at Vrindavan in December
1600, heading a number of signatures in Hindi (IVS-216),

    As Mukhtar Khan, Governor of the Agra province, noted in 1704,

    In the numerous land transactions of the Gosa’ins, Muslim headmen
and peasants appear as both sellers and witnesses. We have seen how three
of them, Nizam, Humaun, and Nasso, appear as sellers among the thirteen
panch in Dosaich (Vrindavan) in 1594 and two of them among seven, now
styled zamindars, in 1601. They must have been converts, as there are no
Muslims among the panch of Vrindavan in the earlier records. As we have
already suggested, such also must have been the case with Bari Khan, who
appears as the head of the panch of village Arith in 1641 = The practice of
naming an individual’s father (and often grandfather as well) to establish
his identity in legal and semi-legal documents seems to have become a
more regularly used method from about the middle of the seventeenth
century; and this helps us to identify converts more easily. Thus ‘All Khan
of Vrindavan, ‘son of Angad, son ofRaghu Gaurua’, so described in 1654,
was an obvious convert; and yet he was acceptable as a surety to Seo Ram,
chief priest of the Govind-dev temple, when buying land from two Hindu
Gaurua peasants.85 There were reverse conversions to Hinduism as well, as
illustrated by the case of a namesake of ‘All Khan at the village of Arith
(Radhakund). It’s here in 1717, that one Har Rai, ‘son of Gulal, son of

a Gaurua) nor one’s legal or even social status.
    Shared poverty also perhaps removed barriers. In 1732 at Mathura
when three Hindu women, viz., Dayali and her two daughters-in-law,
all of them apparently widows, wished to sell away their small piece of
land containing the remains of a ruined hut or cottage (jamla), they had
to offer two sureties (zamin). The first one that they could produce was
a humble 61-year old Muslim cotton-carder (naddaf), Ghasi, son of Taj,
son of‘Alam. And his word was duly accepted by the buyer, Ram Saran,
the future chief priest of the Govind-dev temple.87

                             CHAPTER 2



          Notes on the Economic and
          Ethnographic Geography of
          Braj Bhum in Mughal Times
                     Irfan Habib and Faiz Habib




Chapter 1 offered a general survey of Braj Bhum in Mughal times,
adopting for its territory the group of parganas which in 1704, according
to a declaration of the Governor of the suba (province) of Agra, constituted
Braj Bhum. For this territory we have considerable statistical and
ethnographic information, tabulated pargana-wise, in the A?in-i Akbari
(c. 1595) of Abu’l Fazl. The information was surveyed on the basis of
research already carried out, notably by S. Moosvi; but on reflection it has
been felt that there still remains some room for a second round of analysis
of the data the A’in-i Akbari provides us with.
    Before we undertake the task, let us remind ourselves of the changes that
had taken place in the territorial demarcation of internal administrative
units between the time of the A’in-i Akbari and 1704, when the territory
of Braj Bhum came to be officially defined. All the eighteen parganas.,
listed in 1704 as belonging to Braj Bhum, were situated within the suba
or province of Agra, but for two belonging to the suba of Delhi. The
parganas in Mughal times were grouped under sarkdrs (a number of which
formed a suba). Thus until 1696 Mathura, Mangotla, Ol, Au (or Ao),
Mahaban (or Mahavan), and Jalesar belonged to the sarkdr of Agra; Sahar,
Kaman or Kama, Khoh or Koh Mujahid, Pahari, and Hodal, to sarkdr
Sahar; Khohri, to sarkdr Alwar; and Koi, Noh, Sa*dabad, and Anupnagar
to sarkdr Koi, all four sarkdrs lying within suba Agra. Palwal belonged to
the sarkdr of Delhi, and so was situated within the suba of Delhi. Of the
three new parganas, one apparently represented only a change of name,
42                        Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

as Faridabad had replaced Tilpat as pargana headquarters within sarkdr
Delhi, while the other two, Anupnagar and Sa‘dabad, were carved out of
territories of older parganas, to be placed within sarkdr Koi. These facts
are confirmed by a list of parganas of Mughal provinces, the portion of
which relating to suba Agra, at least, was compiled before 1696? This
floor date is established by the fact that the list still shows pargana Matura
(so spelt) as being within sarkdr Agra. The last reference in the Vrindavan
documents to Mathura as attached to sarkdr Agra belongs to 1695, while
from 1696 onwards Mathura (or Islamabad ‘urfMathura) begins to appear
as a separate sarkdr.123Recurring references in the Vrindavan documents
subsequent to 1695 show that the sarkdr of Sahar was now abolished,
and all its parganas had been transferred to sarkdr Mathura. Pargana
Mahaban, previously in sarkdr Agra, was also attached to the new sarkdr
of Mathura?
    The A’In-i Akbari supplies in its statistical tables, set out in the chapter
entitled Ahwal-i do-azdah suba (Account of the Twelve Provinces), much
information about the localities we are concerned with. The data relate
mainly to matters which were important from the official point of view.
Reading from right to left, after the name of the pargana, comes an entry
for qita or fort (where, in case it has one, it is indicated whether it is of
stone or brick); then drazl or measured area, given in bighas', next, naqdl
or jama1, which represented the estimated or assigned net annual revenue
in dams (40 dams going to a rupee); followed by the amount of jama*
alienated through suyurghdl or tax-free land-grants;4 and in the last three
columns, the zamlnddrs/bumls in the pargana, specified by qaum (caste or
clan) and the numbers of their retainers, horse and foot.

Allahabad, Awadh, Agra, Delhi, Lahore, Ajmer and Ahmadabad (Gujarat). While
copied in the late eighteenth century, internal evidence places the original lists in
the late seventeenth century (see note 2).
   2 The last document mentioning Mathura as belonging to sarkdr Agra is

in Add. 6586, not only shows Mathura in sarkdr Agra, but also shows sarkdr
Sahar with its parganas duly listed (ff 89b, 92b).
   3 This is shown by a document of 27 April 1718 (IVS 157).
   4 On this see Shireen Moosvi, The Economy of the Mughal Empire, c.1595: A
