               Notes on the Economic and Ethnographic Geography                 43

    Since H. Blochmann in his standard edition of the /Tin-i Akbari
dispensed with the columns and their headings, and H.S. Jarrett in
his translation restored the columns very eccentrically, altering their
arrangement and headings, it is desirable to set out the information, given
in the A’ins tables for the Braj Bhum parganas, strictly following the
original scheme (Table 2.1).5
    The influence of an official approach in the compilation of the A*in-i
Akbaris data is shown by the importance it gives to forts. Clearly, it was
of much use to the administration to know whether such a safe place
was available for the storage of money and material, and the residence of
officers and guards. Of the 16 parganas of Braj Bhum listed in the A in
forts are recorded, not surprisingly, in as many as eight.
    The measured area (drazi) statistics, which come next to the column
for forts in the A*ins tables, pose a puzzle that is hard to solve. One would
think that the area was measured by the Mughal administration mainly
for taxation purposes and so would correspond to ‘gross cropped area’
as recorded in the early Agricultural Statistics of British India, as W.H.
Moreland, indeed, argued.6 But once we compare the drazi figures of each
pargana with those of the revenue expected from it (jamaVnaqdi), which
is entered in the very next column, the enormous variations in the rates of
revenue per bigha of drazi that result show immediately that the measured
area must have included much unsown area besides the taxed area or the
area actually cultivated. We set out in Table 2.2 the jama7drazi ratios in
Braj Bhum parganas in terms of dams/bigha.
    Let us, first, consider a possible inference from these figures, viz., that
these ratios represent great variations in agricultural productivity affecting
the taxable capacities of the different localities. In order to test this
(rather unlikely) possibility, Table 2.2 presents the revenue rates (dasturs)
for three major rabi crops (wheat, gram, barley) and four kharif crops


    5 The information is taken from the tables in the Ain~i Akbari for sarkars of
Agra, Koi and Sahar in suba Agra, and sarkdr of Delhi in stiba Delhi. These are
set out in British Library MSS Add. 7652 and Add. 6552 in their original form;

seldom less than one rupee*, each rupee being then worth 40 dams,8 The
context suggests that Abul Fazl has here in mind the net income that the
grantees could derive from their holding of a bigha, rather than the value
of the gross produce it yielded. In other words, this would approximate
to the net revenue the government expected to derive from a btgha of
cultivated land. Even if we allow for the fact that thejiaww' or naqdt stated
in the A’in-i Akbaris tables represents not the gross revenue collected, but
the expected net tax-income,9 it is inconceivable that any cultivated area
could have yielded an amount of less than 10 dams per bigha in net gain
to the state. And we should remember that we are not allowing anything
for state income from sources other than land, which if made would
further lower the amount of land-tax in relation to the ardzl. We must,
therefore, concede the fact that measurement must have included large
areas of untaxed cultivable waste, including pastures and long-abandoned
                      (Measurement here could, doubtless, mean accepting
fields and fallows.1011
past survey records or a rapid eye-survey by the assessor). Where the
measured area is exceptionally large in relation to the Jama* of the pargana,
one must assume, then, that the pargana had a particularly large expanse
of uncultivated land, the abandoned fields perhaps being marked on the
ground and thus measured but not paying tax. It denotes a situation where
the land-man ratio was very favourable, and large numbers of fields could
be left fallow throughout the year.
    The contrary assumption that where the J/A is high, the cultivated area
was much larger in extent relative to the total area, would be valid only
when one is reasonably certain that the measured area included the bulk of
the total surface area (let us call it the ‘map area’). As S. Moosvi has shown
from the A7n-t Akbaris own data, in large areas of eastern Uttar Pradesh,
only a small part of the map area had been measured.11 But in Braj Bhum

and its surrounding region, measurement would appear to have covered

within the coverage of the map. It can be seen that the statistics display
a definite pattern. Over a large belt west of the Yamuna, extending across
Braj Bhum, the J/A is lower than 10 dams in each pargana within the belt.

Jalesar. But for these two belts, the J/A is fairly high, exceeding 20 dams

parganas, then none except Mathura (an urban pargana, with few villages),

01, show a J/A higher than 20 dams, so that one can argue that, unlike
its surrounding areas, Braj Bhum was marked by a large extent of waste
and pasture. The sacred vans could thus have had an actual existence at
the time. In his memoir on Mathura, in 1882, ES. Growse dwelt on the
contrast between the relative aridity and extensive pasturages to the west
of the Yamuna, and the rich cultivation, with little room for pasturages,

waste in a belt on the east side of the Yamuna as well. It must also be said
that Braj Bhum as a whole shows lower levels of current cultivation than                !

    The distinct existence of zones where land-grants were apparently less
 frequently solicited, or made, may be of some ethnographical significance
 in view of the nature of the class of land-grantees. They were largely
 Muslim, with a preponderance of theological and professedly literate, if not
 learned, ‘respectable’ elements.16 The zone where the size of suyurghdl falls
 below 3 per cent ofjama*, then, must have contained a proportionately low
 level of Muslim middle-class population. This accords with what Growse
 found in the area lying to the west of the Yamuna within the district of


    15 Moosvi, Economy of Mughal Empire, pp. 159-61, 172—76.
    16 Habib, Agrarian System, 3rd edn., pp. 345-46, 352-57.
50                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 Mathura—an 'almost total absence of the Muhammadan elements in the
 population’.17
     A fact that Moosvi had marked from a scrutiny of parganas carrying
 large suyurghal figures in Uttar Pradesh was that they corresponded
 with urban concentrations, from which she drew the conclusion that
 'the grantees were in large part a town-based class’.18 In the case of Braj
 territory, this is borne out by the figures for Mathura: we know from our
 documents that this pargana could have had very few villages attached to
 it. The pargana Mahavan stretched along the east bank of the Yamuna
 northward up to beyond Mat; and to the west of the river, villages in
 Mathura’s vicinity, such as Sei, Atas, and Sonrakh, belonged to pargana
 Sahar, while close to it on the south was situated Maholi, the seat of a
 separate pargana. Much of the assessed revenue (januT) of the pargana of
 Mathura must therefore have come from urban taxation rather than land-
 tax, so that its high jamaVarazi rate did not necessarily represent a high
 land-revenue collection. Yet suyurghal here amounted to 6.04 per cent of
jama, while there was no suyurghal at all reported from Maholi, adjoining
 it on the south, and only to the extent of 0.85 per cent o£jama‘ from the
 large Sahar pargana adjoining it on the west and north. It is, however,
worth recalling that the priests of the temples of Vrindavan and Mathura
 also held large land-grants within the pargana of Mathura, presumably
alongside Muslim grantees.19
     In the final three columns of the A’ln’s tables against each pargana
we have the names of the zamlndar/burm castes, tribes or communities
 (qauni), followed by the numbers of horsemen and foot-retainers that the
zamindars maintained (see Table 2.1 in this Chapter as well as Table 1.1
 in Chapter 1). For this reason, it can be safely said that the A’ln-i Akbari
is India’s first ethnographic survey (although confined to the landholding
classes), just as it is our first statistical report, owing to the variety of
quantitative information it offers. Its degree of accuracy in respect of the

information about zamindars may be illustrated by one example. It tells
us that inpargana Mahavan, the zamindars were ‘Sayyids, Brahmans, etc?
In 1882, besides Brahmans and Jats, Growse in fact reports a large Sayyid
clan among the land-owners in Mahavan proper and four other villages.20
We can further deduce from the addition of the Jats to the list that the
Jats possibly obtained their zamindari in the pargana after the rise of the
Jat kingdom in the eighteenth century.
   If we confine our study of the caste-composition of zamindars within
Braj Bhum, based on castes recorded in the A’in-i Akbari, and appearing
in our Table 2.1, the position of the Rajput clans, whether specified
by their individual names or simply recorded under the designation of
'Rajput’, seems to have been a dominant one: they are present in 8 of
the 15 parganas. Among the Rajput clans, the Bachhal and Kachhwahas
are recorded in pargana Sahar. Now, Growse tells us that the Gauruas,
deemed an inferior caste among the Rajputs, have different septs called
Kachhwahas, Jasawats and Sisodiyas, the last more commonly called
Bachhals.21 The Bachhal and Kachhwahas, recorded in pargana Sahar,
were thus presumably Gauruas, forming a class of superior peasants,
whom we meet frequently in the Vrindavan documents from the area,
where we also encounter a Gaurua peasant calling himself a Jasawat, while
some other Gauruas belong to the Jadon sept.22
   As for the Jats, while they are not recorded in the A’in, under Mahavan,
they still appear among the zamindar castes in the Braj Bhum parganas of
Hodal, Noh, Sahar, Kaman and Koh Mujahid, as well as other neighbouring
parganas like Nonera, Bhadauli, and Khatumar, besides Agra. They thus
stood next to the Rajputs in the extent of their landed possessions. Meos
appear in only Kaman and Koh Mujahid, but are recorded in a line of
parganas adjoining Braj Bhum on its west. Gujars are only recorded under
Sahar, within Braj Bhum; and Muslims in general (‘Shaikhzadas’) only in
Ao, while Afghans are recorded in Noh. Rather surprisingly, Brahmans

   We conclude these notes with one on the major route that ran through
Braj territory west of the Yamuna, on which went travellers, messengers
and much merchandise. This was the highway that connected Agra with
Delhi.
   An early European traveller was William Finch, who in 1611 took
along this route twelve carts full of parcels of indigo meant for the Lahore
market. He mentions the places he passed through, viz., Badegsara (Bad
ki Sarai), Acabarpore (Akbarpur) and Houdle (Hodal). By some failure of
memory he confused Akbarpur with Mathura (which he fails to mention)
as being ‘famous for the antiquities of Indian gobins (gosdinsTj and
containing a temple with many idols nearby.23 Then we have the diary
of 1‘timad ‘All Khan who records the major places or stations on the
route between Agra and Delhi that he passed through during a journey
he undertook twice in 1716: it took him seven days when he did not stay
anywhere (except at nights). On the first journey he spent eight days
at Mathura. On both occasions, the places he mentioned as situated on
the road between Mathura and Delhi were Odal/Hodal (both spellings
given) and Farldabad.24 Finally, the Chahdr Gulshan of Rai Chaturman
(completed in 1760) gives us the following list of staging stations on the
route from Mathura leading north towards Delhi: Jait, Chhata, Deotana,
Kosi, Hodal and Banchari, followed by a number of other staging places
until one reached the fort of Shahjahanabad (Delhi). Towards Agra it
records Koela, Aurangabad, Ghoghat (not located), Jhandipur and Farah.25
   The line of the route indicated by these accounts is confirmed by the
positions of Mughal ‘^os-pillars’ that have survived, such being shown
on the Survey of India’s one-inch maps. These were set up by Jahangir
on the highway from Agra all the way to Lahore at every karoh or kos,
and he had previously ordered rows of trees to be planted by the side
of the road.26 Bernier noticed both the rows of trees and the kos pillars
while travelling from Delhi to Agra in 1663, attributing both correctly to

THE STATE
                              CHAPTER 3



              Akbar and the Temples of
                the Mathura Region
                 Tarapada Mukherjee and Irfan Habib




The numerous documents of Akbar’s reign, in what may be called the
Vrindavan Archives, are broadly divisible into two groups: (a) orders of
the Mughal administration concerning grants to the temples and temple­
servants and, in one case, temple management; and (b) sale-deeds of rights
to land acquired by or sold to temple-servants or devotees.
   The present chapter focuses on documents belonging to group (a).1
Full translations of the documents (except for Doc. No. 5, for which a
full translation is not necessary) are offered in Appendix 1 at the end of
the chapter. In our main text they are referred to by the number we have
given to their translation. If a document belongs to the VRI (Vrindavan
Research Institute), the collection and the number assigned to the
document concerned are indicated in the note preceding its translation.
Two forgeries (VRI acc. Nos. 2 and 36), not apparently recognized as such
so far, have been ignored in our study, but they are commented upon in
Appendix 2. The translations strictly follow the spellings of names given
in the original documents. In most cases they are of some interest as they
represent the way in which the names were then pronounced in Braj
country. The occurrence of the spelling ‘Mathur’ or ‘Mathura’ instead of
 Mathura’ is important for its confirming the early date of the documents.12

   1 Beside the Vrindavan collection and NAI, the only known documents from
Akbar’s reign belonging to this category and relating to Braj Bhum are those
published by Krishanlal Mohanlal Jhaveri, ed., Imperial Farmdns (ad 1577 to ad
1803) granted to the Ancestors ofHis Holiness the Tikayat Maharaj, Bombay, 1928.
  2 Abu’l Fazl in the A’in-i Akbari (c.1595) already uses the more accurate
58                        Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

   The documents presented here tell us much that is of interest about
the temples of Mathura and Vrindavan, and about the implementation of
Akbar’s religious policy. In addition, they shed light on other matters of
historical interest, notably Mughal administrative practices. We propose
to cover the latter ground too, but it is best to first concentrate on the
temples and their relations with the Mughal administration.


                                         II

Akbar’s farman in respect of‘Gobind Rai’ (Govind-dev) temple (Doc. No.
4), on its zimn (reverse) gives us a statement of all the grants previously
made to temple-servants. These were conferred through four farmans, the
earliest of which was issued on 5 Jumada II 972/8 January 1565. It is our
good fortune that this has been preserved in the collection of the Madan
Mohan Temple (our Doc. No. 1 in Appendix 1 of this chapter).
    This farman assigns two hundred bighas of land in in dm (revenue-free
gift) to Gopaldas. There is no indication in the text of the farman that
Gopaldas had any religious status; but the listing of the grant as one to
temples/temple-servants in Doc. No. 4 and its preservation in the Madan
Mohan Temple archives both show that this must have been the case.
Indeed, it was, apparently, on this basis that the Madan Mohan Temple
was deemed in Doc. No. 4 to be holding 120 bighas by previous grants,
and its sister-temple Govind-dev, 70 bighas [Doc. 4, zimn, 11(b) (i) &
(ii)]. Both these temples belonged to the Chaitanya sect, and our Doc.
No. 2 shows that in 1568, their management (adhikar) was vested in a
single person, Jiv Gosa’in (Jiva Goswami).3 Eleven years later, ‘Gopaldas
pujari, son of Radha Ballabh, appears as the representative (wakil) of ‘Jiv
Gosa’in, son of Ballabh Gosa’in’, purchaser of land in village Arith alias
Radhakund, in a sale-deed dated 9 Shawwal 987/29 November 1579,4 Our
Doc. No. 3 shows Todar Mai making a grant of 100 bighas to ‘Gopaldas,



spelling ‘Mathura*. The farman of 1598 (Docs. 4 and 5), issued upon the
recommendations of a committee headed by him, nevertheless consistently uses
the spelling ‘Matura(h)’.
   3 Jiv or Jlu or Ji in Hindi derives from Sanskrit Jiva\ and similarly sain is the
popular Hindi form of Skt. Svami. Hence Jiv or Jlu Gosa’in for Jiva Gosvami.
Henceforth in this chapter we will use the more common spelling; Swami.
   4 This document is not numbered, and the collection to which it belongs does
not appear from the photocopy.
              Akbar and the Temples of the Mathura Region                 59

sevak (servant) of Sri Madan Mohan (temple)’ in Rabi‘ II 992/April-May
 1584. Gopaldas here must then be the same as the recipient of the grant
in Doc. No. 1: he must be held to have received the imperial grant of 200
bighas in 1565 in his capacity as zpujdri and servant of the Madan Mohan
Temple. His subordinate relationship to Jiv Goswami is established by the
sale-deed of 1579 we have just cited. He was still alive in 1598, since Doc.
No. 4 does not record him as dead, a statement it makes separately about
all the other grantees whose names it furnishes.
    The reverse side (zimri) of Doc. No. 1 is obscured through having large
pieces of paper pasted over it. We therefore miss the kind of information
we are given in the zimn of Doc. No. 4, where we are told that the grant
to Gopaldas was made ‘on the report’ (ba-risdla) of Raja Bhar Mai, the
ruler of Amber, and the first Rajput chief of importance to enter Akbar’s
service (1561). The association of Raja Bhar Mai with the imperial grant
to Gopaldas in 1565 suggests that the patronage extended by the Amber
court to the Chaitanya establishments of Madan Mohan and Govind-dev
long preceded Man Singhs munificent reconstruction of the Govind-dev
Temple in Akbar’s 34th regnal year (1590).5
    Our documents Nos. 2 and 3 show that the priests of the Vrindavan
temples cast their nets yet wider and were also able to attract the attention
of Akbar’s famous minister, Todar Mai. Doc. No. 2 is zfarmdn explicitly
issued on the representation of that minister in favour of ‘Jiv Gosa’in’.
The farmdn is dated 14 Rabi4 II 976/6 October 1568.6 It implies that Jiv
Gosa’in, ‘a poor man, praying for His Majesty’, needed to be protected in
his right of management (adhikar) of the two temples Madan Mohan and
‘Gobind Rai’ (Govind-dev), entrusted to him by his uncle Rup Gos’ain. He
was accordingly assured by the farman of such protection, and, further, of
the right to claim all offerings (khairat) at the temples and to transfer or
will away such right. Jiv Goswami ultimately exercised the last-mentioned
right, and his will dated 1606 and 1608 has survived.7

    Todar Mai maintained his interest in the two temples, and in 1584,
when he apparently held the area in Jagir, made a grant of one hundred
bighas of land, ‘cultivated, khwud-kashta’ to Gopaldas, the ‘sevak of the
Madan Mohan temple’. The grant of revenue of land already wholly
cultivated was a mark of special favour; but what the term khwud-kashta
(lit. ‘self-cultivated*) implies here is not very clear. It may mean that
the land was already being cultivated by, or under the management of,
Gopaldas; or, if this was not the case, that the grantee was free to evict the
peasants and make it khwud-kashtaf This particular grant was converted
into an Imperial grant by Jahangir through a Jarman of his eighth regnal
year (1613), issued in favour of‘Srichand, sevak (servant) of devala Sri
Madan Mohan’, successor to Gopaldas, who was by then dead.8           9 Such
conversion prevented the grant from being subject to the favour of each
succeeding jagjrdar of the area.


                                        Ill

To return to our Docs. Nos, 1 and 2: Doc. 1 shows that as early as January
 1565 Akbar had become willing to confer revenue-grants for the benefit
of temples. It is, in any case, the earliest known surviving revenue-grant
to a Hindu divine made by Akbar. Doc. No. 2 shows his willingness to
interfere in matters of temple-management and, in effect, appoint the
recipient of temple offerings. A noteworthy feature of the Jarman is that
the zimn contains the endorsement by Akbar’s well-known (and orthodox)
Sadru’s Sudiir (minister in charge of grants), Shaikh ‘Abdu’n Nabi, with all
his titles. The endorsement means that officially the Jarman was supposed
to be a grant, as if the custody of temple offerings emanated from the
Emperor, and so fell within the jurisdiction of the Sadrus Sudiir,
   The two documents may be placed alongside some early episodes
of Akbar’s reign. A Mughal officer, writing of incidents he witnessed,
speaks of a conversation Akbar had with his then Governor of the
Panjab, Shamsu’ddin Atka, in ah 968/1560-1. While on a march, when
the emperor taxed him with his letting his head hair grow, Shamsu’ddin

Atka replied that since Mathura was on their route, he would have his
hair shaved there, just as the Hindus do while visiting that place. The
emperor, we are told, was pleased at the reply?0 In fact, the friendliness
towards his Hindu subjects soon manifested itself in more substantive
forms: the abolition of the pilgrimage tax came in 1562 and of thenzvu
(poll-tax on non-Muslims) in 1564. These measures mentioned by Abu’l
Fazl were probably of real substance and not mere formal gestures.11 Our
Docs. Nos. 1 and 2 suggest that the change in the 1560s went beyond
nominal declarations: there was a new outlook at work in dealing with the
temples and the holy men ofVrindavan and Mathura, and so presumably
elsewhere as well.
    Indeed, the fact that land-grants to religious individuals among the
Hindus began to be made within the 1560s is also attested by later official
documents of 1719 and 1722 (IVS 159 and 191 -2).which refer to the
continuance of a madad-i ma‘ash grant originally made by Akbar in ah
976/1568-9 to one Madho (priest of the Gopinath Temple, Vrindavan) in
village Raj pur, near Vrindavan. The grant was large, amounting originally
to 100 bighas, but reduced to 76 bighas 2 biswas upon the subsequent change
to measurement by bamboo rod and the introduction of gaz-i ildhi—
reference to which reductions reinforces our belief in the genuineness of
the original grant. In the surviving zimn of a later document (IVS 175),
we find a similar reference to a grant made by Akbar on 11 Rabi* II 984
 (8 July 1576) of an area of 30 bighas to one Bhagwandas in a village
 (Hasang) of pargana Mahaban (Mahavan), near Mathura across the river
Yamuna.
    The grants subsequently made to temples in the area before 1598 have
 not survived. Doc. No. 4, which we may expect to give us comprehensive
 information, lists three other farmans of grants, the earliest of which is
 dated 4 Rajab 983/9 October 1575, granting Chain Gopal (dead by 1598)
 an area of 70 bighas of land, on the report of Shahbaz Khan,10  12 in village

     Soon afterwards, again, ‘on the report of Shahbaz Khan’, a farmdn was
 issued in Shawwal 983/January 1576, conferring grants of 320 bighas on
 five persons (all dead by 1598). An area of 160 bighas was still held in 1598
 on the basis of the original grant (by their successors, presumably). The
 grants were located in villages Dosaij (Dosaich) and Dhaulera. Since the
 Akrur temple was deemed to hold a grant in Dhaulera, one of the grantees,
 Ramdas, to whom land was assigned in that village, was apparently a priest
 of this temple. Besides the Madan Mohan and ‘Gobind Rai’ temples,
 already accounted for by Doc. No. 1, the other temples recognized as
 holding grants previously in village Dosaij were those of Gopinath, Radha
Ballabh, and Nandnandan; and we may infer that the other four grantees
were priests of these temples. Apparently, the grant made to Madho, priest
 of the Gopinath Temple, in 1568-9 was overlooked in 1598. As to who
was the priest of that temple among the four deceased grantees holding
grants in Dosa ij (Vrindavan) by the farmdn of 1576 cannot be determined,
except that it could not have been Harbans. For he, given an original grant
of 100 bighas, is surely Harivans, founder of the Radhavallabhi sect. He
was, according to tradition, born in Samvat 1559 (ad 1502), so he could
well have died between 1576 and 1598. He established the Radhavallabh
Temple at Vrindavan; and the grant of 70 bighas by gaz-i ildhi in 1598 was
in lieu of 40 bighas, in the old measure, to which the original area of the
temple’s grant (100 bighas) had been reduced after Harivans’s death.13
    In 1577 there occurred an incident in which Mathura figured, and
which seemed to bring the breach between Akbar and the Muslim
orthodoxy still more into the open. ‘Abdu’r Rahim, the qdzi of Mathura,
complained that ‘a rich Brahman of that place took away materials that
the complainant had assembled to build a mosque, and used them for a
temple the Brahman himself was constructing’. When taxed with this,
the latter spoke abusively of the Prophet and Muslims. An enquiry by
Birbal and Abul Fazl was instituted, and Abu’l Fazl, at least, found that
there was testimony enough to support the complaint. The Brahman was
thereupon detained. Notwithstanding Akbar’s unwillingness to award him

   13 The information on Harivans is drawn from Growse, District Memoir,

 any extreme punishment, his Sadr (Minister for grants) Shaikh ‘Abclu’n
 Nabi, had him executed. Not only was Akbar greatly annoyed over this, but
 theologians also came forward to point out that according to the Hanafite
 school of Muslim law that was followed in India, a non-Muslim subject
 of a Muslim ruler could not be punished for blasphemy. When Badauni,
 to whom we owe a detailed report of the incident, tried to defend Shaikh
 ‘Abdu’n Nabl’s action, this aroused such fury in Akbar that Badauni had
 to retire precipitately from the Emperors presence.14
    The incident, in any case, does not seem to have had any effect on
 Akbar’s policy about grants to temples in Mathura and its vicinity. By a
farmdn issued on 28 Ziqa’d 987/16 January 1580, ‘on the report of Raja
 Blrbar’, or Birbal, a grant of 100 bighas of land was made to Ramdas
 (dead by 1598) in village Gantoli, pargana Sahar. Since the temples of
 Govardhan and Hari Gopal Rai were deemed in 1598 to hold exactly 100
 bighas in the same village from the previous grant, Ramdas was almost
 certainly a priest of one of these two temples. Thus by 1580 at least seven
 temples (devalas) in the environs of Mathura had become beneficiaries of
 Imperial grants of tax-free lands.
    On 9 December 1581 Akbar himself visited Mathura, 'that ancient
 pilgrim town, in order to observe the spectacle of those sunk in the
 domain of the search for Reality’.15 Whether he visited temples or merely
 conversed with Mathura’s religious men is not made clear. In any case, no
 grants to temples seem to have followed immediately afterwards. Indeed,
 to go by the official information given in Doc. No. 4, there was no further
 Imperial revenue grant made to any of the temples in the Mathura region
 during the eighteen years following 1580. This reinforces one’s doubts
 about the authenticity of two farmans of 1593 reproduced by Jhaveri
 granting tax-remissions in one village and making a revenue-grant of the
 entire village of Gokul, to Gosain Vithalrai for the expenses of the temple
 of ‘Gordhan Nath’ (Govardhan Nath) at Gokul. The farmans do not seem
 to have been prepared in the Imperial chancery, though there are otherwise
 no obvious errors in the texts such as are found in the two VRI forgeries
 discussed in Appendix 2 of this chapter.16 However, this was the period in

                                       IV

We may now consider the contents of what we believe to be one of the
most fascinating documents regarding religious grants that came out of
Akbar’s chancery, namely, Doc. No. 4. This contains his farman of 1598,
which record^ land-grants made to thirty-five named temples of Mathura
and its environs. From what is stated on the zimn (reverse) of the farman,
the way in which it came to be issued was as follows:
   Akbar ordered Shaikh Abu’l Fazl, his celebrated counsellor and
historian, to enquire into the merits of the different temples of Mathura,
Bindraban, Govardhan, Nandgram (Nandgaon), etc., and propose the area
of land-grant that would be appropriate for each. We know that at this
time Akbar’s court was stationed in Lahore, the city having been the
capital of the empire for upwards of thirteen years (Akbar finally left
Lahore on 26 Aban 43 Z/dbi/18 November 1598).19 Abul Fazl was assigned
the assistance of four persons, ‘Batacharaj, Chintaman Bhat, Upendar

Misr, and Bhat (?) Pandit’. The names of the first three can be easily
restored to Bhattacharya, Chintamani Bhat, and Upendra Misra, while
at least the word ‘Pandit’ in the fourth name is clear enough. These were
obviously Brahman divines of some status or repute, who were supposed
to possess special knowledge about temples of Mathura and its environs.
None of them, however, is otherwise heard of in the annals of Akbar’s
court. Since the court was then at Lahore, it is not likely that Abul Fazl
and his ‘committee’ actually visited Mathura. We are then left with the
possibility that the four Brahman divines were themselves from the area
concerned, very possibly pleading the case for different groups of temples
there; and Abul Fazl was appointed to consult with them in order to
frame appropriate proposals for the Emperors approval.
    These proposals were put before Akbar on 24 Khurdad 43/14 June
 1598 (Gregorian calendar). The date is interesting for a reason connected
with Abu’l Fazls life: his position at the court had encountered a crisis,
and he temporarily withdrew from the court and all official work on 11
Khurdad/1 June.2021  He returned to duty soon afterwards, but it is not clear
whether he undertook the present task before the crisis, or later. There is
no reference to his work connected with these grants in his Akbarndma.
Despite Akbar’s approval having been obtained on 14 June, the farmdn
was formally issued only on 19 Shahriwar (11 September), though the
 full statement (ta'liqa) copied on the zimn had already been prepared on
 13 Amurdad (5 August),
    The proposals put to Akbar were framed in three categories (shiqqs),
 presumably high, medium, and low as regards the area of grants to be
 conferred. Akbar prudently accepted the second, i.e. presumably the
 medium-level scheme. As accepted, the proposals were in two parts. The
 first part (Zimn, II (A)) dealt with the existing grants. These were found
 to have been made through four farmans granted in favour of eight persons
 in total. Some area having apparently been resumed after the deaths of the
 original grantees, and a certain area deducted on account of the introduction
 of the bamboo-rod, the net area held under previous grants was found to
 be 504 bighasf It was now proposed to supersede these previous grants

by assigning the previously granted area to seven temples,22 with which
the eight grantees were apparently connected, the total area so confirmed
being deemed to be 500 bighas ilahi (Zimn, 11(B)). This was actually on
the generous side, since the previous grants were made in terms of the
older bigha, and the replacement of that unit by the bigha ilahi in Akbar’s
31st or 33rd regnal year would generally have required a reduction in every
grant by at least 10.54%.23 A yet more important feature of the change
proposed was that the previous grants to named persons were now to be
converted into grants to temples as institutions—a practice exceedingly
rare in the Mughal system of revenue-grants. Since the institutions were,
so to speak, deathless, the grants by such conversion became perpetual.
   The second part (Zimn, III) consisted of additional grants proposed for
twenty-eight other temples. The names of the temples already benefiting
from previous grants did not occur in this list, possibly except for the
temple of ‘Gordhan’ (Govardhan) which was assigned a further 9 bighas.24
The total grant proposed was to be of 500 bighas ilahi, half to lie in
cultivated land and half in waste. The average area of land granted to
these temples was naturally smaller than that of the seven temples already
holding lands. But the effect was that the total size of revenue-grants to
the temples of the region was doubled in one stroke. Since a bigha ilahi
was about six-tenths of an acre,25 the total area of the revenue-grants now
decreed amounted to about 600 acres (300 acres held previously and 300
now granted), 600 acres being equal to nearly 243 hectares.
   The temples were finally (Zimn IV (A) and (B)) put into seven groups,
each consisting of a major temple and what were presumably its sister or
daughter foundations. Thus the temple of‘Gobind Rai’ (Govind-dev) was

grouped with Akrur, Krishan Rai Jalori, and Radha Rawan, while the

   All farmans, excepting the one issued to the Govind-dev temple, were

carried only the essential details pertaining to their respective groups of

   It is most fortunate that the farman to the Govind-dev temple (our Doc.
No. 4) has survived in the original (now in the National Archives),26 while
there is also an early copy (photographed by the team of the International
Association of VRI). Both Docs. 4 and 5 show the farmans as having
been prepared ‘on the report’ (ba-risala) of Azam Khan or Khan-i A‘zam
(Akbar’s foster-brother Mirza ‘Aziz Koka, then the Vakil, the minister
highest in status at the court)27 and during the chauki (‘watch’) of one
of Akbar’s favourite officers, Ramdas Kachhwaha. Doc. No. 4 (original)
predictably contains Mirza ‘Aziz Koka’s seal; but it also bears that of the
famous general of Akbar and Braj poet, ‘Abdur Rahim Khan-i Khanan.
The processing of these grants should have involved the office of the
Sadru’s Sudur, but that minister is mentioned nowhere. However, our
Doc. No. 6 shows that copies of these^rmam went to him whereupon he
too issued parwanchas or orders informing the officials concerned of the
issuance of the grants.28

                                     V

For the history of the temples of the area, the most valuable service our
Doc. No. 4 renders is to provide us with a list of the major temples
of Mathura and its neighbourhood. The list does not give us the exact
situations (village/town-quarter) of the temples, the villages mentioned
being those not necessarily where the temples, but their land-grants, were
located. At the same time, the area of grant assigned to each may represent
roughly the comparative assessment of its status and popular prestige in
the eyes of the Mughal administration.
   The temples, as we have seen, were divided into seven groups for the
convenience of issuing a limited number of farmans. Each group was
headed by a major temple, and we may begin by considering these temples.
   The devala or temple of‘Gobind Rai’, to which Doc. No. 4 was issued,
is mentioned already in our Doc. No. 2 of 1568 as being situated in ‘the
village of Bindraban’ or Vrindavan. It is the temple of Govind-dev which
was rebuilt by Raja Man Singh in 1590, the date being recorded in an
inscription. It is quite well known as a representative piece of Mughal
architecture, and there is a fairly long description of it by Growse.29
    The farmdn carried in our Doc. No. 4 shows that there were three
temples with separate grants, but attached to Govind-dev. The first of these,
Akrur, still exists in a village of this name (Akraur in old maps) close to
Dhaulera or Dhaurera (between Mathura and Vrindavan), where its grant
was also located. The second temple Krishan Rai cannot be identified, but
the third, Radha Rawan is Radha Raman, located in Vrindavan, though
apparently provided with a new building in the nineteenth century.30 It is
a little surprising that the temple of Radha Damodar, built at Vrindavan
by Jiv Goswami as a daughter-foundation of Govind-dev, is not listed in
the farmdn 31
    We know from our Doc. No. 2 of 1568 that Jiv Goswami (‘Jiv Gosain’)
held the management (adbikar) of the temples of ‘Madan Mohan and
Govind Rai’, so that these two were sister-foundations, which indeed was
the case since both are said to have been built by Jiv s uncles, Rup and
 Sanatan.32 But apparently after Man Singhs munificent reconstruction

Athar Ali, Apparatus of Empire, entry no. A438).

of the Govind-dev temple, the two became independent entities; and the
Madan Mohan temple (with its own individual grant of 100 bighas as
against the 70 bighas of Govind-dev) headed a separate group of three
temples, to which a separate farman was issued (Doc. No. 5). Like the
Govind-dev temple it was situated at Vrindavan (Doc. No. 1), and there
is, again, a good description of it in Growse.33
    Doc.No. 5 attaches the grants of two other temples to Madan
Mohan, namely, Dwadas Adat, and Jasodha Nandan. The latter cannot
be identified,34 but the former having its grant assigned at Sonrakh
near Vrindavan might have been situated at Jahangirpur Adda nearby,
its second name ‘Adat’/Adda surviving after the place was presumably
renamed during Jahangirs reign.
    Thefarmans issued to the five other groups of temples have not survived,
and we are, therefore, unable to say how the remaining twenty-eight
temples were grouped under them. However, we may assume that the
temples whose names are given for identifying each group were regarded
as the other major temples of the area. The first of these is Gopinath (70
bighas). It was situated at Vrindavan, and its founder Raesil Ji is said to
have received a grant from Akbar. But, in fact, the recipient of Akbar’s
grant in 1568-9, as we have seen, was Madho, the first historically attested
priest of the Gopinath temple. The temples architectural style has been
pronounced similar to that of Madan Mohan.35
    The foundation of the temple of Radhaballabh at Vrindavan (70 bighas)
was ascribed to Harivans, and, as previously noted, it is almost certain that
he is the same as Harbans, the recipient of an imperial grant in village
Dosaij in January 1576 (Doc. No, 4, at 11(a) (i) (4)).36 The temple of
Radhaballabh had its grant (70 bigha) allotted out of the land previously
granted in Dosaij, so that ‘Harbans’, on whom the earlier land-grant was
conferred, could well have been a priest of this temple. The temple is
described by Growse.37

    The temple of Keshav Rai received its grant (45 bighas out of the
additional grants recommended in 1598) within the limits of the township
of Mathura. It is almost certainly the famous Keshav-Rai temple of that
city, rebuilt by Bir Singh Bundela, and destroyed in 1670 under orders
of Aurangzeb.38 The temple of Nandrai was assigned its grant (out of
the additional quota) of 30 bighas in Nandgram (Nandgaon), so a strong
presumption can be made that it stood at the site of a large temple of the
same name, supposed by Growse to have been built in the mid-eighteenth
century.39
    Finally, there is the pair ‘Gordhan and Hari Gopal Rai’ which had 100
bighas assigned to them from the previously held grants in village Gantoli.
The temple of ‘Gordhan’ is also mentioned in a farman of Akbar of 1588,
which grants exemption from tax on ‘the cows and bulls of Gordhan’,
pasturing in certain villages.40 This was apparently the shrine of Shri Nath
or Govardhandhara founded by Vallabhacharya of Gokul on the ridge of
Govardhan; there was apparently much disputation over its possession
between his followers and those of the Chaitanya sect.41 The temple of
‘Hari Gopal Rai* seems to be the same as the Hari-dev of Govardhan,
said to have been built during Akbar’s reign under the patronage of Raja
Bhagwandas of Amber.42
    On the remaining temples named in the Farman the following notes
can be offered.
    Nandnandan. Growse notices a temple of this name at Nandgaon;43 but
its identification with the one mentioned in Akbar’s farman is unlikely
since its land-grant was assigned at Dosaij within Vrindavan.
   Jugal Kishor (now called Purana Jugal Kishor temple, near Keshi Ghat).
Said to have been built at Vrindavan in 1627, but it is possibly older or was
built on the site of an earlier temple of the same name.44

   Bihari Jiu. Probably the temple of this name (Bihari Ji also called
Banke Bihari) at Vrindavan, since the grant of 25 bighas to it is assigned
in Dosaij. The present temple of this name is of modern construction.45
   Bhuteshar. The temple of Bhutesvar Mahadev at Mathura.46 The grant
of 25 bighas entirely from the township of Mathura shows its relative
importance in Akbar’s time. Bhuteshwar is shown on maps as a locality on
the north-western skirts of Mathura city.
   Dharu. The assignment of a grant of 8 bighas within the township of
Mathura suggests it was situated in that city, and it must therefore be the
older shrine dedicated to Dhruva Ji whose ruins existed at the time that
Growse wrote.47
   Chaturbhoj. The old temple of Chaturbhoj at Paitha or Petha must be
the one meant since the grant was also assigned in that village.48
   Shaikhsai. The temple was obviously named after the village Shaikhsai
(app. 27° 53’N, 77° 27’E), east of Hodal. The name is derived from Sesasayi
(‘Sessai’ in Growse) ‘where Krishna revealed his divinity by assuming the
emblems of Narayan’.49 Naturally enough its grant of 20 bighas was located
in a village (unidentified) in pargana Hodal.
   Saket Pat. Obviously, a temple at Sanket between Nandgaon and
Barsana, where Krishna and Radha used to meet. The temple must be the
same as the one now called Radha Raman, whose original structure is said
to have been built by Todar Mai in Akbar’s time.50
   Radhakund. One of the two tanks where Krishna is held to have purified
himself after killing the bull Arishta.51 The tank named after Radha has
now given its name to the village, which was called Arith (giving its name
to Arishta, the bull?) till Akbar’s time and much later (see Chapter 8). The
grant of the temple was assigned within this village.
   Barkhan Pur (?). This is undoubtedly the temple dedicated to Brikhbhan
at Barsana,52 where its grant (10 bighas) was situated.

    Gordhan. This must have been the temple named Govardhan at
 Mathura, since it has a rather small grant (9 bighas) assigned in that
 township.
    Sankri Khor Ballabh Jiu. There are ‘two little shrines’ at the ravine of
 Sankri Khor near Barsana, one dedicated to Krishna, the other to Radha.53
 One or both of them could be meant.
    A word might be said about the places where the grants were situated,
which were likely to be close to where the temples stood. As the farmdn
 itself indicates, of the total grant of 1000 bighas, an area of 730 bighas lay
 in pargana Mathura; and of this area again as many as 469 bighas were
given from village Dosaij (or Dosaich). This village was later absorbed
into the town of Vrindavan, where its name survives in Dusayat, a quarter
of the city.54 Village Dosaij contained also a hamlet (nagla), within which
too grants were situated. It may be inferred that Dosaij was marked as a
source of grants for the temples of Vrindavan. Temples which have not
otherwise been identified, but whose grants have been assigned to Dosaij,
should therefore be looked for in Vrindavan. These are Jasodha Nandan;
Nand Nandan; Radhakarun Chakamba; Banas Si(?) Thakur; Kalyan Rai;
Radha Jiwan; Radha Krishan; and Har (?) Chand.
    The township of Mathura furnishes 136 bighas of grants, and it may
again be assumed that temples obtaining grants there were situated
within Mathura town. Of the other two villages in pargana Mathura from
which grants were assigned, Raj pur is to the south of Vrindavan and is
now practically its suburb; and Dhaulera (Dhaurera) is situated between
Vrindavan and Mathura.
    Next to pargana Mathura, pargana Sahar contributed 215 bighas of
land in grants. The villages where grants were located are Gantoli, a few
miles south-west-west of Govardhan; Sonrakh, about three miles west
of Vrindavan; Nandgram (Nandgaon); Barsana; Chak Sauli; and Arith.
We have not been able to locate Dokan. Arith furnished a grant of only
10 bighas, but relating to this village we happen to have a number of
documents from Akbar’s reign, arising out of Jiv Goswamis acquisitions
of land there. These show (e.g. the sale-deed of 1579 already referred to
earlier in respect of the identity of Gopaldas) that Arith was then the
proper name of the present village of Radhakund, its then emerging alias.
But by Growses time, the earlier name, which recalled Aristha, the bull
slain by Krishna, had completely disappeared; and now Aring is popularly

                                     VI

 It may seem a digression from the main theme of this chapter, but
 comments seem called for on information that our documents provide
 on some matters of administrative history. Those familiar with the A ’in-i
Akbari may have noticed that it lists i/(wr«r-circles under various sarkdrs
 or divisions of subas (provinces), these circles being groups of contiguous
parganas assigned the same revenue-rates (dasturu'l ‘amals) on various
crops. It is not precisely established how each of these revenue-circles
came to be formed. In sarkdr Agra, there were three such circles: Agra,
Bayana, and Etawa. Mathura and the surrounding parganas belonged
not to the circle of Agra, but to that of Bayana.56 The reason for this
is now found in our Doc. No. 1 (of ad 1565), which shows Mathura to
have then belonged to the sarkdr of Bayana, not Agra. The dastilr-circle
must, therefore, have been framed before this sarkdr was dissolved and
absorbed in sarkdr Agra. When the latter event occurred, the revenue rates
previously enforced could not be altered, so that Bayana had to continue
with Mathura and the other parganas grouped under its name, as a dastur-
circle separate from that of Agra.
    Doc. No. 4, of 1598, sheds some light on the pargana boundaries of the
time, since it mentions the parganas to which the villages belonged where
the grants were assigned. Thus, the limits of pargana Sahar came up to
the doorstep of Vrindavan, since Sonrakh was in that pargana. Similarly,
‘Gordhan’ (Govardhan) belonged to pargana Mangotla, but both Arith
to its east and Gantoli to its west belonged to pargana Sahar. To the
south-west of Mathura, Maholi was the headquarters of another pargana.
This meant that pargana Mathura must have been rather small, its limits

  5i Growse, District Memoir, pp. 62, 83 & n., 381.
  56 Ain, vol. I, p. 356.
74                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 not extending beyond Vrindavan to the north, and Yamuna in the east,
 bordering Maholi in the south and falling well short of Govardhan to the
 west.5758
     The earliest document known thus far, which uses the term jagir or
jagirdar (for territorial revenue-assignment or its holder) is a farmdn of
 Akbar, dated 1 'Rabi’ I 967/1 December 1559, in the possession of the
 late Maulana Raza Ansari of Farangi Mahal, Lucknow (photocopy in the
 library of the Department of History of AMU). This gives the spelling
jaigirdar not jagirdar, as in later documents. This form of spelling is
 important as it indicates that the term had recently come into use, with
 the form jaigirdar clear in the writers’ minds. Our Document No. 1 (of
January 1565) too gives us the earlier form of the spelling (jaigir), while
 it is not found in any of the later documents, which simply employ the
 shortened formja^fr.
     There is a reference in Doc. No. 1 to the ‘Rule of Half ’ (tarlq-i mundsifa)
 which applied when a revenue-grant was conferred for the first time. The
farmdn also makes clear what these words meant, viz., that half of the
 area of the grant was to be given in cultivated land (mazritj and half in
 waste (ufiadd). This practice was inherited from the Sur administration,
 and the rule is specifically set out in the A’ln-i Akbarl.™ Our Doc. No.
 1 shows that it was being applied as a matter of course in 1565 and so
 confirms our other evidence to the same effect. It will be seen that Doc.
 No. 4 (issued in 1598) does not apply this rule to grants held from the
 past, since the grantees were expected to have extended cultivation once
 they had obtained land under the grant. But in the case of the new or
  additional’ grants the ‘Rule of Half’ {‘Bij Mundsifa1) is insisted upon
 in both Docs. Nos. 4 and 5. The rule thus remained sturdily in force
 throughout Akbar’s reign.
     Farmdns of revenue-grants normally contained lists of taxes, cesses, etc.,
 from which the grantees were exempted. Our Docs. 1, 4, and 5 have lists
 similar to those in other such documents. Since these have been discussed
 by earlier editors of similar documents, notably by B.N. Goswamy and J.S.




   57 Since pargana Sahar belonged to sarkar Sahar, these particulars would
require a redrawing of the Agcu-sarkdr boundary north of Mathura, bringing it
southwards, much closer to that town than it is in Irfan Habib, Atlas of the
Mughal Empire, New Delhi, 1982, Sheet 8A.
   58 The evidence is discussed in Habib, Agrarian System, 1st ed., p. 302; 3rd
ed., pp. 346-47,
              Akbar and the Temples of the Mathura Region                   75

Grewal, the need to discuss them here is obviated.59 The one term which
earlier editors do not seem to have discussed, but which all of our three
documents contain and which is also found in many other documents of
Akbar’s reign as well, is savart. Tek Chand ‘Bahar’ in his famous dictionary,
Bahar-i ‘Ajam (c. 1740), says it meant gifts and presents, and points out
that it occurs in Yazdis Zafarndma (History of Timur).60 Significantly
enough, all three of our documents group savart with two other terms of
the same meaning, qunalgha and peshkasb, though this may not necessarily
be the case in other grants of Akbar’s reign.
   One final digression, in the interest of a special curiosity: Todar Mais
seal on Doc. No. 3, dated 1584, reads: ‘Todar Mai, Banda-i Dargdh, Ram
Ki Panah\ thus expressing both his loyalty to Akbar, and his allegiance to
Rama, the latter in a Hindi phrase. This seal with the same text was seen
in ifarman of Akbar, dated 4 Rabi‘ II 983/13 July 1575.61 That Akbar
permitted the use of such a seal on his own farman as early as mid-1570’s
is once again proof that his possible shift to orthodox Islam at that time
was not made at the expense of religious tolerance.




   59 B.N. Goswamy and J.S. Grewal, The Mughals and the Jogis ofjakhbar, Simla,
1967, notes to the translations of documents, passim.
  60 Tek Chand ‘Bahar’, Bahar-i ‘Ajam, litho, ed., Nawal Kishor, Lucknow,

  61 Examined by Irfan Habib when its owner, Mr. Mohd Akbar Ali, Vakil,

1960s. A transcript (but unfortunately not a photograph) is preserved in the
Department of History Library.

[Endorsement on margin:]
  It is established that next year a newparwancha would not be demanded.


4. Grants to Thirty-five Temples of Mathura and environs,
1598. Farman issued to Gobind Dev Temple, etc.

Note: The translation follows the text of the original/arman in the National
Archives of India, NAI-2671 (1) (our ‘A’). Wherever ‘A* is damaged or
undecipherable, the text of an early copy in VRI (‘B’)70 has been used.
It will be seen that a substantial part of the main text of the farman

round brackets by us, the closing bracket being marked with an asterisk.*

preserved in the original (‘A’). Wherever ‘A’ is clear, the variations (or
omissions) in ‘B’ are not noted, since ‘B’ is merely a copy of‘A’.

                 Appendix 2: The Forgeries

NOTE ON TWO FORGED ‘FARMANS OF AKBAR

1.  VRI, Acc. No. 2, purports to be & farman of Akbar granting 70 bighas
    of land ‘pukhta’, without deduction on account of the measuring rod,
    to Rup Gosain, with sons, generation after generation, in the environs
    of ‘Mominabad’. Dated 1st Sha'ban 16 ry. Hijri.
        Several seals and endorsement on the back, one endorsement
    dated 947(1). The main endorsement is in the name of Sadrus Sudur
    Shuja'at Khan.
       The farman is an obvious forgery. It uses the name Mominabad
    for Bindraban, the latter being the form of its name in use in Akbar’s
    time, while the name 'Mominabad’ was given to the place only in
    Aurangzeb’s reign (1659-1707). The deduction on account of the
    measuring rod could be instituted only after the bamboo-rod was
    introduced in Akbar’s 19th ry, so that there was no question of it
    being so mentioned in the 16th ry The word ‘pukhta’, in the sense
    of the larger bigha was not in use in the sixteenth century. There was
    no Sadrus Sudur of Akbar by the name of Shuja at Khan. While the
   farman is put in Akbar’s 18th ry (1571-2), an endorsement is dated
   ah 947 or ad 1544-5, when the Surs were ruling over the territory!
    The indifferent quality of the writing in the title of tughrd, with even
    the kdfstroke in Akbar’s name missing, shows it could not have come
    from the Imperial chancery. Finally, Akbar’s court used the Hijri
    calendar without reference to his regnal years, until the Ilahi calendar
    replaced Hijri dates, and so no official document of Akbar’s time
    could bear a regnal year with a Hijri month.
2. VRI, Acc. No. 36, purporting to be zfarman of Akbar, recalling a grant
    of 200 bighas pukhta, in the environs of 'Brindaban alias Mominabad
    in the sarkdr and suba of the Capital Akbarabad’, allegedly made in
    the 3rd ry, and recognising its transfer by gift (fiba) by Rup Gosain
    to his ‘brother’ Jiv Ji ‘with sons’. Dated 5 Zil'hijj 14 ry corresponding
    to 955 ah. Jiv Gosa’in was, of course, not a brother but a nephew of
   Rup Gosa’in.
       The zimn gives endorsement of Sadru’s Sudur Shuja'at Khan, with
   other seals and endorsements, all bearing the year 14.
       The forgery is proclaimed by the place-names Mominabad and
   Akbarabad. The former name was given by Aurangzeb to Vrindavan,
90                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

     and the latter by Shahjahan to Agra. The word pukhta as defining a
     unit of measure too is alien to documents of Akbar’s reign. There
     were no subas in Akbar’s 14th ry, and, as already noted, Akbar had
     no Sadru’s Sudur of the name or title of Shuja‘at Khan. The year
     955 ah, and Akbar’s 14th ry do not correspond, 5 Zfl hijj 955 being
     5 January 1549, seven years before Akbar came to the throne. The
     tughra or title is fairly well written, but by mistake omits the name
     Akbar, thus missing out the vital name! The seal, with names of
     Akbar’s ancestors, seems similarly to have been copied from a genuine
     one. The use of Akbar’s regnal year with Hijri month would alone be
     enough to proclaim it a forgery, since Akbar’s regnal years were not
     used until the Ilahi calender was adopted later in his reign.
        There is a copy of this forgery, without the zimn, in VRI’s
     Radhadamodar temple collection, Doc. No. 81.
                             CHAPTER 4



     The Mughal Administration and
   the Temples of Vrindavan during the
     Reigns of Jahangir and Shahjahan
                Tarapada Mukherjee and Irfan Habib




In this chapter we continue the examination of the documents from
Vrindavan (for which, see Introduction) by taking up those belonging to
the reigns of Jahangir (1605-27) and Shahjahan (1628-58). We present
here a calendar of over forty-five Persian documents of the two reigns,
shedding light on the relations between the Mughal administration and
the temples of Vrindavan. As in the case of our study of Akbar and the
Temples of the Mathura Region [Chapter 3] a set of ‘private’ documents
such as sale-deeds have been here excluded. These will largely be dealt
with in chapters 7, 8, and 9 of this volume.


                                      I

 We may recollect that Akbar enlarged and consolidated grants to
 temples and their priests in the Mathura region by his farmdns of 4 and
 19 Shahriwar 43 Ilahi year (27 August and 11 September 1598), which
 accounted for a total grant of 1000 bighas of land to thirty-five temples in
Vrindavan and Mathura, and their environs. The temples to which fresh
 grants were issued in 1598 were put in seven groups, for each of which a
 separate farmdn was issued. Details of these were brought together in the
farmdn for the Govind-dev temple and three other temples issued on 19
 Shahriwar. Since our documents come from the two groups of Vrindavan
 temples, Madan Mohan etc. (assigned 170 bighas) and Govind-dev etc.
 92                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

  (assigned 135 bighas), we are unable to trace the history of the grants of
  the other groups; but the Vrindavan documents show fairly well what
  happened in the case of these two groups of temples.
     Under Jahangir the grants continued to be honoured; as may be seen
  from doc. No. 3 in the calendar annexed to this chapter (being Ftibar
  Khan's parwdna of 1608), confirming the grants to Govind-dev and three
  other named temples strictly in conformity with Akbars farmdn. A Sadrs
 parwdna of 1614 was issued to the same effect (Doc. No. 10). The grant
  to the Madan Mohan temple also continued unaffected, since it was
  confirmed exactly in terms of Akbar’s farmdn by a farmdn of Shahjahan
 of 4 Azur 6 ry (26 November 1633), itself not extant, but cited in Docs.
 Nos. 19 and 36, A nishan of Dara Shukoh issued in 1658 (Jhaveri XI)
 implies that Jahangir confirmed in favour of Girdhar the grant of the
 village of Gokul that Akbar had conferred on the latter’s father Bithal Rai
 (Vittalnath).
     Jahangir himself added to Akbar’s grants. By his farmdn of 14 Aban
 8 ry (7 November 1613) (Doc, No. 9) he converted Raja Todar Mais
 grant (as jagirddr) of 100 bighas (‘by bamboo rod’), conferred in 1584
 on Gopaldas, the sevak of the Madan Mohan temple, into an Imperial
 grant. The equivalent area by gaz-i ilahi being 89 bighas, 9 biswas (1 bigha
 = 20 biswas), the grant was now made permanent in favour of Srichand,
 Gopaldas’ successor as sevak of that temple. This meant that it was no
 longer resumable by any jagirdar.
     Further, Jahangir added at least two temples to the list of the thirty-
 five already supported by Akbar’s grant of 1598. Through Doc. No. 4, a
farmdn of February 1612, he granted 10 bighas to the sevak of the temple
 Thakur Dwara (Thakurduara) at Vrindavan, and by No. 7, a farmdn of
 April 1613, he granted 15 bighas to the sevak of Thakur Bhoj(?) Sundar,
 a temple in Basahi (Basai) Buzurg in pargana Sahar, near Mathura. In
 addition, he issued a number of grants to individuals of which chance
 records have survived in the Vrindavan archives:

               TABLE 4.1. Grants Issued to Individuals by Jahangir

 Grantee                                   Area of Grant       Document
 (a) (1) Kamdev Acharya                      bighas 12           No .5
     (2) Narayan Das                         bighas 12
 (b) Bindrabandas and Nand La‘l              bighas 50          No. 6

        The Mughal Administration and the Temples of Vrindavan             93

   Except for (b) and (c), all the above grants were assigned out of
pargana Sahar whose limits came close to Mathura and Vrindavan. Grant
(b) was made in a village of pargana Mathura. Grant (c) was assigned
in a pargana of sarkar Udambar (Tanda) in suba Bengal, which reminds
us of the affiliation between Bengal and the devotees of the temples of
Govind-dev and Madan Mohan. It may be noted that the formula 'with
sons’ indicated that the grant would not lapse on the death of the named
grantee, but would continue to be vested with his sons, up to a half of
the area.1 It was, therefore, a way of indicating special favour. Grant (e),
made to a woman Kunjdasi (30 bighas), along with Damodar (20 bighas), is
remarkable, looking at the sex of the first grantee. In any case, the number
ofJahangirs grants to individuals which we find almost incidentally among
our documents are in conformity with the reputation of great liberality in
this respect that he came to acquire.1
                                     23
                                      4
   None of the grants appear to be connected with Jahangirs own visit to
Vrindavan in 14th RY (1620), which he paid specifically to see its temples.
His impressions were not altogether favourable:

During the reign of His Majesty Emperor Akbar, Rajput nobles erected buildings
(of the temples) in their own style and decorated them profusely from outside.
But inside there were so many bats and owls that owing to their bad smells one
could not breathe?

   Among the temples built by ‘Rajput nobles’, Jahangir must have visited
the temple of Govind-dev, whose building was constructed by Raja Man
Singh of Amber, as recorded in an inscription? Man Singh committed
himself to a daily payment of Rs.5 and then, from 1608, of Rs.9, for the
temple. Of the latter sum Rs.8 were to be spent on the upkeep of the
temple, and Re.l on the maintenance of its sevak Haridas (Doc. No. 2).
Owing to their initial expenditure on the building of the Govind-dev
temple and regular financial support, the Amber family remained closely
interested in its management—a position of which they were to obtain
Imperial recognition during the reign of Shahjahan.


   1 Irfan Habib, The Agrarian System of Mughal India, 1st ed. Bombay, 1963,
pp. 305-06 & n. 35 on p. 306; 3rd. ed., New Delhi, 2014, pp. 349-528 nn.

94                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

    The temple of Govind-dev had another visitor, Mahmud Balkhi, in
 1625. He refers to it as having been built by Raja Man Singh [spelt:
‘Manak Sing’], ‘a noble of King Akbar’. He speaks of its large idol of black
stone and great gong, and of the crowds of men and women that came and
listened to the sermons of Brahmans and the sounds of music.5
    The temple of Madan Mohan (‘Madan Gopal’) was also supported by
financial aid from Rajput nobility. For example, there was the jagirddr
of Jewar, who assigned its sevaks an annual payment of Rs.60 in 1607,
reiterating the obligation in 1621 (Docs. Nos. 1 and 12). Unfotunately,
the jagirddr cannot be identified, but Jewar, out of whose revenues the
payment was to be made, was the seat of a pargana of sarkdr Delhi,
situated east of the Yamuna.6
    Incidentally, Jahangir describes in his Memoirs his meetings with the
Vedantic ascetic, Gusain Jadrup (Chitrarupa), with great enthusiasm.
First, he met him at Ujjain while on his way to Gujarat in 1616 and then
on the way back in 1618.7 Soon thereafter Jadrup shifted to Mathura,
where Jahangir sought him out more than once.8 There was no other
religious figure on whom Jahangir lavished so much praise. He must have
been greatly annoyed when Hakim Beg, a brother-in-law of queen Nur
Jahan, in charge of administration in Mathura (presumably as its faujdar),
had Jadrup whipped, apparently due to some grievance. When Jahangir
learnt of it, Hakim Beg was forthwith dismissed to pass the rest of his
days in retirement in Agra.9 It is not clear if Jadrup had any association
with the Gosa ins of the Chaitanya sect, or, indeed, with any temple.


                                      II

True to his reputation for saving state income after Jahangirs open-handed
years,10 Shahjahan seems to have made no additional grants to the temples
or their attendants and devotees. But he seems to have carefully honoured
all the grants previously made. At first sight, this is a little surprising in

Karachi, 1961, 1970 & 1974, vol. II, p. 288.
  10 Amin Qazwini, Padshahndma, Br. Lib. Or. 173, f. 221a-b.
         The Mughal Administration and the Temples of Vrindavan          95

view of a passage in Lahori’s Padshahndma, according to which Shahjahan
 ordered in his 6th ry (1633-4) that no temple whose foundations had
been laid in Jahangirs time but had not been completed, would be allowed
 to be completed; and in the Banaras area, accordingly, 76 such temples
were reportedly destroyed.11
    Yet during this very period, Shahjahan showed no intention to disturb
 any grants of the temples in Vrindavan, By a farmdn of 5 ry (November
 1632) he confirmed Jahangirs conversion of Todar Mai’s grant to the sevak
 of the Madan Mohan temple into an Imperial grant, and permitted it to
 pass in its entirety from the previous sevak, Srichand, now dead, to his
 successor, Gosaindas (Doc. No. 16). Two farmdns (not now extant but
 cited in later documents) were issued on 4 and 14 Azur 6 ry (26 November
 and 6 December 1633) to renew Akbar’s grant of 170 bighas to the temples
 of Madan Mohan, etc. The farmdn of 4 Azur confirmed grants to the
 temples of Madan Mohan (120 bighas) and Jasodhanandan (15 bighas), a
 total of 135 bighas in the villages Dosaich and Rajpur, pargana Mathura;
 the farmdn of 14 Azur confirmed 35 bighas granted to the temple Dwadas
 Adat in the village Sonrakh, nearby (farmdns cited in Docs, Nos. 19, 36,
 and 43).
     Shahjahan also took a measure to enhance the Amber family’s control
 over the management of the Govind-dev temple. This was done by his
farmdn of 1 Amurdad, 6 ry (24 July 1633). This farmdn cites Akbar’s
 grant of 1598 to the Govind-dev group of temples, but the zimn contains
 Shahjahan’s observation that ‘it is not the rule (to make grants) in the
 name of temples’. Since, as we have seen, the Madan Mohan temple
 grants were confirmed at this very time, the observation was seemingly
 made only to enable the Govind-dev grant to be transferred to the Amber
 house, which would then control the Govid-dev group of temples through
 its possession of a major source of their income. The entire grant of 135
 bighas was thus converted into an in‘dm (tax-free gift) of Raja Jai Singh of
 Amber (Doc. No. 17). Thereafter the Mughal administration recognised
 the control of the Govind-dev temple management as vested in Raja Jai
 Singh, who could appoint a person (Radha Kishandas Chakravartl) to
 supervise worship there (A'zam Khans parwana, 1643, Doc. No. 26)
 and demand the expulsion of certain persons from the temple (the same
 officer’sparwana, 1644, Doc, No. 27). Subsequently, in 1652, the followers
 of a Brahman, Har Govind, were expelled from the temple at the instance
 of Seo Ram, pujdri of the temple, armed with a recommendation from

  11 ‘Abdu’l Hamid, Lahori, Padshahndma, ed. Kabir al-Din Ahmad et al.,
Calcutta, 1866-72, vol. I, pt. I, pp. 451-2.
96                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

Rao Kalyan Singh, apparently a scion of the house of Amber (Khalilullah
Khan’s parwdna 1652, Doc. No. 38).
   No such external control seemed to have been exercised over the
Madan Mohan temple, which appeared to have enjoyed some special
Imperial patronage. We have referred to Lahori’s statement that in his 6
ry, Shahjahan issued instructions to destroy certain incomplete temples.
Such a spirit is not present in his Jarman of 14 Azur ry 7 (6 December
1634), only a year later. We give a translation of this document in the
Appendix to this chapter.
   What is important in this farmdn is not only that the gharial (time­
gong) was permitted again to be sounded in the Madan Mohan temple.
The farmdn goes on to say that this was essential for ‘the worship of
God’ (‘ihddat-i ilahi); and the image-worshippers themselves are flatly
described as being engaged in ‘the worship of God’ (Khudd-parasti).
There is no reference made to the Shari‘at or Muslim Law to justify the
tolerance of an impiety, for sounding the gong is not deemed an impiety
at all. Similar phrases also occur in Afzal Khan’s parwdna (Doc. No. 22),
ordering compliance with the farmdn, but then he was merely repeating
the sentiments of Shahjahan’s own farmdn.
   Some time later, Shahjahan issued anotherfarmdn, Doc. No. 30A, dated
9 December 1644. This farmdn directed that Kishandas (Krishandas),
‘devotee of Jiv Gosa in’, should have the adhikdr (management) of the
temples of Vrindavan. It specifically recalls Akbar’s farmdn of 6 October
1568, vesting the adhikdr of the temples Madan Mohan and Govind-
dev with Jiv Gosa’in, whom Kishandas was successor to. Indeed, a letter
(Doc. No. 32) assignable to 1645 describes Kishandas as the incumbent of
the seat (dstdna) of Shri Jiv Gosa’in. In Doc. 33 of about the same time,
Kishandas is seen moving the administration to protect the pond, Radha
Kund, laid out by Jiv Gosa’in (or rather, Raghunath) in the village Arith
(now Radhakund). The nature of his control, if any, over his contemporary
Gosaindas, sevak of Madan Mohan (Docs. Nos. 17, 19, 20, 37, 42, etc.)
and Seo Ram, pujari of Govind-dev (Doc. No. 38)12 is not very clear.
   From the cases cited here from the calendared documents, it becomes
obvious that the Mughal administration saw it as its inherent right to
exercise the power of ultimate control over the management of certain
temples. Its authority was, in any case, indispensable for the protection
of any installed management against pretentious usurpers (see especially
Docs. No. 26, 27, 33 and 34, 38),

  12 In sale deeds of 9 March 1653 and 21 May 1654 (not calendared here) Seo
Ram is designated ‘adhikari’ of the Govind-dev temple.
        The Mughal Administration and the Temples ofVrindavan                 97

   In Doc. No. 39 (of29 April 1654), the administration acted in Mathura as
a mediator in a dispute between a follower of ‘Kishan Chaitan’ (Chaitanya)
and a Radhavallabhi over encroachments on ‘a seat’ (nishastgah) of Shri
Chaitanya. The matter seems to have been settled to the satisfaction of
both parties.
   Prince Dara Shukoh appears at the close of our period, when Mathura
and its vicinity were held by him in jagir. Besides the parwdnas of a routine
nature (Doc. Nos. 41, 42, and 43), there is a parwana of his official Daud
Khan, issued on 14 January 1658 (Doc. No. 44), which contains the
Prince’s order, issued upon the complaint of the pujaris of Govind-dev
temple. Responding to their petition, the Prince remitted the cattle-tax
(gdushumdn) upon the temple cows grazing in pargana Mahaban (across
the Yamuna) and prohibited local officials from subjecting gardeners of
the temple s gardens and orchards in Mahaban to hegdr or forced labour, A
similar exemption from grazing-tax (charat) was granted to the temple of
Govardan Nath in the village Gopalpur (in pargana Ao by a nishan of Dara
Shukoh), (8 Jumada II 1068 = 13 March 1658).13 A nishan of the same
Prince (27 Rajab 1068 = 30 April 1658) confirmed the grant of the village
Gokul (the famous holy place in pargana Mahaban) in favour of Girdhar
 (now deceased), son of Bithal Rai (Vitthalnath), sevak of the temple of
Gokul Nath, stated to have been originally conferred by farmans of Akbar
and Jahangir.14


                                       Ill

The documents we have calendared not only shed light on the relationship
between the Mughal administration and the Vrindavan temples and their
priests and devotees, but also on the functioning of that administration.
As Docs, Nos. 36 (of 1651) and 40 (of 1655) make clear, each grant
established by the Emperor’s farman had to be supported by parwdnas of
succeeding jdgjrddrs which were, in a sense, certificates for the genuineness
of the original farman. The jagirddr needed to see the farman and issue
the parwana because he had to relinquish the land-revenue due from the
land. His officials thus needed to ensure that the area of the grant was duly

   13 K,M. Jhaveri, Imperial Farmans (ad 1577 to ad 1805) granted to the ancestors
°f....... the Tikayat Maharaj, Bombay, 1928, Doc, No. XII, The cows belonging
to this temple were allowed to graze in two neighbouring villages (duly named)
without any imposition of kdh-chardi or any other constraint.
    14 Ibid., Doc. no. XI.

delimited by a chakndma, or demarcation-deed, recording the boundaries
 of the area of the grant on the ground. The Sadr's tashihas (certificates)
were similarly needed to reinforce confidence in the genuineness of the
grant.
    A very large number of our calendared documents are thus parwanas
ofjagirdars or their officials. Arranged chronologically, as in our calendar,
they enable us to reconstruct a list of successive jagirdars who held the
area of Mathura and its environs during Shahjahan’s reign (see Table 4.2).
    Doc. No. 36 adds the name of Mukramat Khan just before that of
Ja‘far Khan, by a reference made to his son ‘Abdul Latif issuing a parwdna
on his behalf
    Thus we have, at least, eleven jagirdars holding the area successively in a
period of 30 years of Shahjahan’s reign, giving an average term of just 2.7
years (say, 2 years, 8 months). This is an important piece of evidence which
confirms the general statement in our authorities that jdgir assignments
used to be of very short durations.15 Our documents also shed some light
on how the jagirdars' internal administration functioned. Doc. No. 39
(of 29 April 1654) introduces to us Shaikh ‘Abdun Nabi, whose name :
is prefaced with titles suggesting that he was faujdar or commandant ‘
(Shaukat o hashmat dastgah). He was called upon to settle a dispute J

 the Madan Mohan temple in village Rajpur. He was accompanied by his
 clerk (farkun), Shankardas.

Note

Shaikh Farid Bhakkarl, writing in 1651, refers to two officers posted asfaujddrs
at Mathura (though in one case he makes the slip of using the term silbaddr),
viz. Murshid Quit Khan Turkman and Murawwat Khan Turkman, crediting both
of them with possession of large harems and attributing to the former a
discreditable escapade involving the abduction of a Hindu woman at a fair
(Zakhiratu’l Khawanin, ed. S. Moinul Haq, Vol. II, Karachi, 1970, pp. 372, 398).
It turns out, vide M. Athar Ali, The Apparatus ofEmpire', Awards ofRanks, Offices
and Titles to the Mughal Nobility, Delhi, 1985, entries nos. S 280 et seq., that the
two persons were really one, Murawwat Khan Turkman having received in due
course the title of Murshid Quit Khan. He was appointed faujdar of Mathura and
Mahaban in 1635-36 (ibid., S. 1141), which office he held until his death in
1637-38 (ibid., S. 1322, S. 1450 and S. 1936). At his death he held the fairly high
mansab (rank) of 2,500 zat/2,50Q sawdr.
