                               Appendix

Translation of Shah Jahans Farmdn (No .21 in the Calendar)
Invocation:
‘God is Great’
Tughra                                              Square Imperial Seal
                                                    of Shahjahan
It has been represented to His Sacred Majesty that in the temple (devala)
of Madan Mohan, situated in village Bindraban, in the jurisdiction of
pargana Mathura, stiba Akbarabad (Agra), a large body of God-worshipping
Hindu mendicants are engaged in the worship of God, according to their
own religion and custom. For practising the rites of Gods worship in the
said temple, a time-gong (gharial) was in use for the purpose of intimating
the passage of hours and the time of night and day. For some time, as a
result of the interference and prohibition by officials, this (the sounding
of the gong) has been in suspension. As a result, the proper timings of
the acts of devotion and worship of God cannot be maintained owing to
the passage of hours of night and day not being sounded. (The devotees)
are hopeful that the (sounding of the) time-gong will be permitted within
the said temple in accordance with past practice. Therefore the world­
subduing, sun-illuminating, sky-reaching Order has been issued to the
effect that no one should obstruct the sounding of the time-gong in the
said temple. Officials, present and future, should strive for the continuance
and fulfilment of this order, ensure that the time-gong is in use in the
said temple, and not obstruct it for any reason. They are not to practise
delay in carrying out what has been ordered. Written, dated 14 Azur
month Ilahi Regnal Year 7 (29 November 1634),
                           CHAPTER 5



             Dealing with Multiplicity
     Mughal Administration in Braj Bhum
        Under Aurangzeb, 1659-1707

                              Irfan Habib




The state everywhere must deal with a certain degree of variation in
terms of class, religion, and custom among the population over whom its
authority is exercised. In Mughal India, owing to the caste system and the
two major religions, Hinduism and Islam, the element of multiplicity was
exceptionally complex. There was the further possible complication that
the local administration’s conduct was not necessarily always in consonance
with the declared policies of the central government, however despotic the
sovereign in theory might have been.
    A study is offered here of the ways in which the Mughal administration
in Braj Bhum, with Mathura as its centre,1 dealt with Hindus, and
occasionally Muslims, in matters of worldly concern, as well as religious
life, during the reign of Aurangzeb (1659-1707). One is able to present
such a study with some confidence because of the large amount of material
in the shape of contemporary documents in Persian, and, in smaller
part, in Braj, that has been preserved by the Gosa’ins (Goswamis) of
Vrindavan, and then brought together in the form of photographs and
xerox copies by the late Dr Tarapada Mukherjee (see Introduction to this
 volume). Unfortunately, most of the photocopies were unnumbered and
 many did not even indicate to which collection or source they should
 be credited. When I had transcribed them, I arranged the transcribed
 material chronologically (by years) and then numbered the sheets. I have
 found it easier to cite each document in this chapter by the number of the
 sheet of the transcript which contains it or on which it begins, in case

   1 For the boundaries of Braj Bhum in Mughal times, see Map 1.
110                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

the transcription extends to more than one sheet. The photographs and
xerox copies that I possess also have had these numbers put on them at
the back. In addition, there are documents from other sources, notably
the National Archives of India, which duly bear their own numbers. In
such cases I give both the official number the document bears and the
transcript sheet number. All these documents have been made available
to public access through being scanned at the library of the Department
of History, Aligarh University. All Hijri dates have been converted into
equivalent dates of the Christian era (Gregorian system) in this chapter,
and only the latter are specified here.
   1 have chosen three out of the several aspects of the activities of the
Mughal administration that can be studied on the basis of the material we
possess: first, the treatment of temples and of their priests especially with
regard to their grants, properties and income; second, the operation of the
machinery of justice and law-and-order: and, third, the application of the
law of escheat to the Gosains’ property, illustrated by one long drawn-out
case. It will be seen that in order to introduce some relevant evidence I
have sometimes gone beyond the limits of Aurangzeb’s reign.


State Grants to Temples and Priests

The temples and priests of Vrindavan and Mathura depended on the
support they received from their princely and rich, as well as poor, devotees.
The temples at Vrindavan, especially Govind-dev, had, in addition, the
consistent patronage of the Amber house, whose rulers throughout the
great days of the Mughal Empire remained in high favour at the Imperial
Court. But the aid of the Imperial Court itself, in financial terms, was not
inconsiderable. Imperial grants of land to temple-priests began as early
as 1565 (200 bighas granted to Gopaldas of Madan Mohan temple, No.
17), and all grants to temples of the vicinity were revised and raised to
1000 bighas ilahi, by Akbar’s farmdn of 1598 (NAI-267 (l)=No. 57; copies,
Nos. 66 and 78), the grant area being equal to about 240 hectares or 600
acres.23To this total Jahangir is known to have added at least 253.45 bighas
ilahi amounting to nearly 60 hectares or over 152 acres? Shahjahan, while

   2 The grants under Akbar have been discussed by Tarapada Mukherjee and
Irfan Habib, ‘Akbar and the Temples of Mathura and its Environs’, PIHC, 48th
session (1988, Goa), pp. 214-50 (Chapter 3 in this volume).
    3 Data derived from Tarapada Mukherjee and Irfan Habib, ‘The Mughal
Administration and the Temples ofVrindaban during the Reigns ofJahangir and
                           Dealing with Multiplicity                          111

benevolent in confirming older grants, does not seem to have issued any
new grant of his own to any temple or priest ofVrindavan.
    When the reign of Aurangzeb (1659-1707) began, there was apparently
little indication that a change in the official attitude would occur. In
May 1659, the faujdar of Mathura, Jafar Saljuqi, administering pargana
Mahaban, exempted from cattle-tax (gdu-shumari) all the cattle belonging
to the Govind-dev, Gopinath, and Madan Mohan temples (ofVrindavan)
that were brought to graze in that pargana in the vicinity of Mansarowar,
situated on the eastern side of the Yamuna, opposite Vrindavan (No. 216).
In September 1659, the same official gave special permission for cattle of
the Madan Mohan temple to graze freely in four named villages, including
Begampur and Mansarowar (No. 217).
    The first indication of change seems to have come in 1666 when
Aurangzeb ordered the destruction of the stone-railings (katehra) of the
Keshav Rai temple of Mathura.4 But what caused the most alarm was
when Aurangzeb, all of a sudden, early in 1670, without any provocation
or apparent cause, ordered the demolition of the Keshav Rai temple itself.
This temple had been built by Jahangir’s trusted noble, Bir Singh Bundela,
the ruler of Orchha and the murderer of Abu’l Fazl, at a reputed cost of
Rs.33 lakh (a more moderate contemporary estimate: Rs.10 lakh). A large
number of labourers were put to the task of dismantling the temple so
as to achieve its destruction as quickly as possible. Its idols were taken
to Agra to be laid on the ground for being stepped on by worshippers at
the mosque of Princess Jahanara. Further, Mathura was given the official
name of Islamabad.5

Shahjahan’, PHIC, 49th session (Dharwad, 1989), pp. 287-300 (Chapter 4 in
this volume). The grant of 20 bighas to Swamidas is excluded from our figure.
    4 An eye-witness account of this was provided in the report of the vakil of
Mirza Raja Jai Singh in the Akbbdrdt-i-Darbar-i-Mu‘alld, printed in G. H.
Khare, Persian Sources of Indian History, VI, Poona, 1973, p. 20. The faujdar
‘Abu’n Nabi Khan had the old wooden railing reinstated by the temple attendants,
in place of the stone one. On this officer, see footnote 20.
      For these details, see Saqi Musta‘idd Khan, Ma’dsir-i ‘Alamgiri, Bib. Ind.,
Calcutta, 1870-3, pp. 95-6. His estimate of Rs.33 lakh spent on its construction
is surely excessive: Around 1650 Shaikh Farid Bhakkari gave an estimate of Rs. 10
lakh, while saying that Bir Singh Bundela had built the temple in such a manner
that ‘it would last till the Day of Judgement’ (S. Moinul Haq, ed., Zakhlratu-l
Khawdnin, Karachi, 1971, vol. II, p. 364)—a prophesy tragically falsified so soon.
See also F.S. Growse, Mathura: A District Memoir, 3rd ed. 1882, reprint, New
Delhi, 1979, pp. 126-29, where descriptions of the temple by two contemporary
French observers, Bernier and Tavernier, are also quoted.
112                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

   That this incident caused great consternation among priests of other
temples is shown by the action of Seo Ram (Shiv Ram), the custodian
of the Govind-dev temple at Vrindavan. As his vakil (representative)
acknowledged before the Qazi of Mathura in 1675, ‘when the dehra
(temple) of Keshav Rai was destroyed, he (Seo Ram) removed himself to
Kaman Pahari’ (No. 233). He did not, however, go alone—he took the
idols at the Govind-dev temple with him, and later received instructions
from Raja Ram Singh of Amber to remove the idols further to Bhatesar
(No. 233, dated 19 October 1671), a stage in their journey to Amber as
their ultimate destination. Thus even a high noble of Aurangzeb now
felt nervous about the idols’ safety.6 That the alarm was not altogether
without cause is shown by the fact that as late as June 1681, Hasan
‘All Khan, Governor of suba Agra, reported to the Court that he had
destroyed a temple at Mathura and laid out a village adjacent to it, naming
it Hasanpur, after himself.7
   It seems, however, that no major temple in Vrindavan was, in fact,
affected.8 Seo Ram, who had gone to Kaman with the idols, had come back


   6 For the removal of the Govind-dev idol from Vrindavan, see also R. Nath,
‘Sri Govindadev’s Itinerary from Vrindavana to Jayapura, c. 1534-1727,’ in
Margaret H. Case, ed., Govindadeva: A Dialogue in Stone, New Delhi, 1996, pp.
161-184, esp. pp. 166-69. (Place names and personal names are often converted
into imaginary Sanskrit forms in this volume).
   It was not only the idols of the Govind-dev temple that were removed from
Vrindavan. In a statement of 1739, and in a further undated one, Gosa’in Ram
Jiwan, priest of the Gopinath temple, referred to the removal (‘departure’) to
Kaman of the thakur of the Gopinath temple as well, over sixty years earlier,
whereafter for some time much neglect occurred in the repair and protection of
the temples property in Vrindavan (Nos. 401 and 405).
   7 Akhbdrdt, extracted in G. H. Khare, op. cit., p. 118.
   3 The sole reference to temple demolition in Vrindavan that I have been able
to trace is in a sale-deed of ad 1691, where, while giving particulars of a plot of
land sold near Chirghat, it is said to be bordered on the west by the wall of
Gobindi Bagh, a garden attached to a ‘demolished temple’ (butkb.dna-i munhadima)
(No. 245). There is no other hint of any harm caused to any temple in the large
number of documents from Vrindavan that have come down to us from the
period, nor in the enthusiastic account of the temple-town left by the historian
Bhimsen, who visited it in Aurangzeb s 41st regnal year (1697-8) (Nuskha-i
Dilkushd, Br. Lib. MS, Or. 23, £ 127a).
   This, therefore, raises the question as to when the sanctuaries (garbhagrtha)
of the Govind-dev and Radha Vallabh temples were destroyed, apparently
constituting the sole physical evidence of (partial) temple destruction at
                         Dealing with Multiplicity                         113

to Mathura-Vrindavan by 1675 (No. 234), and, as our documents suggest,
continued to stay there till his death in or before 1692? A more telling
indication of normality was the continued maintenance of the land-grants
previously issued to the temples or their custodians. Thus the grant of
135 bighas for the maintenance of the Govind-dev temple, assigned to the
Amber house, was repeatedly confirmed. Such confirmation was provided
in 1661, and then in 1671, 1695, and 1707 (Nos. 220, 231, 258, and 293).
Similarly, the grant of 89 bighas, 9 biswas, in favour of the custodians
of Madan Mohan temple was confirmed in 1691, 1697, and 1707/1711
(Nos. 244, 266, and 290). The fact that a grant of 76 bighas, 2 biswas, in
Ilahi measure (originally, 100 bighas), conferred on Madho, priest of the
Gopinath temple, by Akbar in 1568-69 continued with his successors until
 1722, when it was again confirmed (No. 22), shows that this grant too
had been maintained under Aurangzeb. That the local administration had
little interest in dispossessing the Gosains of their grants is further shown
by the fact that when in April 1692 Gosaindas, custodian of the Madan
 Mohan temple, claimed that the chakndma (demarcation document) of
 the imperial grant of 89 bighas, 9 biwas, in village Rajpur, that he held,
 had been lost in the Jat uprising (fiitur-i Jat), Muhammad Hashim, the
 revenue-assessor (amln) of the pargana, raised no objection to a fresh
 demarcation and, after a fresh survey, issued a new chakndma which has
 survived (No. 247).
    Not only did the local Mughal administration thus refrain from casting
 covetous eyes on the land-grants of the Gosa ins, despite Aurangzebs
 general order of 1672-73 requiring resumption of all grants held by


Vrindavan. Shrivatsa Goswami (in Case, ed., Dialogue in Stone, pp. 271-72)
suggests this might have happened in the case of the Govind-dev temple at
Aurangzebs direction, c.1670. This is doubted by George Michell (in ibid., pp.
120-2), on the ground of the garbhagrihas alone being chosen for destruction and
for the further reason that worship in the Govind-dev temple continued at least
until the 1730s, which would not have been possible had the sanctuary been
demolished. Indeed, given the profusion of Persian and Braj documentation from
the reigns of Aurangzeb and his successors relating to Vrindavan, it would be
strange if such an act were not hinted at anywhere. It would also not have been
possible for the imperial land grants to the Govind-dev temple to be maintained
had the temple been officially marked for destruction. How and when the
sanctuaries of the two temples were demolished thus remains a puzzle.

pp. 156-57 (Chapter 10 in this volume).

Hindus,1011 or, as we have noted in Chapter 1, imposing on them thejizya

At this time, it has been brought to notice that Rup and Haridas, the preceptors
of the preceptors (pirdn-pir) of Swami Brajanand,12 having abandoned the world,
settled in village Bindraban [Vrindavan] when it was all jungle, without human
 habitation. At that time they made their home here, establishing the institution
of headship and custom for their own sect of mendicants, so that they, after
retiring to a corner, should not seek (worldly) pleasure, but busy themselves in
worship of Divinity and in wandering within Braj Bhum. They do not (thus)
reside outside Braj Bhum, and they entertain mendicants, both Hindu and
Muslim, who arrive hungry. The zamindars of the parganas of Braj Bhum have,
in addition to the assessed revenue (jama"), been paying one rupee on account of
the kharj-i sadir-warid (‘expenditure on those arriving and departing’) for the
mendicants (bairags), each village paying half [a rupee] in the kharif season, and
half in the rabi season. Now, it has been agreed with the zamindars that,
recognising the said Brajanand as successor to the sons of the said Rup and
Haridas, the said amount be converted into an amount of charity, as alms on
behalf of His August Majesty, for the benefit of the mendicants and the indigent,
without any loss to the government being involved, as per reverse (zimn).
Henceforth with effect from the beginning of tahsll (revenue-collection), season
to season and year to year, they should pay the amount to [Brajanand] aforesaid,
so that spending it on their subsistence, they [the mendicants] may engage
themselves in worship of the Creator and praying for the long life of His Majesty.

   The reverse (zimn) reads as follows:

The amount payable to Swami Brajanand, from amongst sons [successors] of Rup
[and] Haridas: One rupee per year, per village in parganas, including Islamabad,
i.e. Mathura, etc., within siiba Akbarabad [Agra], not affecting the jama‘, being
entirely [itemised as] kharj-i sadir-warid with agreement of jagirdars and
zamindars.

   The conversion of a prohibited cess such as kharj-i sadir-a-warid,
usually exacted by visiting officials,13 into an annual levy of one rupee from
each village, over and above the states land-revenue, was an ingenious
way of augmenting the income of Swami Brajanand and his followers,
for though the rate was low, the area from which it was collected was
large, comprising as many as eighteen parganas. Seeing as the suba of Agra
c.1690 contained 233 parganas and 30,180 villages in Aurangzebs reign,
yielding an average of 129.5 villages per pargana, it is fair to assume that
the 18 parganas of Braj Bhum contained about 2,332 villages. In that case,
if most villages met their obligations, the total annual receipts should
have exceeded Rs.2000, a tidy sum for those days.14 Brajanand himself was
the representative (vakil) of Gobind Charan, custodian of the Govind-
dev temple, and so belonged to the line of Rup Gosain. (See entry on
Brajanand in Chapter 10).
   What could be gained under Aurangzeb naturally continued under
his successors, who did not necessarily share his prejudiced outlook.
Jagannath received his entitlement to the custodianship of the Govind-

reign are tabulated in Habib, Agrarian System, 3rd ed., p.4, where sources are also
indicated.

transcript No. 85, p. 329, in the Library of the Department of History, Aligarh).

Justice and Law Enforcement

In local Mughal administration, the sphere of justice seems to have been
broadly divided between the adjudication of civil or property matters,
which lay within the jurisdiction of the qdzi, each pargana usually having
one, on the one hand, and the handling of criminal cases or violent
disputes, which were generally dealt with by the faujdar (commandant) or
his agent, or, in a large town, the kotwdl, on the other.17
   The qdzi, who held his appointment from the imperial court,
represented a class, one which had its sharp critics among contemporaries,
ranging from high officials to foreign observers.18 It would be hardly
reasonable to expect that the documents which the qdzts themselves have
left behind would display the malpractices attributed to them; rather, we
may assume that their actions, as recorded formally on paper, would be
what was officially expected of them in respect of propriety and rectitude.
   It is obvious from our documents that for persons of property or those
entering into property transactions, the qazi’s role was crucial, for he
was the certifying officer for all sale-deeds, transfer and gift documents,
and contractual agreements, by which rights and entitlements could be
protected in the future. He was expected to certify, with his endorsement
and seal, copies of all original documents. When the original document
was drawn up, the qdzi was expected to put his endorsement and seal
after examining the parties, guarantors (if any), and witnesses, their names
being recorded with their marks, signatures, or seals. And, after about
1650, the physical descriptions (huliya) of the persons concerned also
began to be furnished in the margin of each document.
   From our present point of view there are other matters of interest too
in the documents which the qdzis certified by their seals. We should bear
in mind that there were some Muslim Gaurua peasants who often acted in


   17 There were, however, occasions, as in the case of zamindari disputes and
other land-right matters, when the jurisdictions would overlap. See Habib,

and Francois Bernier, Travels in the Mogul Empire, 1656-68, tr. A. Constable,
revd. V. A. Smith, London, 1916, p. 257.

unison with their Hindu fellow-villagers in selling land—as, for example,
Nizam, Hamyan (Humayun), and Naso (Nasir) appearing as sellers along
with Gahan, Hardas, Sital, and others in a sale-deed of 1594, apparently
unregistered with a qdzi (No. 48-50; Braj version, No.51), and Junaid
and Najm in a group headed by Sital in a ^zz-certified sale-deed of 1601
(No. 89).19 One presumes that these Muslim peasants were converts from
Hinduism, as was ‘All Khan, ‘son of Angad, son of Raghu,’ who at the
age of 50 years, appeared as a guarantor of a land-sale at Vrindavan in a
gazz-certified deed of 1654 (No. 202). (He also appears in Nos. 180, 187,
and 218.) The reverse process in the change of religion could also have
occurred. ‘All Khan’s namesake at the village Arith (Radhakund) had a
son named Gulal, whose son, in turn, bore the name of Har Rai (NAI-
26911: 11, = No. 325). Such abandonment of Islam was a capital offence
under Islamic law, and yet here in 1717 the Qazi of the pargana Sahar,
Muhammad Hafiz, certified the original deed in which Har Rai appeared
as a seller, with his grandfather’s Muslim name clearly stated, without the
Qazi taking any notice of this apparent case of apostacy.
   An interesting feature of these ‘registered’ deeds is that Hindu women
are recognised as independent parties and proprietors without any demur.
Usually, their rights seem to have accrued to them as widows often through
a claim on their husbands’ property, as shown by two ^zz-registered
deeds of 1732, relating to property at Mathura and Vrindavan: in the
first of these (No. 391A) three widows, without any male co-sharers, and
in the second (No. 392A), two widows with male co-sharers, appear as
sellers. In 1669 the payment of a sum due to a woman Isarwi, on account
of a claim to house-materials belonging to her deceased father, is duly
certified by the qazi ‘after obtaining Ms Isarwi’s consent’ (No. 228). In
1693 the qdzi similarly certified the sale of a plot in Vrindavan by a widow
along with her two daughters (No. 252).
   When a woman did not authorise any male representative (wakil), she
had to make a personal appearance before the qdzi\ and, in such cases,
women had to submit to their physical appearance (huliya) being recorded
in the registered deed. To take the case of Ms Isarwi, just mentioned, her
description in the document of 1669 (No. 228) is as follows:

Physical Appearance: Ms Isarwi, deponent. Wheatish complexion. Broad

   Incidentally, she had also to sign, which she did by marking a rough
cross, showing thereby that she was not literate. Similar descriptions of
women deponents occur in Doc. No. 253, of 1693, where Ms Ramkunwar,
instead of being credited with a mole, is said to have a hole in the left
nostril; and in No. 391A of 1732, where both women deponents are stated
to have their left nostrils and both ears pierced, while Ms Dayali had
a mole below her right eye as well. Clearly, the purdah system did not
operate here. One is left to wonder what happened when Muslim women
appeared before the qdzi.
   While, as might be clear from the above, qdzi-cerufied documents
that have survived are fairly numerous, this cannot be said of the texts
of judgements the qdzis delivered upon disputes brought before them.
From this point of view much interest attaches to the original text of the
decision of the then qdzi of Mathura, Muhammad ‘Arif, that he recorded
on 27 February 1675. The document is preserved in the National Archives
(NAI-2671/13=our No. 234), and shows how the qdzi proceeded in a
matter in which both parties, viz., Seo Ram, the custodian of the Govind-
dev temple, and Arjun, his alleged tenant, were Hindus.
   The qdzi states that Hir Ram, representing Seo Ram Gosain, appeared
in his court, bringing with him one Arjun. He alleged, on behalf of his
principal, that seven years earlier Seo Ram had given out to Arjun his
house (baveli) in Mathura, the situation of it being carefully described, on
a monthly rent of half a rupee, this being entered in a written agreement,
endorsed by witnesses, whom he had also brought with him. When the
temple (dehra) of Keshav Rai was destroyed (in 1670), Seo Ram had left
Mathura for Kaman. Now, when he returned, he found Arjun refusing to
leave the house or pay the rent, on the pretended ground that the house
was his own. Hir Ram prayed that the Qazi should order his principal’s
rights to be restored. On being asked to reply, Arjun denied his ever
having agreed to pay rent and claimed that his written admission to do so
was not genuine. Thereupon the complainant was asked to present proof,
and he presented two witnesses, who had signed the original document,
and they affirmed on their ‘customary oath’ (qasm-i mutaarif) that Arjun
had in their presence written out the undertaking for rent in ‘Hindawi
characters’.
   The qazl, in face of this disagreement, decided to go to the spot,
accompanied by Anbiya, the Superintendent of the Court (J)arogha-i
120                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 ‘Addlat), chaudhuris (headmen), and other Hindus to take evidence
 directly from the people of the neighbourhood. There seven persons
were encountered, all named, including two Brahmans and one woman
 (‘GangadasI, daughter of Kishandas, son of Kaun Rai’)- They all stated on
 oath, in front of the two parties, that the property belonged to Gosa’in
 Seo Ram and that Arjun’s own elders (huzurgdn) had resided there as
tenants, duly paying rent.
    The facts being thus established, the Qdzi ordered that the accused
 must surrender the house to the complainant and not vex him thereafter.
Apparently, no orders were given about the arrears of rent due for past
years.
    Although the mufii (jurist) is not represented as being present in these
proceedings, his seal (‘All, mufti-i Shar*) yet appears on the right margin
of the judgement along with those of the qazi himself and the ddrogha-i
 ‘adalat.
    While one may suspect, given the general repute of qazis, that bribes
privily offered might have oiled the judicial machinery quite frequently,
it must be admitted that on paper the judgement is represented as having
been reached after extra care had been exercised to get at the truth. The
qazi even entertained the oral evidence of the woman GangadasI, although
in Muslim law the testimony of a single woman is valueless.
    As we have noted, while the qazi decided issues of civil rights, matters
like crimes and acts of violence were dealt with by executive officials such
as the faujdar and kotwdl, though the boundaries of their jurisdictions
could overlap. Here our records offer us accounts of two incidents where
the Mughal administration was called upon to adjudicate disputes over
alleged forcible usurpation.
    The first incident occurred late in Shahjahans reign and is related in
a bilingual document (No. 200) of 29 April 1654, which in its Persian
portion recites that Gauranga Bangall brought a complaint before Shaikh
‘Abdu’n Nabl, the faujdar of Mathura,20 to the effect that Damodar Das

of the Radha Ballabhi sect had taken possession of two trees under which

   This was thus a settlement in which the faujdar involved local notables;
and the state machinery worked as a mediator. But in the second case,
where the state machinery did get involved, much confusion and official
embarrassment resulted from an apparent conflict between the executive
and judicial arms.
   The incident occurred during a particular phase in the long period of
hostility that existed between the lines of the successors of Rup and Jiv
Gosa’ins, the former having pushed the latter out of the custodianship of
the Govind-dev temple through a decision of Mirza Raja Jai Singh, early
in Aurangzeb’s reign (No. 215 of ad 1659). The heads of the line of Jiv
Gosa’in, however, continued to occupy retreats (kunj) and a garden near
the Govind-dev temple. When Braj Kunwar, head of this line, died in or
about 1698, there was no male of adult age apparently left in the family to
succeed him, but his widow Kishan Priya continued to occupy the estate
with the support of her husbands followers. This was contested by Gobind
Charan, the current custodian of the Govind-dev temple, who now laid
claim to the property, since a woman could not be a religious head.21
The qdzi of Mathura, along with the news reporters (Wdqi‘a Nigdr and
Sawanib Nigdr) wrote to the Governor of tuba Agra in favour of Kishan
Priya, alleging that Nand Kishor Sayamanandi, who at the time was acting
on behalf of Gobind Charan, was trying to seize Kishan Priyas property.

 Mukhtar Khan, the Governor, thereupon issued a parwdna, dated 19 May

    The story is now taken up by a statement of a number of officials (No.

four foot soldiers, Dudhram, etc., to proceed from Mathura to Vrindavan

vacated the garden of Gawandas (?), which was handed over to Nand

thdnedar of Vrindavan, and Ram Prasad, agent of the Waqai Nigar, and
other official staff (ahali mawali) also put in their appearance. But Kishan

 Sukh Ram Brahman opened the gate from inside and came out. When
he was seized by the foot soldiers, he struck his head violently against
the wall. Now Gobind Charan’s representative Brajanand and others were
summoned from their homes; and Faqir Shah, thdnedar, and Ram Prasad
also arrived. Despite repeated demands, Kishan Priya did not open the
door. Finally, Sukh Ram, having contrived to get on to the roof (kothd),
jumped from it to the ground so as to hurt himself. Kishan Priya’s agent
(vakil) (not named) and Sukh Ram then went off to Mathura to lodge a
complaint (istaghasa) with the Qazi to the effect that Brajanand and Nand
Kishor had beaten up Sukh Ram and looted their money and goods (mal
o muta). Thereupon Jan ‘Ali Beg, darogha-i ‘adalat (superintendent of the
qdzis court) and Mir Madan asked the horseman and foot soldiers, as well
as the other staff, to explain their conduct. The statement giving the above
facts was drawn up in response to this demand, on behalf of the officials
and men involved in the incident. The fact of the matter, they submit, was
that it was Sukh Ram himself who had hurt himself by hitting his head
against the wall and jumping from the roof; and there had been no looting
by Brajanand and Nand Kishor as was alleged. The signatories included
Muhammad Faqir (Shah), thdnedar, who in his endorsement noted that

a representative of Ghulam Nabl (who, we know, was in charge of Baitu’l

members of staff. Arjun Singh, accompanying Shaikh Farid, horseman,
and Dudhram, foot soldier, put down marks instead of signatures. The
net result of the three-day-long tumult, with its physical excitement and
some paper work, was that Kishan Priya remained in possession of the
estate, despite the efforts of all the Kings horses, and all the Kings men.


Escheat

In the Mughal empire, the property of those who died intestate was held
to belong to the emperor, and so could be taken into custody by the state
treasury, the so-called Baitu’l mdl. Badauni cites an order of Akbar of the
year 1594 to the effect that until the treasury officer (baitu’l mdl-chi) issued
a certificate of clearance in regard to claims on a dead persons property,
the dead could not be buried.22 In his twelve accession ordinances of
1605, Jahangir ordered that for the custody of such property where heirs
could not be found, whether the dead be non-Muslim or Muslim, special
officers were to be appointed, who were to use the income from property
so secured on ‘the construction of mosques and inns, repairs of bridges,
and construction of tanks and wells’.23
   The Gosa’ins of Vrindavan do not seem to have been affected by
the enforcement of this regulation until the reign of Aurangzeb. That
emperor had, in respect of imperial officers, issued an order in 1667 to the
effect that, failing any heirs, their property was to be taken over by officers
(tahvlldar) of the Baitu’l Mai.24 This order, however, merely recapitulated
the existing rules with some clarifications. In fact, all subjects, and not
simply officers, were covered by this regulation, as the Gosa’ins of the line
of Rup Gosa in were to find out in 1692 and thereafter.
   In 1689, Seo Ram of that line (whom we have already met) made a
gift (hiba) of his property to his ‘nephew’ Kishan Charan, and got the
deed certified by the qazis of parganas Kaman and Mathura. The property
included a house and two adjacent shops in Mathura (No. 242), Upon Seo
Ram’s death, and during the absence of Kishan Charan from Mathura,

  22 BadaunI, Muntakhabu’t Tawarikb. op. cit., vol. II, pp. 390-91.
  23 Jahangir, Tuzuk-i Jahangiri, ed. Saiyid Ahmad, Aligarh, 1863-64, p. 4.
  24 ‘All Muhammad Khan, Mird’t-i Ahmadi, ed. Nawab Ali, Baroda, 1927-8
& 1930,1, pp. 266-67.
124                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

‘owing to the uprising of the Jats (jutur-i Jdtdnf, Ghulam Nabi, the
darogha (superintendent) of Baituj Mai, Mathura, seized the property
and appropriated the rent paid by the tenants. Kishan Charan succeeded
through an agent (vakil) in persuading an official, Mir Husaini, of the
Bayutdt (Imperial Property and Household Department), Agra, to issue an
order (11 November 1692) to the officials of the Baitu’l Mai, Mathura, not
to seize the property (Doc No. 251). In February 1694, Muhammad Baqir,
Mir Husaini s successor, issued a similar order, invoking his predecessor’s
instructions (No. 254). These facts were recorded in a document, dated
May 1694, prepared on behalf of Kishan Charan, on which he was able to
get supporting endorsements or seals from as many as eight local Muslim
officials, including a qdzi (NAI-2671/14=our No. 255).
    Apparently these efforts bore fruit since, when in 1696 Kishan Charan
himself gifted all his property, including the house and shops at Mathura,
to his younger brother, Gobind Charan, the hiba-nama bore the seal not
only of the deputy qdzi of Mathura but also of Ghulam Nabi, ddrogfea of
the Baituj Mai, Mathura (No. 277), though the general hibanama, Doc.
No. 262, which bears many Nagari signatures and three in Persian, does
not carry Ghulam Nabi’s endorsement. Yet upon Kishan Charan’s death,
the property in Mathura was again seized by the officials of the Baituj Mat,
Thereupon Malik Muhammad, Diwdn-i Bayutdt at Agra, issued an order
in December 1703 in which he took note of Gobind Charan’s complaint
and required the Baituj Mdl officials to release the property, in case the
facts as stated by the complainant were correct (No. 277). Nothing seems
to have happened until Izid Bakhsh ‘Rasa, Malik Muhammad’s successor
as Diwan-i Bayutdt at Agra, issued two peremptory orders (parwanas) on
the same date, 2 February 1706 (Nos. 286 and 286A).25 In both he pointed
out that this defiance of orders had continued despite parwanas from the
Governor of Agra, and his own two predecessors, Malik Muhammad and
Ziyau’ddin Khan. In one of these parwanas (No. 286A), he added that
all this was due to the ‘mischief’ of the mushrif (account-keeper) of the
Baituj Mdl, who had entered the house and two shops in the register

    25 Izid Bakhsh, a great-grandson of Jafar Beg Asaf Khan, died in 1707-08

Let them act according to the above and so behave that no harm comes to God’s
people. Follow strictly the orders in this matter.

   Even this reprimand did not immediately prove effectual, so on
28 February 1706, with all these documents in hand, Brajanand, as
agent (vakil) of Gobind Charan, appeared before the qdzi of Mathura,
Qutbu’ddin, seeking relief. The judgement was given on 16 March in
his favour, the Qazi directing that the seized assets be deleted from the
register and the shops and house returned to the petitioner as directed by
Mirza Izid Bakhsh (No. 286C),
   That, now at last, after nearly 15 years, the States claims on the rent
from the property were abandoned, is shown by the copy (No. 287) of an
extract from the Daily Register (Rozndmcha) of the treasury of Baitu’l
Mal, of Rabi 1, ry 50 of Aurangzeb (June-July 1706). This contains the
statement that the Governments claim to the rent of the house and shops
paid by ‘Tankraj, etc.’, and the rent collection (amounting to Rs.20 as.8
per month) was to cease by the end of Safar (12 June 1706) in terms of
Izid Bakhsh’s parwdna. The restoration to Gobind Charan of the rent
previously collected was apparently not contemplated.
    A later document which is relevant to the problem that this case hinges
upon is a parwdna of‘Inayatullah Khan, issued on 1 June 1721 (No. 356).
It refers to a petition submitted by Raja Sawai Jai Singh to the emperor
Muhammad Shah, drawing attention to the distress caused when, upon
 the deaths of Hindu and Muslim mendicants, their possessions, derived
from public charity, were seized by officials of the Baitu’l Mal who would
not allow their bodies to be removed for two or three days until they
themselves had completed their enquiry. The petition pleaded that this
practice should cease, and that in the case of the Govind-dev temple,
the possessions of the mendicants associated with it, who died heirless,
 should be handed over to that temple. The Emperor accepted the plea,
 and ‘Inayatullah Khan, therefore, ordered that the temple’s claims upon
 the possessions of the deceased mendicants who had belonged to it should
 henceforth be honoured.26


   26 ‘Inayatullah Khan was appointed Naib Diwan (Deputy Finance Minister)
on 9 February 1721, in addition to his duties as Khan-i Sdmdn (Hadi Kamwar
Khan, Tazkiratu’s Saldtin-i Chaghatd, op. cit., p. 334). Since the Khan-i Sdmdn
headed the Bayutat department to which the Baitu’l Mal was subordinate,
‘Inayatullah Khan could have issued this order in either of his two capacities.
PEASANTS
                              CHAPTER             6



             Land Rights in Braj Bhum
             during the Reign of Akbar
                 Tarapada Mukherjee and Irfan Habib




In Chapter 3 we presented the information we derived from Vrindavan
documents of Akbar’s reign regarding that emperors grants to the temples
of the Mathura region and related matters. We will now deal with another
set of documents of the same period that are primarily concerned with
the property acquisitions of the Chaitanya divines, as well as those by the
temples and their priests.1 These are of considerable historical importance,
since they enable us to judge, from the details of actual transactions, what
the nature of the individuals’ rights to land were, and where, if at all, the
village community stood in respect of land transfers. As an initial measure
we have taken twenty-seven documents, all belonging to the period before
the death of Akbar (1605), of which we offer a calendar at the end of the
chapter. These are mostly in Persian; but eight of them are in Braj. We are
greatly indebted to Professor J.C. Wright of the University of London for
letting us use his decipherments and translations of the Braj sale-deeds,
along with his notes on them. He is, of course, not responsible for any slip
we may make in interpreting these documents.12
    In the Calendar3 accompanying this chapter we have arranged the
documents in five sets, according to localities. The first set, consisting


    1 The original text of this chapter was published in PIHC, 50th session (1989,
Gorakhpur), pp.236-55.
   2 As Vice President of the International Association of VRI, Professor Wright
was involved in organising the project of exploring and photographing the
documents.
    3 Our Calendar does not profess to give literal translations except where words
are put within quotation marks. However, effort has been made to remain as loyal
to the originals as possible. Original spellings (e.g. Bindraban for Vrindavan) have
 130                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

of eleven documents (Nos. 1-11), relates to Arith, a village now known
as Radhakund, a name already found in our documents. This village is at
some distance from Vrindavan and Mathura, and was then in a different
pargana (Sahar), It may, therefore, be thought to represent the rural
conditions in a less alloyed form than Vrindavan, which was by Akbar’s
time becoming a pilgrim centre of some importance. All the other
four sets of documents relate to Vrindavan and its immediate environs.
Treating them in four different series will help us to better understand the
successive transactions that the documents describe.


                                        I

 Taking the Arith set first, the reader may be well advised to begin with
 Doc. No. 11, since it sheds important light on the nature of all the earlier
 documents. Here, in a deed dated 2nd June 1588, Goswami Raghunathdas
 declared that he possessed six plots of land in the village of Arith, holding
 them by different acts of purchase at the total price of Rs.238, and that
 he had now decided to gift them all to Jiv Goswami. For this purpose,
he obtained fresh deeds drawn up in favour of Jiv Goswami from the
 muqaddams (headman) who had originally sold the land to Raghunathdas
himself He then proceeds to list six plots specifying their limits, dates of
purchase, and prices, the earliest transactions occurring in 1546. If we now
turn to Doc. 10, dated 29 November 1579, we see that this is a declaration
to the effect that Kamana and others, seven ‘inhabitants’ of the village, had
sold six plots of land for a total price of Rs.238 to Jiv Gusain, represented
by Gopaldas Pujari. The six plots correspond to the six mentioned in Doc.
 11, as may be verified from the limits defined for each. One must then
conclude that this was really a fictitious deed which Raghunathdas had
prepared in favour ofJiv Goswami in order to make the latter’s entitlement
safer.
    But there are other deeds also in favour of Jiv Goswami, relating to
plots specified in Docs. 10 and 11. These are Docs. 3, 5, 6, 7, and 9,
being respectively the sale-deeds of plots 2, 6, 4, 5, and 3, as listed in
Doc. 11, with the same dates as specified there. They must, therefore,
be fictitious as well. The sellers in all of them, except for the seemingly
transcriptional omission of one name in Doc. 8, bear the names of the
very same ‘inhabitants’ who sold the whole set of the same plots in 1579,

been retained. Ilahi dates, as compared to Hijrl dates, when used in non-official
documents, seem to have been loosely employed and are likely to be inaccurate.
                        Land Rights in Braj Bhum                        131

although the deeds purport to bear dates from 1546 to 1577, a period of
over thirty years. Not only are the names of the sellers, along with their
fathers’ names, exactly the same, but the sellers’ names are also listed in
exactly the same order. The same qazi Badruddin Husain constantly put
his seal on the documents (Docs. 3, 5, 6, 7, 9, 10, 11). There remains
little doubt, then, that these documents too are fictitious, to the extent
that they were all prepared much later than their purported dates. Their
actual preparation took place either with Doc. 10 in 1579, or, with both
Docs. 10 and 11, in 1588.
    This conclusion also makes any pre-1588 date dubious in respect of
Docs. 1, 2, 4, and 8. Of these Docs. 1, 2, and 4 purport to be deeds of
sales to Raghunathdas, and Doc. 8 of a sale to Jiv Goswami ‘through’
Raghunathdas. The plots mentioned do not correspond exactly with the
plots in Doc. 11, so far as dates and prices are concerned, but the sellers
are the same as in Doc. 10, identical again in names, parentage, and order
of mention. This is so even when Docs. 1 and 2 are dated 1538 and 1545,
and there is thus all the reason to believe that these two deeds were in
fact ‘created’ much later. In the case of Doc. 1, in any case, the reference
to ‘rupaiya* proclaims its fictitiousness, since in 1538 Sher Shah had not
seized the throne of Delhi and the rupee had not yet been minted by
him. We must, therefore, assume that for some reason Raghunathdas was
anxious to establish his claims (and those of Jiv Goswami) on the land and
thus created the fiction of successive sales to Jiv Goswami, which had not
actually occurred in the manner represented.
    We may thus take it for certain that irrespective of their dates, all
these documents in the Arith set were probably prepared very close to
each other in point of time. While this element of fictitiousness must
be borne in mind when we scrutinise the evidence of these documents,
it does not by any means reduce their significance for our understanding
of contemporary agrarian conditions. Obviously, the seven sellers were
actual individuals. In the Persian documents (Nos. 3 and 4 to 10), they are
modestly described as ‘inhabitants’ (mutawattanan) of village Arith. But
in Doc. 11 they are termed muqaddams (headmen). In Doc. 2, which is in
Braj, they are called panch mukadaman and if Doc. 1 were not damaged
here, it might also have so described them. They are also called panch
elsewhere in the relevant portions of Docs. 1 and 2 and in Doc. 4. Since
they number seven, panch cannot mean literally ‘five’ here. The sense
seems to be of their being mukadams (=muqaddams), holding a position
which is assumed to be identical with that of panch, in the sense of village
oligarch.
132                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

   The names of the fathers furnished for each of the seven sellers show
that none of them were brothers. In other words, there is no reason to
believe that they were holders ofjoint-family property. Though they claim
in Doc. 10 that they were the ‘proprietors’ of the land sold by them, in all
the other documents they make no such claim, but say simply that they
had sold ‘the land of the village’ (zamin-t mauza'). It must be remembered
that Doc, 10 is a formal deposition they explicitly made before the Qazi,
and so has to carry the statement that the land sold was their exclusive
property. It would tax one’s credulity to suppose that in each of the six
plots they had equal shares of property as individual owners. The real state
of affairs seems to be that though not formally recognised by the Mughal
administration as headmen and so compelled to style themselves as mere
‘inhabitants’ in all the Persian deeds of sale, the sellers were in actual fact
‘headmen’ of the village body. As "punch headmen’ they disposed of village
land, and so were called upon to sell, or recognise the sale of, village land
such as that acquired by Raghunathdas through mere occupation or by
earlier purchase, to create the Radhakund and Kishankund ponds.
    Whether the amount of Rs.238 in fact changed hands or constituted
an imaginary price cannot be established, given the way in which, and
the purpose for which, the documents were prepared. The seven ‘sellers’
declared repeatedly that they had actually received the sums in question.
What they did with the money—or with whatever they received—is not
recorded. We would not know, unless further records appear, whether the
price was just distributed among the seven ‘sellers’ or went, in part at least,
into the village financial pool, from which the land-revenue was paid and
village expenses met.4


                                      II

The second set of documents (nos. 12-15) is the first of the four sets
concerned with Vrindavan and its immediate neighbourhood (for which
see Map 4). This particular set belongs to the village (and hamlet) of
Vrindavan proper.
   Here too, while the buyer again is Jiv Goswami, the sellers are numerous,
though not identical, in the different documents. The only exception is
provided by the pair of Docs. Nos. 12 and 13, where the sellers are not

   4 For such a village financial pool, see Irfan Habib, The Agrarian System of
Mughal India, Bombay, 1963, pp. 124-28; also ibid., 3rd ed., New Delhi, 2014,
pp 144-60.
                         Land Rights in Braj Bhum                          133

only identical, but placed in the same order, though Doc. 12 is dated 1558
and is in Braj and Doc. 13 is dated 1569 and is in Persian. From the limits
of the land specified and the identical price (Rs.60) one can see that the
land transferred was the same. (The only particular detail the Persian deed
adds is that of the area, viz., 10 bighas, 11 biswas). We must then assume
that the Persian deed was drawn up to formalise the transaction for legal
purposes (by obtaining the qazls seal of certification thereon),5 or that
the Braj document bears an earlier, fictitious date, and that the actual
transaction occurred in 1569. Of the sellers in Docs. 12 and 13, two,
possibly three (if Manga in Doc. No. 14 is the same as Gawanu) recur in
Doc. 14, of 1577. Since Doc. No. 15 was written twenty-four years later
(1601), it is not surprising that except for two common names (one Bhana
and the other that of a son of one of the sellers in Doc. 13), the other
five names (one is lost through damage) are new. Despite this change of
persons, the group of sellers seems to be a continuing body and to consist
of‘headmen’ representing the village.
    Doc. 12 does not give the sellers any designation, but says that besides
the cash price of Rs.60, garments worth Rs.10 were given to the panch.
At the end, the panch of the nagara (nagla, hamlet) depose as to the
correctness of the facts in the document. Since the same sellers in the
same transaction are designated muqaddams in Doc. 13, we have a fair case
for arguing that the sellers were not the owners of the land in question,
but were overseeing the sale of village land, in the same manner as the
Arith headmen had done.
    The sellers are still called muqaddams in 1577 (Doc. 14). But a change
in their designation takes place in Doc. 15 of 1601. The portion of the
text is damaged so that it is not absolutely clear that it has used the word
zamlndardn (zamindars), but the qdzi while affixing his seal, clearly uses
the word zamindars for the sellers, ‘Sital, etc.’. Otherwise here too, as in
all the other documents of this set, the sellers do not expressly claim that
the land they were selling had been their own personal property. Probably
they still remained headmen, but as the use of the term zamlndar was now
spreading,6 it came to be employed almost as a matter of course for them
as sellers of land.


    5 It may be noticed that the year on the qdzts seal is ah 978, whereas the
document is dated 977. This suggests that the document was drawn up in the
earlier year but the qdzts seal was obtained much later.
    6 Cf. Irfan Habib, ‘Aspects of Agrarian Relations and Economy in a Region
ofUttar Pradesh during the 16th Century’, IESIIR, vol. IV, no.3, 1967, pp. 211-

134                    Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

   Document 15 contains another point of interest. Two of the eight
headmen are Muslims (Junaid, Najm) judging by their names. This
suggests that ones position within the village community was not here
affected by a change of religion by any of its members.
   One cannot also pass by ‘All Khan, the Muslim muqaddam of village
Sei, in Doc. 13, who appears as the thakur of village Sei in Doc. 12,
with the further appellation of Chaudhurl. Obviously, he was more than
a headman, not to be put in the same class as the set of numerous sellers
we have so far been dealing with. He acted alone: having first obtained
land in Vrindavan, he then passed it on to Jiv Goswami. The term thakur,
therefore, expresses his position better, and in the official terminology
that would soon come into use he would be properly entitled to the
designation of zamindar. The avoidance of the word chaudhuri for him in
the Persian deed suggests that ‘Ali Khan was chaudhurl by status; he was
not an official of that designation.


                                    Ill

Docs. 16-20, all belonging to just two years, 1593 and 1594, relate to the
hamlet (nagla) Nagu of village Dosaij or Dosaich (now Dusayat, part of
the town ofVrindavan). In Docs. 16, 17, and 20 Goswami Haridas is the
buyer, in Doc. 18 the purchase is made on behalf of Raja Man Singh, the
ruler of Amber, by Haridas as an agent of his. These three documents
mention the reigning Emperor (Akbar), as well as the karori (or Imperial
Revenue Collector) of the sarkdr and, in two places, his subordinate,
the shiqqddr of pargana Mathura. This pair of officials is mentioned in
yet another document (No. 25) of 1598, in which Haridas once again
appears as the purchaser. In Docs. 18 and 23 the karori Mathuradas alone
is referred to. In Docs. 17 and 18 the sarkdr of Bayana is said to have been
under the charge of the karori and in Doc. 20 it is the tarafor tract of
Mathura that is placed under him. There was no sarkdr of Bayana now,
but there had been one once, and Mathura was still within the dastur-
circle of Bayana.7 These references to the Karori also show that during the
years 1593-8, Mathura and Vrindavan were part of the Khalisa (territory
under Imperial management) and not in the jagir of any noble. The
karori, we may remember, was an official of the khalisa; ajagirdar’s revenue
collector could only be called ‘dmil. Besides these pieces of information,

  7 Cf. our comments on this point in PIHC, 48th session, 1987, p. 245 (our
Chapter 3).
                         Land Rights in Braj Bhum                          135

the references to the karori and shiqqdar are only idiosyncrasies of Haridas
or his favourite draftsman, and cannot be held to mean that these two
worthies—any more than Emperor Akbar—were either present at, or in
any manner verified or guaranteed the sale. The documents too make
no such statement, the mention of officials being apparently merely to
reinforce the date and not to invoke their authority. Doc. 19, on the
contrary, was at least made before the hakim, the man-in-authority over
the area, that is, the local official appointed by either a jagirdar, or, if in
Khdlisa, the karori. One would have thought this was because Raja Man
Singh was a party here, if the same kind of statement had not been made
in three other documents of sale where a plebeian is the purchaser.8
   It would seem that the five documents of this set are divisible into
two classes. In the first class we have Doc. 16, 17, and 18, which record
transactions where individual persons could be selling their rights. In
Doc. 16, we have land, which is obviously on the way to becoming, if
it has not already become, township land. It belongs to a pati (patti) or
portion of the hamlet (nagard) of the Govind-dev temple. The sellers are
four in number, all brothers, and could have been joint-holders of the
land. In Docs. 17 and 18, there are two sellers each, and they again could
be disposing their own joint holding. The boundaries of the land given
in Doc. 17 reinforce this impression, since the fields of individuals who
are named are recorded on all sides, and among whom we find one of the
sellers, Nagu, himself.
   The second class consists of Docs. 19 and 20, where the picture is
markedly different. We have here as many as thirteen sellers, described in
Doc. 20 first as ‘Braj inhabitants of Shri Vrindavan’, and then as ‘all the
panch, acting jointly’ (savpanchan milikari). ‘All the panch’ are also made
to declare that the sale had actually been affected. The land sold is called
vithal-wari, presumably meaning land which could only produce stunted
crop (baithal),9 and thus otherwise waste. This would be the kind of land
which would be no mans land, and where accordingly the community
would have the sole voice in its disposal.
   Of the thirteen sellers, three are Muslims, viz., Nijam (Nizam), Hamau
or Humyan (Humayun), and Naso (Nasir?). The last would not have been
recognised as a Muslim, but for the spelling of his name in Doc. 19, with
an Arabic swad (s) used, and not an ordinary ‘s’, which implies that the
writer felt the villager’s name was a corruption of the Arabic Nasir, or

   8 Docs. 21-22 and 23 similarly state that the transactions were made ‘before
the Hakim.
   ’ See G.A. Grierson, Bihar Peasant Life, Delhi, 1975, p.269.
136                    Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

Nasir. This again underlines the fact that the panch of the village could be
heterogeneous in terms of their religious affiliation.
    Unlike Docs. 16, 17, and 20, which are all in Braj with much influence
of Persian, Doc. 19 is in Persian, and the buyer is Raja Man Singh; yet
the sellers are exactly those named in Doc. 20, and listed in exactly the
same order. There is no obvious element of fictitiousness here, since the
documents are only a little over six months apart, so that all the thirteen
‘sellers’ could have been alive on both occasions. In Doc. 20 they appear
as ‘the panch’; and it follows that in Doc. 19 too they act in the same
capacity. This may further explain why the order of names maintained
is the same: this was the customary way of placing each individual in
the hierarchy of the panch. The sellers’ position as the panch perhaps
explains the presence of the curious word hissa-i rasad, meaning share
to be divided proportionately, which they must have distributed among
themselves. Was this the remuneration they received for acting on behalf
of the village?
    Doc. 19 is interesting for other reasons too. It suggests how ‘community’
and individual rights could arise in the same piece of land. The thirteen
sellers claimed that they had lent Rs. 150 to Pathal Bairagi, deceased, with
part of which he had put up a structure (‘amid) on land belonging to the
sellers, which was rather small (4 biswas) in area. They were now selling
this ‘waste’ land, with the consent of Narayan, the Bairagjs disciple, to
Raja Man Singh for the sum of Rs.2O72. How the thirteen together could
have lent the Bairagj a large sum like Rs. 150 is not easy to understand,
unless those were payments made to the mendicant out of village funds or
raised by general contribution among villagers. The Bairagj was allowed
to set up a structure on the land, and thus occupy it without any act of
purchase. This nevertheless resulted in the creation of an individual right
in the land, and the sellers are careful to say that the Bairagi’s successor
had consented to the sale. The document, therefore, suggests that the
village community, through its accepted representatives, could only
dispose of ‘waste’ or unoccupied land; if the land was already occupied,
the agreement of the occupant would be necessary.


                                    IV

Docs. 21-25 record transactions in another portion of village Dosaich, the
patti or patl of Gopa, the deeds being made within the years 1595-98. In
four cases, the purchaser is Bhavanand, adhikari of the Gopinath Temple
                        Land Rights in Braj Bhum                         137

(the buyer also in Doc. 18) and in the fifth is Goswami Haridas, whom we
have already met. The sellers in all the cases are single individuals, except
in Doc. 21, where we have a pair. The actual area of land is indicated in
all transactions. The price of ‘cultivated land’ (Doc. 20) is exceptionally
high, amounting to over Rs. 14 per bigha (we take it that the tankas in the
amount of 2214 tankas mentioned in addition to the price in rupees, are
really dams or half-tankas, and represent as many fractions of the rupee
(40 dams = 1 Rupee)). On the other hand, the price is only Rs.4 for a
bigha of waste land in Doc. 22. How land prices could vary is illustrated by
Doc. 25, where one plot carries a rate of Rs.4 per bigha, but another only
Rs.2. All the transactions are among individuals, and there is no evidence
of any reference to ‘the panch* for their recognition or consent, even in
Doc. 23, which is in Braj. But Doc. 23 does give us some additional
insight into what the term panch meant. Mathuradas {Karori), described
as hakim (person in authority) in the Persian version, appears in the Braj
version as panch of the sarkdr of Bayana’. The word clearly means the
principal man here.


                                     V

In our last set, comprising two documents (Docs. 26 and 27), the sellers
made their position unambiguous by designating themselves ‘the panchdyat
of the village {deb)’ of the Gopinath Temple, the deh being presumably
a hamlet close to that temple. The names of only four sellers are given
(identical and with the same sequence of names in both documents), but
the addition of‘etc.’ {waghaira) indicates that the actual number was much
larger, and only four could be mentioned.
   The term panchdyat occurs very rarely in Persian documents. In a
document of 1730 from the district of Gurdaspur (Panjab), the phrase ‘the
entire panchdyat of the market {bazar) of the township’ obviously meant,
in this particular instance, the entire body of shopkeepers, who had agreed
to pay a certain rate to a local divine.10 In our present documents too, we
must assume that the panchdyat of the village meant the body of rural folk
who had a say in the allotment or alienation of village land that would
affect them all.
   Doc. 26 reminds us of Doc. 19, in that the panchdyat here was selling
land which had already been occupied, since it contained the house of one

   10 B.N. Goswami and J.S. Grewal, The Mughal and the Jogis of Jakhbar,
Shimla, 1967, pp. 157-58.
138                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

Kishan Gangar Baishnav, apparently not a member of the village body. No
reference to any consent from the Baishnav was made, which is in contrast
to what was carefully done in the transaction described in Doc. 19.
   In Doc. 27 the word representing what was sold is unfortunately not
clear; but it refers to the necessity of the sale because one Khesu had
already sold a house (havelt) in the vicinity of the Gopinath Temple, The
connexion is not immediately apparent, but the need for acquiring or
erecting buildings seems to be the particular purpose behind the two
purchases. We are now moving into an urban environment. This is brought
home to us especially by the witnesses who appeared in Doc. 26. One is
the muqaddam (headman), Raghu, not mentioned among the sellers and
so possibly the headman of the main village of Vrindavan. But among
other witnesses there are three baqqals (Banyas), and one (Dayal) a sarraf
(money-changer, banker). The temples and pilgrims were now obviously
drawing business to Vrindavan.


                                      VI

Before we close, we need to note the constant use of the rupee, a coin
introduced by Sher Shah during his short reign (1540-5), as the basic
coin in these documents. Even if we disregard the early dates affixed to
the Arith documents, as well as the date of Doc. 12, for reasons already
spelt out, it is worth noting that Doc. 13 of May 1569 still quotes the
price of land in rupees, and so too the Arith documents, Docs. 10 and 11
of 1579 and 1588. The tankas, when mentioned (Docs. 20 and 21 of 1594
and 1595), were obviously for fractional use only. It would seem, then,
that Mathura, being on the main route between Agra and Delhi, tended
to take to the new silver coinage much earlier than localities placed at a
distance from the principal mints.11



   11 On the slow pace of the rupee supplanting the copper tanka in quotations
of land prices from Akbar’s reign in other areas, see Irfan Habib, ‘A System of
Trimetallism in the Age of “the Price Revolution”: Effect of the Silver Influx on
the Mughal Monetary System’ in: John F. Richards, ed., The Imperial Monetary
System ofMughal India, Delhi, 1987, pp. 143-144. Cf. also Irfan Habib, Aspects
of Agrarian Relations and Economy in a Region of Uttar Pradesh during the 16th
Century’, IESHR, vol. IV no.3, 1967, pp. 217-18, for the rather late (rare before
1592-93) use of the rupee in sale-transactions in the area around Bilgram (West
U.P.).
                         Land Rights in Braj Bhum                          139

   It may also be noted, in passing, that not much should be made of the
use of the figure 8 for V2 rupee in Doc. 24. Ordinarily, it would mean that
annas, the silver fractional pieces, 16 to a rupee, were already in use under
Akbar. But the document is obviously a late copy and when the copy was
made, the scribe is likely to have written the figure for half the rupee in
the same manner as he would have had in a document of his own time,
when, unlike earlier times, annas were in everyday use.

                               CHAPTER 7



              The Peasants ofVrindavan
                                  Irfan Habib




As Vrindavan was the principal seat of the Chaitanya Gosa’ins, most of
their documents naturally relate to that township, which essentially grew
out of a few rural settlements near the village of Dosaich.1 The site of the
village of Dosaich is now represented by the Dusayat or Dosait Mohalla
of Vrindavan, 27°35’N., 77°42’E., situated about 9 kilometres north of
Mathura (27'30’N., 77°41’E), on the right bank of the Yamuna river, just
where it takes a short eastward turn in its general north-south course.12 To
the north-east of Dosaich was a hamlet (nagara, mod. Hindustani nagala)
attached to it, also called ‘Nagu ka Nagara’: the hamlet is first mentioned
in a Braj document of ad 1558 (Wright 3) and then in 1593 (Wright 5),
giving the hamlet’s longer name. Its situation is indicated by a Persian
document of 1695, where the ferry of Chirghat, which still appears on
the Survey of India’s Guide Map, 0.8 km. north-east of Dusayat, is placed
within its limits (Govind-dev coll., Jaipur). Another hamlet was that of
Nagla or Patti Gopa, which was situated to the south of Dosaich, since in
1598, land sold within it is shown as bordering the limits of village Rajpur
(Wright 7); and Rajpur stands south ofVrindavan (see Map 4).
    Our earliest document relating to land transactions in Vrindavan refers
to land transferred within Nagu ka Nagara. This is a Braj document dated


   1 Cf. Shireen Moosvi, ‘Vrindavan: the Evolution of a town in Mughal Times’,
in D.N. Jha, ed., The Evolution of the Nation: Pre-colonial to Post-Colonial: Essays
in Memory ofR.S. Sharma, Delhi, 2014, pp. 223-33.
   2 The most detailed map ofVrindavan available to me is the Survey of India’s
1:20,000 grided Guide Map of Mathura with an inset of Vrindavan. For the
environs ofVrindavan we have the Survey’s Sheet 54E 10, a map on the scale of
1:50,000.
150                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 vs 1615 (31 March 1558) recording a sale transaction, which is also the
 subject of a Persian document of ah 977 (21 May 1569)? On comparing
 the two versions, their sense becomes clear. The four persons making the
 statement of affirmation (tamassuk/iqrarfndmaj) (Nagu, Gavnu, Anant,
 and Ahladu) are the same, with identical names of their fathers, in both
versions. The difference is that while in the Braj version the four persons
 are called ‘the panch of Nagara, in the Persian version they are designated
 ‘muqaddams of village Bindraban (Vrindavan).’ Apart from showing that
Nagara was (now?) a part of Vrindavan and that panch and muqaddam
were treated as synonyms, the two versions establish that the transaction
involved land falling within the control of the village headmen in their
official capacity. This land had been sold to ‘All Khan, who in the Braj
version, is given the title of chaudhuri and the designation of jhakur of
 Sei’. In the Persian text he is called merely the muqaddam of the village of
Sei. He had paid Rs.60 (as purchase price) and Rs. 10 additionally towards
pahardvani (from Pers, pairdhan, upper garment?) for the panch. ‘All Khan
now transferred his rights to Jiv Gosain, the famous head of the Chaitanya
sect at Vrindavan, at apparently the same price of Rs. 60.
    There are two matters of interest here: first, we witness the right enjoyed
by a small set ofpanch to sell away what apparently was uncultivated land.
This kind of control by the panch over unoccupied land is well evidenced
by other transactions in Vrindavan as well as in Arith (Radhakund).3        4 In
a Braj document of 1662 from Nagar Nagu (Vrindavan), the distinction
between privately held and />anc/?-controlled land is put beyond doubt,
since it records simultaneously the sale of a peasant Hlra’s land (jimi,
from Persian zamin) for Rs.22, along with ‘land of the panch’ (jimi panch
ki) for Rs.28. Indeed, it is surely for this reason that six other villagers
(apparently the panch) appear in it as sellers, along with Hlra, the whole of
the land amounting to no more than 6 biswas (a mere 0.072 hectare, and
that size too only if biswas are of the ilahi scale) (Wright 14). Less easy
to understand is a transaction referred, to in a document (CU) with a text
difficult to decipher, probably of c.1707, in which two Vrindavan peasants,
Dundl and Manika, sold 7 biswas of land to Bhaiya Ganga Ram Prasad,
receiving Rs.72 as the price thereof, of which Rs.61 were appropriated by
‘the set of the panch (jamaat-i panchdn) of the said village’, while Dundis

   3 The Braj version is found in two copies preserved in Radha Damodar
collection (VRI, Ser. 5, Acc. 3 and Ser. 21, Acc. 37) and is deciphered in Wright
3. The Persian version also is from Radha Damodar, VRI, Ser. 56, Acc. 13).
   4 See Irfan Habib, The Agrarian System ofMughal India, 3rd ed., New Delhi,

                      Paiz Hob, b
                          The Peasants of Vrindavan                     151

son Beni Ram received the balance, viz., Rs. 11. Presumably, the land
concerned had belonged to the village, and so, in effect, to the panch,
while the two sellers were its secondary occupants.
    Second—to return to the sale made to ‘All Khan in 1558—the initial
sale was to a person whose village (Sei, 27°40*N., 77°39’E) stood 1014
kilometres away from the land sought to be purchased. Though 'Ali
Khan is described in the Persian version as a village muqaddam, his titles
chaudhurl and thdkur in the Braj version suggest that he was what would
have later been called a zamindar5 rather than a peasant elder. That he had
resources to invest in the acquisition of land would not then be surprising.
What is surprising is that he should have attempted investment in land
at such a distance from his home village. Did he expect that with the
demand for land growing in a developing pilgrim centre he could make
a gain from its sale later on, or was he merely expecting to get the land
cultivated at a profit, despite the distance from his home? Whatever the
case, a land market must have existed to make either kind of investment
possible.
    Another document, one of July 1652, shows that the right of the
panch implied a claim to a one-twentieth share in the tax, if not in the
gross produce, of peasant-held land. We have here the sale of the biswa-i
muqaddaml (claim to a twentieth on the basis of muqaddaml right) over
just one bigha (0.24 hectare, if bigha-i ilahi) in Nagla Nagu, Vrindavan,
for as much as Rs.17. This amount was obtained by three brothers, sons
of Jadu Gaurua, who transferred their right to Seoram, adhikafi of the
Govind-dev temple (Govind-dev Collection No. 39). Once turned into
monetary-income, the muqaddaml right could presumably also pass into
the hands of members of a non-peasant class, as in the present case.
    In the documents of sale of lands, careful distinction continued to be
made between ordinary peasants and the panch. Since the names of the
fathers and grandfathers and sometimes great grandfathers are specified it
has been possible to build up a family tree of one such peasant family in
Vrindavan, beginning with Jadu (son of Sandar Gaurua), who appears in
sale-deeds of 1599 and 1600 as a panch. It turns out that at any given time
only one member from amongst Jadus descendants was designated panch,
the principle of primogeniture being apparently followed.6
    Still, the number of the panch or muqaddams tended to increase with
time. Even in the case of the Jadu family, three brothers (one a minor of
 12 years) in 1653 claim to possess muqaddami right in a one-bigha plot

   s For the position of zamindar, see ibid., pp. 169 ff.
   6 Ibid., pp. 150-51.
152                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 (Govind-dev Collection, No. 39). Over eighty years earlier, in 1572, the
 personnel of the set of muqaddams in Dosaich appears to have undergone
 some change after the transaction recorded in Persian in 1569: of the
 five panch in the earlier sale Ganu drops out, but Kotu and Bhana are
 added (Radhadamodar 145: land sold for Rs.8 in village Vrindavan). By
 1594, the number ofpanch increases to thirteen (in village Dosaich, nagala
 Nagu, and [Patti] Bhana) with Ahlad and Bhana surviving from the set of
 five panch recorded in 1572. Of the eleven additions, three have Muslim
 names: Nijam (Nizam), Hamau (Humayun), and Naso (Nasir/Nasir) (sale
 of land by ‘all the panch together’: Braj version, Wright 6; Persian version,
 Govind-dev).
    Now, of these thirteen men, six appear as witnesses in a deed of sale
of land in ‘Nagu ki Nagara, by two peasants, one of them Shital, himself
a panch. Besides him, of the other panch appearing as witnesses, three
 (Ahlad, Narwe, and Gavan) are styled Gaurua, while Naso and Humau
 (Humayun) are not given any caste designation. Another peasant witness
Lakhe is also called a Gaurua (Wright 5). A panch in the set recorded
in 1594 may be the same as Ganesa, son of Murera, found selling land
in Nagla Nagu the same year (IVS 208), where along with his co-seller
 (Sahib son of Nanu), he is called a Gaurua. If Humau s field in nagala
Gopa continued to bear his name even in 1654 (Govind-dev coll., Persian
version; the detail is missing in its Braj version), he too was a Gaurua for
he is there so described. In subsequent times almost all the panch whose
caste is given turn out to be Gauruas: La'lchand in 1653 (Govind-dev 39);
‘All Khan, son of Angad, in 1654 (Persian, Govind-dev; Braj, Wright 12);
and Mankanth (son of La‘lchand), Dunda, and Madho in 1695 (Govind-
dev, Jaipur). It would seem as if all the panch or muqaddams here were of
the Gaurua caste, for no other caste name is suffixed to that of any panch
or muqaddam.
    Similar is the case with the ordinary peasant sellers of land wherever
the detail about their caste affiliation is supplied. Among those specifically
styled Gaurua are Lakhe in 1593 (Wright 5), Badi in 1604 (IVS, 183),
Sodaman and Saknl, sons of Bansi, son of Badi, in 1654 (NAI-2691/6),
the sons of Tursi/Tulsi also in 1654 (Persian version, Govind-dev; Braj,
Wright 12), Kisor in 1667 (Wright 15), three named sons of Sandal
(Sanbal) in 1701 (Madan Mohan 114), and Majhu in 1733 (NAI-2691/14).
One witness to a sale in 1599 is said to belong to the Jdti Kachhwaha
(IVS 156); and Kachhwaha was one of the two important branches of the
Gaurua caste, as we shall presently see.
    The Gaurua are Thakurs or Rajputs, whose status was treated as inferior
to others of that caste because of the prevalence of widow remarriage
                           The Peasants of Vrindavan                           153

among them. They carried among them the names of well-known Rajput
septs, such as Bachhals (mainly to be found in the north of Mathura)
and Kachhwahas (around Mathura).78While the A’in-i Akbari shows no
zamindar-caste against the pargana of Mathura, which was territorially
a very small sub-district of sarkar Agra, it shows against the adjoining
large pargana of Sahar (whose limits came up close to village Dosaich,
Vrindavan) the castes of Bachhal and Kachhwaha (between which are
sandwiched the names of Gujar and Jat) in the column for zamindars* It
follows that, at least, in this region Abu 1 Fazl was counting as zamindars
such castes as constituted the bodies of village panch or muqaddams,
though some of them must also have been zamindars proper to account
for the 200 horse and 7000 foot assigned to them in the Sahar pargana.
   There are only three instances on record where the actual cultivators
recorded are of a different caste from that of Gauruas. In 1706, among
the lands on borders of the plot sold is put the bagh (here meaning flower
garden?) of Parasram mdli, mdli being the professional caste of flower and
vegetable gardeners (document in coll, unspecified). In a chakndma of
January-February 1708 for 135 bighas of grant in Nagla Nagu, Dosaich, a
plot of 13 bighas out of the grant-area is said to be under ‘the cultivation
(kdsht) ofJuna Kachhi’ (CU), the Kachhis being a caste of reputed market
gardeners. And in 1725, among such fields bordering a plot is mentioned
‘the cultivated field (kdsht) of Kunja, bada-farosh (wine-seller)’ (IVS 207,
continued on IVS 213; copy, IVS 218). The last probably belonged to the
Kalal caste. The intrusion of these persons from non-peasant castes in
local land-holding may be explained by the demand for flowers, vegetables,
and intoxicants generated in the village of Dosaich as it developed into the
temple town of Vrindavan which naturally invited both the holy and the
unruly to itself. The presence of a wine-seller in the village is attested to
much earlier as well: in 1600 Gopinath, bada-farosh, who is described as
