a Nirankali (believer in a formless God), gifts a small (one-door) temple
 (devala) he owned to Naraindas Misra (a Brahman) (IVS 216).
    The temples that lent sanctity to Dosaich also sought land-ownership
there. Already in 1593, a Braj document, recording a land-sale in Nagar
Nagu (Wright 7), notes amidst the bordering land ‘the khet of Shri
Govindjiu’, i.e. a cultivated field of the Govind-dev temple. In 1653,
another Braj sale-deed of the same nagara (Wright 11) records ‘a bdgicha
(small garden) of Shri Govinddevajiu’ as well as ‘a kheta* (field) of the
same temple on the borders of the land sold. In 1654, the Braj version of
a sale-deed (Wright 12, to be considered further on) testifies to extensive
khetds of the Govind-dev temple in the Gopa ka Nagara. A document of
1653 (Braj, Wright 11, as well as its Persian version, Govind-dev 39), also
puts (in Braj) ‘the khetd of Radhabalabha (in Persian: ‘agricultural land
(zamin-i ziri) of Radhabalabha) on the border of the land sold. Here we
have the Radhaballabh temple, which, as we know from Akbar’s farman of
1598, had assigned to it 70 bigha ilahi in Dosaich (see Chapter 3).
    We do not know how the temples got these fields on their lands
cultivated. Had they leased out the land to peasants, one would presume
that the lessees’ names would have appeared in the records just cited.
A chaknama of January-February 1708 setting out the plots, amounting
to 135 bighas, into which the original grant of the Govind-dev temple,
in Nagla (Nagu), Vrindavan, was divided, shows just 10 bighas being
cultivated by one Raj pahi (peasant resident in another village) and 13
bighas under cultivation (kasht) by Juna Kachchhi (of the Kachhi caste)9
(CU). The plots must have been leased out to the two peasants, but we
do not, of course, know anything about the terms of the lease or the
amount of rent they paid to the temple. One infers, then, that most of
the temple’s remaining land must have been cultivated by its servants and
hired labourers—or through temporary lessees.
    Since on such land the management of the temple paid no tax, any
profit secured from direct management or the rent received from tenants
would have been unalloyed gains for the temple. But in one case we find
taxable land also being purchased by the temple, which could only happen
if it was expected that some gain would accrue from it even after paying
the tax due on it: in 1654, two Gaurua peasants sold 3 bighas of land to
the chief priest (adhikan) of the Govind-dev temple, when, the Persian
version (Govind-dev Coll.) makes it clear that the land was zir‘i khardji

   9 For the Kachhis, a caste of skilled market-gardeners, see W. Crooke, Tribes
and Castes of North-Western Provinces and Oudh, Calcutta, 1896, vol. Ill, pp.
77-83.
                          The Peasants of Vrindavan                          155

(agricultural, tax-paying), and that it bordered on three sides the in am
(tax-free) land of Mirza Raja Jai Singh, and on the fourth side the kharaji
(tax-paying) land of Hamau (Humayun) Gaurua. It may be borne in
mind that though the tax-free grant of 135 bighas held by the Govind-
dev temple in Dosaich had been transferred by Shahjahan’s farmdn of
1633 nominally to Mirza Raja Jai Singh (NAI-2671/4, also 2671/5),
the Braj version of the transaction of 1654 (Wright 12) puts ‘the khetas
of Shri Govinddevji’ on all three sides of the purchased field instead of
designating it Jai Singhs in dm land (Wright 12), the same being the case
with the chaknama of 1700.
    The Persian version of the 1654 document we have just been referring
to is the only one in the Vrindavan collection that alludes to the burden
of tax (fharaj) lying on peasant-held land. We have to turn here to Abu’l
Fazl’s A’in-i Akbarl, which gives the rates of tax jdasturs) in money per
bigha-i ilahi (0.24 hectare) for each crop. Dosaich/Vrindavan belonged
to the pargana of Mathura, and the dasturs applied in this pargana were
those sanctioned for the Bayana circle.1011 The rates for Bayana, as for other
circles in suba Agra, are set out in the A’in-i Akbarl separately for the rabi
and kharif harvests, through a fairly careful tabulation.11 Eighteen crops
are rated under the rabi season and twenty-three under the kharif for the
Bayana circle. Mathura was close to Agra, but it gained from being under
the Bayana circle, whose rates compared to those of Agra were slightly
lower, but for a few exceptions. Thus ordinary gram is rated under the
Agra circle at 44 dams 18 jitals (25 jitals making a dam), but at 42 dams,
 12 jitals, under Bayana; and cotton is rated at 87 dams, 5 jitals, under Agra,
but at 83 dams, 11 jitals under Bayana. Since the prices in Agra and its
environs are likely to have been higher, such marginally lower tax rates for
Bayana seem to be what one should expect.12 Basing herself on the Agra
rates and using price data from the A’in-i Akbari and the output estimates

156                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

and prices reported around 1870, Shireen Moosvi demonstrates that the
land-revenue under Akbars dasturs must have accounted for nearly half the
produce rather than a mere third.13 And this would have been true for the
 tax-rates levied on Vrindavan peasants as well, given that the lower price­
levels would have offset the slightly lower dastur rates there.
    One document, though late (1752), coming from the Jaipur archives
is of some interest here, for, while assigning the ‘village’ of Bindraban
placed within the jagir of the Amber ruler, it gives the jama1 of the village
 (nominal figure used for jagir assignment) as 1,75,500 dams, (nominally
Rs.4,388) with the more realistic estimate of actual receipts (basil) set at
Rs.1,437. In contrast, Bishanpur, an ordinary village in the same pargana
of Mathura whose figures appear side by side, was assigned a jama of
74,000 dams (nominally Rs.1,850) and a hdsil of Rs.606.14 Clearly, as a
village grown into a township, Vrindavan bore a much heavier tax burden
than an ordinary village. But how much of it came from the peasants who
were still left in the township cannot be established.
    We have seen in Chapter 1 that once Vrindavan began to develop into
a township, land prices began to rise, as illustrated by the rising rates per
bigha at which land changed hands. This meant that a number of peasants
in time parted with their land-holdings, selling at what they regarded as
favourable prices. Inevitably, this also led to mortgages where the peasants
would borrow against the value of their land. In 1628, when Kishan
Charan Gosa’in, adhikdri of the Madan Mohan temple, advanced Rs.30 to
four peasants while holding in mortgage 3 bighas of kyari or dressed plot,
he was entitled to receive interest from them but apparently obliged to pay
tax on the land (Wright 22). In 1743 his successor Ram Kishor advanced
Rs. 132 to four peasants (called jimidar here) at interest against land and
orchard valued at Rs, 137 (Wright 31). The peasants could not meet the
high rate of interest (which Professor Wright calculates to have been as
high as 3.788 per cent per month!), and so lost their land (Wright 32).
    As Vrindavan turned from a village into a township, and pressure
mounted on agricultural land, its peasants would have had to look for
land for habitation as much as for cultivation. This explains a bilingual
statement in 1706 by six named persons and others, who described


   13 Moosvi, op. cit., 2nd ed., pp. 108-111.
   14 G.N. Bahure and Chandramani Singh, Catalogue ofHistorical Documents in
Kapad Dwara, Jaipur, Jaipur, 1988, Plates 29 and 30. My reading of the date of
the document and the tax-figures is different from that of the editors, op. cit.,
p. 3. It may be kept in mind that the dams used in jama* figures bore the
character of a money of account, 40 such dams being deemed equal to a rupee.
                         The Peasants ofVrindavan                         157

themselves as 'zamindars of Sri Brindaban’. They had established their
huts on apparently vacant land belonging to the Madan Mohan temple.
When government horsemen (sawdrdn-i sarkdr) came to evict them, they
themselves demolished the walls and removed all the other materials.
They now affirmed that they had no claims over any material left on
the temples land, and admitted that it was unlawful to intrude into the
temples land, whether cultivated, residential, or containing a garden (MM
3).
    We need no sources to imagine that illiteracy was rampant in the rural
population. In 1640, five peasants in Nagara (at least two of whom were
designated panch) who sold away land, could only affix marks (nisani)
on the Braj sale-deed (Wright 8). In 1650, all three peasants acting as
sellers similarly had to scrawl marks (Wright 10). In 1654, all three
peasants selling land in Gopa ka Nagara affix marks, as do three witnesses,
including a muqaddam. Only ‘All Khan, a peasant witness, was able to
write ‘All’ in Nagari for his signature (dastkhat) (Wright 12). In 1659 all
six 'panch mukadams’ of Nagar Nagu who had land to sell had to affix
marks (Wright 13). So many persons in our documents have their names
recorded without even their marks, that one feels they could not even
handle the reed pen with their fingers to scrawl anything.
    Illiteracy existed side by side with ill health. One indication of this was
 the common occurrence of smallpox, of which we learn from the huliyas
 (physical descriptions) of persons who appeared before qazis or judges. In
 the surviving documents relating to Vrindavan in the period from 1653
 to 1734, we find a total of twenty-six peasants whose huliya descriptions
 have survived (we here eliminate reappearances of the same persons).
 Of these peasants ten, or nearly 40 per cent, bore pockmarks. On the
 other hand, among others including women, in Vrindavan, belonging to
 classes other than peasants, we find five persons who bore pockmarks
 out of a total of 20, or just 25 per cent. (None of the four women bore
 pockmarks; but a woman who appears in a slightly later document (MM
 25 of 1758), a member of a Mathur (high caste) family who sold urban
 land in Vrindavan, had pockmarks on her face.
    The huliyas also touch upon another custom, which probably had
 some superstition behind it: the piercing of mens earlobes. The practice
 seems to have increased with time at Vrindavan: in 1653, none of the
 four Gaurua peasants whose huliyas are recorded (Govind-dev 39) are
 shown as having their earlobes pierced, whereas from 1702 onwards, one
 hardly finds anyone, whether among peasants or men of upper classes,
 with unpierced ears. (None of the four women whose huliya is recorded
 158                    Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 in various documents were from peasant families. Nothing is said about
 their ears, but three of them had their noses pierced, all on the left side).
     From these mundane matters, one can move on to the peasants’
 religious affiliations. As may be expected, the overwhelming number of
 peasants of Vrindavan whom we meet in our records have Hindu names.
 But Muslims also appear by their side. The raised-mud boundary (med)
 of (the land of) Nijam (Nizam) is mentioned in 1593 (Wright 5); and
 in 1594 he appears alongside Hamau (Humayun), and Naso (Nasir)
 among the panch of Dosaich and Nagu, thirteen in number (Braj version,
Wright 6; Persian, Govind-dev). In 1601, among the muqaddams (now
styled zamindars) we find Junaid and Najam (an incorrect restoration of
 ‘Nijam’?) (Radhadamodar, 136). Subsequently, Muslim peasants cease to
appear among the muqaddams or panch, and one wonders whether this
disappearance is because their sons bore Hindu names. This seems at
least to have been the case with Junaid, whose sons bore the names of
Gorai and Hira, according to a Braj document of 1662 (Wright 14). On
the other hand, we have ‘All Khan, who first appears in our documents
in 1648 (Wright 9). His khet or field is mentioned in 1650 (Wright 10),
and in 1654, he appears in the important capacity of guarantor (kafil) of
a sale: he is now described as the son of Angad, son of Raghu Gaurua,
and as aged 50 years (Braj, Wright 12; Persian, Govind-dev). He was thus
a convert. He is finally mentioned in 1659 and 1662 as a witness to sales
in two Braj documents (Wright 13 and 14).15 Apparently, conversions to
Islam or reconversions to Hinduism hardly affected ones status in the
village or caste. Could one say, then, that while caste was fixed, religion
could be fluid?16
    Finally, a few words about the language the peasants spoke. Of this
we have no record from the peasants themselves, for, as we have seen,
they were practically all unlettered. For all legal purposes, the statements
of transactions had to be drawn up in Persian. This was a language the
peasants could not have at all comprehended. Thus for their comprehension
and consent as a party to any transaction, a version had to be drawn up
and written out in Nagari or Kaithi in the local dialect, which we now



   15 Yet another similar convert was Jahangir, son of Balbadar, son of Autam,
who stood as guarantor for the sale of a plot of land in 1731 (Radhadamodar
132).
   16 See Irfan Habib, ‘From Arith to Radhakund: the History of a Braj Village
in Mughal Times’, PIHC, 71st Session (2010-11, Maida), 2011, p. 294 (Chapter
8 in this volume) for the same inference from similar evidence.
                           The Peasants of Vrindavan                             159

call Braj.17 Since this had necessarily to be written by literate persons well
above the peasants in station, we may assume that this too was not exactly
the dialect the peasants spoke; but it had to be one, at least, that they
could understand, and so must not have differed greatly from their own
common speech.
    That this was so is obvious from the highly Prakritic nature of the
language employed in our Braj documents, especially those written so as
to be intelligible to villagers involved in the transactions they record. It
is practically devoid of compound consonants, and ‘b* invariably supplants
‘v’. Both these features explain the spelling ‘Bindaban’ used in them
for the Sanskrit form Vrindavan. The letter ‘b’ was represented by the
Nagari letter for ‘v’; in the very rare cases where ‘v’ was required to be
pronounced, a dot was placed beneath this letter. The consonants ‘s’ and
‘sh’ were not distinguished: thus Krishna was written as Kisan (e.g. in
Kisandas: Wright 3). ‘Ya’ tended to be pronounced ‘ja: so for Yamuna
we have Jamuna (Wright 3). Particularly noticeable in the dialect is the
frequent occurrence of Persian words. The conjunction ‘and’ is normally
represented by wa (pronounced H, directly derived from Persian; both
tathd and aur (or rather aru) are very rarely used. If one takes just one
document at random, a short Braj land sale-deed (1653) at Vrindavan by
three peasants, all of them brothers (Wright 11), we find such Persian-
derived words as jimi (zamin, land); mukra (rnuqarrara, established,
standard); mdlkal (bi’lkul, absolutely); khusa-rajd (khushi-razd, own
volition); tul (till, length); araj (,‘arz, width); had (hadd, boundary); mana
(mana1, forbidding); ugahi (gawdhi, statement of witness); and sahi (sahih,
correct). The Prakritic tendencies in transforming Persian words are
noteworthy here; but the extensive drawing upon Persian vocabulary is in
itself remarkable. Indeed, in such plurals as panchan (plural of panch) we
can see even the influence of-an, the suffix in Persian indicating plurality
(e.g. mardan, men).
    While, generally speaking, the syntax of Braj in these documents
conforms to Hindustani usage of later times, there is considerable variation
in both vocabulary and pronunciation. ‘That’ is represented not by wah/us


    17 It is to be noted that we are dealing here with a dialect that stands far away
from the kind of literary Braj of the Mathura district, samples of which G.A.
Grierson provides in his Linguistic Survey of India, 1916 (reprint, Delhi, 1990),
vol. IX, Part 1, pp. 271-79. To mention one difference: where Grierson's sample
for the Mathura district, on p. 273, has wd or even wah for ‘that’, ‘he’, ‘it’, our
documents have ta. Nor do our documents have aur as a conjunction; they have
instead wa (ba), and, only very rarely, tathd or aru.
160                    Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

but td‘, the possessive (feminine) ki (but not ka) is present but alternates
with kau or ko. For simple kar di (‘has/have done’) in Hindustani we
have kari dim. Forms of words vary considerably from Hindustani, e.g.
utdr (uttar, north); pdcbhlma (pachchhim, west); man manwahl (=Hind.
manayen, mana* karenge, ‘will persuade and forbid’ with mana* borrowed
from Persian); kalikal (Hind, kabhi kal, ‘any time in future’); karare (Hind.
kinare, river-bank); tlsd (Hind, tis, thirty); and mah (Hind, men, in).
   We can thus see how different this village dialect was from the spoken
Hindustani of today. It is far more eclectic, variable, and Prakritic than the
Hindustani literary languages (Hindi and Urdu). Not aiming at style or
rhetoric, its simplicity, directness, and lack of verbiage give it a character
of its own.
                             CHAPTER 8



             From Arith to Radhakund
             The History of a Braj Village

                                Irfan Habib




Less than 5 kilometres to the north-east of Govardhan, within the
district of Mathura in western Uttar Pradesh, is situated the township of
Radhakund, 27° 32’ N., 77° 29’ E. In the early years of the sixteenth century
there was a hamlet here, bearing the name Arith, with no pretensions
apparently to any sanctity. But then came the Gosa ins (Goswamis) of the
Sri Chaitanya or Gaudiya sect and the village gradually gained in size and
importance as Krishna devotees (and others with them) began to come
and settle here. This process, fortunately for us, generated transactions
and disputes, and so led to the creation and preservation of a number of
documents relating to them, which form part of the Vrindavan archives
on which this volume is mainly based.1


                                       I

In two Braj documents of ad 1713 (Wright No. 17) and 1716 (NAI-
2671/21), the latter also containing a Persian version, the jamldar-
mokadams (headmen) of village Arith (which belonged to the Mughal
pargana of Sahar) declare that ‘Jati Raghunathdas Gosai Gaudiya’ cut down
the jungle and, while buying land from their ancestors and inspired by the
Lady (ThakuranijI, i.e. Radha), discovered Radhakund and Krishnakund.
These are the two tanks that are found today to the east of the town, both

   1 For a more detailed description of this collection, see the Introduction to
this volume.
 162                      Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

within a considerable stretch of scrub shown in the Survey of India’s Map
 54E6. Radhakund, the much larger of the two tanks, is situated to the
west of Krishnakund.
   Now, Rughunathdas Gosain is known to us from a number of
documents that range in dates (as given) from 1537-8 to 1588. All
of these relate to lands acquired in Arith. From these and some later
documents, it is possible to obtain an impression of what Arith was like
when Raghunath arrived there. As was recognized by the inhabitants
later, the two tanks did not exist, and jungle or scrub covered much of the
ground which Raghunath then began to clear and within which he dug
the tanks. The cultivated area was marked by raised mud-lines (medb),
and there was much waste beyond the line of cultivated fields and dressed
beds (khatwari, kydri).2 Near the hamlet, there were small heaps of ruins
or old debris (kucha khera)3 However small a kingdom it was, Lord Shiva
seems to have reigned over it, for there was a devala (temple) of Mahadeva
here. A well was identified by the name of its owner or builder (Gobinda)
and was presumably of masonry, but this is not stated. There was a drain
channel (nala), by the side of the land sold to Raghunath; and a large
neem tree, a karel tree, and a his tree (thorn bush) served as boundary
markers. All these details come from two Persian documents of ah 987/
ad 1579 (RD No. 11), and 996/1588 (VRI, S. No. 47, Acc. No. 8). These
features are also recorded in the earlier deeds, in Braj and Persian, for the
transfer of each of the six plots of land, which the two Persian documents
of 1579 and 1588 just mentioned record collectively.
   Such documents, numbering eleven in all, introduce us to one set of
inhabitants of the village. These are the seven sellers who appear constantly
(with the seventh omitted inexplicably in two of the sale deeds). Since the
Nagari characters are likely to represent the names of the sellers more
accurately than the Persian versions, these are reproduced below from the
Braj documents:

1.   Kamna, son of Kanhar /Kanhar
2.   Salu, son of Dusa
3.   Adhara, son of Mugasa
4.   Majha, son of Gula/Gale
5.   Ghaja/Gauhaja/Gohoja, son of Asuya/Asuba

    2 RD 11 of AH 987/ad 1597.1 take khatwari in Persian characters to represent
khetwari. Kyari should properly mean prepared beds for sowing (Fallon, S.V.).
    3 In a Braj text (Wright No. 1), this is called ‘Gav kheda, village ruins, not
‘fields’, as provisionally translated by Wright.
                           From Arith to Radhakund                              163

6. Gobinda, son of Chuhra/Chuhra
7. Baharna/Bo(ha)riya, son of Gangu/Gangu

    These persons describe themselves in the Persian versions of the sale
deeds as just ‘natives (mutawattandri) of the village’, but in the Persian
hibandma by Raghunath of 1588 (VRI, Si. No. 47, Acc. No. 8) they are
designated muqaddams or headmen. In the three Braj versions of the sale-
deeds they designate themselves mukadaman (Wright 1A), and grdm-vdsl
(village-resident) panch (Wright 1 and 2). In the Persian versions the land
they alienate is called zamln-i mauza‘ (‘land of the village’), as in MM
Nos. 14 and 17 (of 1546), Nos. 7, 8, and 10 (of 1577), and No. 6 (of
1578); and in RD No. 11 of 1579; all the six plots sold are stated to be
zamin-i ufiada or wasteland. Quite obviously, then, the sellers were the
village heads, representatives of certain families within the village, who as
a group exercised control over the disposal of wasteland (the descriptions
of all the lands transferred show that no cultivated land was involved).
To what caste they belonged is not indicated in these papers; but in a
Persian sale-deed of ah 1129/ad 1717 (NAI-2691/11), the two sellers,
the second of whom is Ranjit, son of Jagannath, son of Jairam, claim
to be of ‘the caste of Gaurwa (Gaurua) Jadon, [and] residents of village
Arith alias Radhakund’. Now, in a sale deed of 1667 (IVS 190) Ranjit s
father, ‘Jagannath, son of Jairam, son of Sanwle, Rajput Jasawat, resident
of village Arith’, records the sale of his own land to two Brahman buyers.
Jasawat is another name for the Gaurua caste in the Mathura District. The
Gauruas formed an old Rajput peasant caste in which widow remarriage
and levirate were practised.4 In 1713 and 1716, Jagannath’s son Ranjita
(=Ranjit), of‘the Sanvle clan’ (firqa/thok) joins other headmen from other
family-clans in stating that the land acquired by Raghunath Das was
obtained from their ‘ancestors’ (purakhdn) (Wright 17 and NAI-2671/21).
By this reference we can presume that the seven headmen selling land to


   4 H.M. Elliot, Memoirs of... the Races of the North-Western Provinces ofIndia,
ed. J. Beames, London, 1869, vol. I, p. 115; Denzil Ibbetson, Panjab Castes
(Report, 1883), Lahore, 1916, p. 138 (‘Gaurwa’); W. Crooke, The Tribes and
Castes of the North-Western Provinces and Oudh, Calcutta, 1896, vol. II, p. 404.
In the Agra region (to which Radhakund, of course, belongs), the Gauruas took
their Rajput status seriously enough to enter military service. See the story of the
two Gaurua brothers from Agra, who went in Sikandar Lodi’s time (c.1500) to
Raisen to serve as soldiers there, in Rizqullah Mushtaqi, Wdqi‘at-i Mushtaql, ed.
I.H. Siddiqui and W.H. Siddiqi, Rampur, 2006, p. 36. This work was completed
in 1572.
164                       Braj Bhiim in Mughal Times

Raghunath Das were also Gauruas; and so we had at Arith in the latter
part of the sixteenth century, a peasant settlement mainly of that caste,
comprising possibly no more than the seven families whose heads appear
here as the muqaddams, or, with a greater modesty in description, 'natives
of the village’, able collectively to dispose of the wasteland no one was
tilling.
    There were possibly peasants of some other castes as well to be found
in the village. A Braj deed, professedly of vs 1602/ad 1545, has Dangu
Gujar and Thalu Bagari as witnesses. Gujars are a well-known pastoral and
agricultural caste, and Bagris are often treated as a sept of the Jats.5
    A peasant settlement needs services of other groups as well. The
presence of Chamars, the disposers of dead animals and leather workers,6
is attested by a reference to a settlement of theirs. In the Braj portion
of the sale-deed of a plot of land, dated ad 1667, its southern boundary
is said to have been formed by chamdratd; in the Persian version this is
described as ‘the thala of Chamaran (Chamars)’ (IVS 190), thala, from that
(cf thala), meaning ground, here occupied by Chamars, which Chamdratd
also appears to signify. In vs 1785/ad 1728, a Braj sale-deed records the
sale by two Chamars (also written ‘Chamar’), who were the grandsons of
one Manohara, of a small piece of land (Wright No. 23). So a community
of these leather workers also served the hamlet.
    At the other end of the caste scale were the Brahmans. One supposes
that the devala of Mahadev must have necessitated the presence of a ,
Brahman priest already at the time that Raghunath arrived to pursue his
project. A Braj sale-deed of vs 1602/ad 1545 (date presumably fictitious)
shows Tribhangi Pande and Horala Pande appearing as witnesses (Wright
1), while in a later deed, doubtfully of vs 1605/ad 1548, Tribhangi Pande
appears as a witness along with Dasu Pande and Benbu Pande. We have
no knowledge of what occupations these Pandes pursued in the village, •
and even whether like the two deeds, these are not also fictitious persons.



   5 On Gujars, see Crooke, op. cit., vol. II, pp. 439-54. For Bagris see Elliot,
Memoirs, etc., vol. I, pp. 9-10.
   6 ‘Originally he (the Chamar) seems to have been an impressed labourer1
(begar[ij) who was made to hold the plough for his master, and received in return
space to build his mud hut near the village, a fixed allowance of grain for every
working day, the free use of wood and grass on village lands, and the skins and
bodies of the animals that died. This is very much the status of the Chamar at
the present day. He is still the field slave, the grass-cutter and carrion-eater of
the Indian village.’ (Nesfield, quoted by Crooke, Tribes and Castes, vol. II, p. 169).
                          From Arith to Radhakund                             165

                                       II

Into this village, with a small peasant settlement and large area of waste,
entered Raghunath, a Goswami of the Chaitanya sect. The first documented
act on his part was the acquisition of areas of village wasteland (zamin-i
uftadd) from the headmen, which was apparently done in six separate
transactions, for six different plots (Radhadamodar No. 11, of 1579, and
VRI, SI. No. 47, Acc. No .8, of 1588). It has been argued already7 that
all eleven documents recording the sales of these plots, extending in their
given dates from ad 1537-8 to 1588, cannot possibly be genuine, since
over a period of forty years (from 1537-8 to 1578) they list the same set
of persons as sellers, without a change in even their order of mention.
Moreover, this set of persons is said to sell the same plot in one document
to Raghunath, and in another to Jiv Gosa’in.8 All the extant sale deeds in
Persian for the different transactions make Jiv Gosa’in the buyer; but in
Raghunaths hibandma in Persian (VRI, SI. No. 47; Acc. No. 8), of ah
996/ad 1588, the dates of these very sale-deeds supposedly transacted by
Jiv Gosa’in are given for Raghunaths own acts of purchase of the same
plots of land, at identical prices, from the same set of sellers. It thus
seems certain that a fictitious record of the transactions was created in
Persian with the complicity of both Raghunath Das and Jiv Gosa in as well
as the seven headmen of Arith, this having been done probably around
1577-9, the period to which seven of the Persian documents belong. But,
then, the three Braj documents (of 1537-8, 1545, and 1548?) must also
be fictitious since they contain the names of the very same set of sellers!9
    It is, therefore, best to avoid going by the dates of the documents,
and only to accept as plausible the fact that by 1579 Raghunath had

    8 Thus, according to a Braj document (Wright 1), Raghunath Das bought a

being the same. The same is the case with the Braj deed of 1548 (?) (Wright 2)

in the same collection is another copy), Raghunath being the buyer in the former,
Jiv Gosa’in in the latter, and the price being the same (Rs.20).

166                        Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 successively bought six plots of untilled land to the east of Arith. The
 total price paid, reinforced by detailed plot prices recorded in Raghunaths
 hibanama (gift-deed) of 1588, was Rs.238, the same total as given in the
 headmens statement of 1579 in respect of all the plots (RD No. II).1011
    From the descriptions of the plots of land transferred we learn
 something about what Raghunath did with the land. He dug the two
ponds, the Radhakund and Krishnakund, within it, both of these being
mentioned in these documents. There was already a nala or drainage
channel, as we have seen; and this brought water ro Radhakund when in
flow, as we learn from later documents (MM No. 4 of ah 1052/ad 1642
and RD 102 of ah 1055/ad 1645). He also dug a well to supply water to
the pond, this being called the ‘pond-well’ (chah-i talah) in MM No. 8,
of ah 985/ad 1577 (cf. also kolab-o-chah (‘pond and well’) mentioned in
Raghunath’s hibanama, ah 996/ad 1588). This pond digging required
space in which the excavated soil was to be piled up in mounds (matt dari
ve ko, mail rarave kau—Wright 1A and 2, of 1537-8 and 1548(?) and Braj
Lals statement, post-1725 (CU)), for which too land had to be acquired.
    How Raghunath Das came by Rs.238 to invest in village wasteland in
Arith, we do not know. In ah 987/ad 1579, the seven headmen of Arith
were made to affirm, quite falsely, that all the money had been received
from Jiv Gosa’in through his agent, the pujari, Gopal Das, son of Radha
Ballabh (RD No. 11). This, as we have suggested, was probably done
to ensure that Jiv Gosa in’s claim over the land was placed legally on a
firmer basis. In ah 996/1588, as if to make assurance double sure, Gosa in
Raghunath Das transferred by way of gift (hiba) all the six plots of land he
had bought at Arith to Jiv Gosa in (VRI, SI. No. 47, Acc. No. 8). By about
this time at least two retreats (kunj), one of Raghunath himself and the
other of Jiv Gosa in, had also been set up near Radhakund (Krishnadas’s
statement, text pub., BSOAS, XLII (2), 1979, between pp. 308 & 309,
dated vs 1694/ad 1637). Finally, a small temple of Radhakund came up—
small, because in Akbar’s farmdn of 1598 (Zimn, III, xviii) it was not
thought of as worth more than ten bighas (ilahi) or 2.4 hectares of land in
tax-free grant (NAI-2671-1).11

   Jiv Gosa’in had by now doubtless attained a dominant position within
the Chaitanya sect in Vrindavan. Through his Sanskrit will of 1606 and
codicil of 1608 he left all his entitlements and effects to his disciple Bilasdas
(Vilasadasa), with instructions that after Bilasdas’s death these were to be
passed on to Krishandas (Krishnadasa).12 This is also put on record by
Krishandas in a bilingual statement, dated ad 1637, he having by now
received all the rights and properties, held by Jiv Gosa’in from Bilasdas,
including the effects of Jiv Gosa in’s uncles, Rup and Sanatan, and of
Raghunath, all of whom had gifted them to Jiv Gosa’in (BSOAS, XLII
(2), Plate III), This document further shows that Krishandas was now
exercising full control over the properties in Arith where he gifted the
house of Raghunath and Jiv Gosa’in to Chaudhuri Kishordas Soti, purohit.
The description of the boundaries of this property shows that the house
had Radhakund (the pond) to its south, and there were around it the kunj
(dwellings) of Radhaballabhjiu and of certain Mathurs (Brahmans from
Mathura?),1314and (a temple of) Biharijiu. Obviously, a settlement around
the ponds had by now been established.
   The Radhakund tank came after some time to face threats from the
extension of village habitation. There was an attempt to grow singharas
(water-chestnuts) in the pond. Here, for the first time, the Mughal
administration entered the picture. An unidentified local official issued
a parwdna in ah 1050/ad 1640-1, addressed to ‘the muqaddams and
bavdla-ddr [officer-in-charge] of village Arith’, directing them to prevent
cultivation of the plant in the pond, since it had been dug ‘by a person for
gaining merit’. The mention of ha vala-ddr, presumably the tax-collector’s
representative in charge of the village, is interesting, his jurisdiction
deriving, perhaps, from the fact that singhara cultivation could yield
revenue to the authorities?4
   In ah 1052/1642, three headmen of the village, Bari Khan, Chand, and
Girdhar, gave an undertaking to Gosa’in Krishandas that they would not
extend tillage around the ndla draining into Radhakund, nor cut any trees
there (MM No. 4), Krishandas also obtained a parwdna in ah 1055/ad

  muqaddams, gave a pledge to Sukhdev, the shiqqddr, that they would
  not till the land around the nala nor cut trees or their branches on land

     The ‘Vrindavan estate’, as we may for convenience call it, was also
 being menaced by the interference of moneyed men from Mathura. Bari,
 Chandrasen, Banwari, Gurusen, Dayal, etc., muqaddams, were enticed
 into selling some land ‘above the Kund and the kunj to Khamanand, a
 Brahman of Mathura, for Rs.20. Later (ah 1051/ad 1641), the headmen
 admitted that this they were not entitled to do, because, as Krishandas
 had shown them, the land had been sold to ‘Gosa’in Rup, Sanatan, and
 Ji Gosa’in by our ancestors’. The sale to Khamanand, therefore, stood
 annulled (RD No. 9). Khamanand was probably the unnamed Brahman
 who had been ‘making use of his money for some time’ to intrude into
 the estate around ‘Shri Radhakund’. Krishandas sought to foil his attempt
 by seeking introduction to a noble (nawwdb-i mustajdb), presumably the
jagirddr, through an intermediary (RD No. 96). This letter, as shown by
 the form of its invocation, cannot be later than the end of Shahjahans
 reign (1658),16
     The threat to the Vrindavan estate in Arith around the pond Radhakund
 seems obviously to have arisen because of the place’s growing sanctity and
 settlement, which invited the attention of the devout and caused land

There were two other adjoining kunjs and ‘the Arith road’ ran to their

    From these transactions, it seems established that the village headmen
(panch or muqaddams) were entitled only to dispose of the wasteland of the
village, and were not entitled to sell it again, even if it remained untilled
in the hands of the purchasers and their successors. As far as the privately
held lands adjacent to houses were concerned, these could be sold away
by the current holders without reference to the headmen. No instance,
however, of a cultivated field being offered for sale is documented in Arith
from the period before the end of Shahjahan’s reign.


                                       Ill

Arith was affected indirectly by a change in the Imperial government with
the deposition of Shahjahan and the accession of Aurangzeb in 1658-9.
In 1568 Jiv Gosain, at the recommendation of Todar Mal, had won for
himself the custodianship of both the Madan Mohan and Govind-dev
temples in Vrindavan ‘as it has been given to him also by Rup Gosa in’,
his uncle (Akbar’s farmdn, ah 976, VRI, Acc. No. 1). Expressly citing
this, Shahjahan had reserved for Jiv Gosa’in’s successor Krishandas the

 But Mirza Raja Jai Singh of Amber, who had already in 1633 obtained
 from the Emperor the stewardship of the Imperial land grant assigned to
the Govind-dev temple (NAI-2671/4), was apparently dissatisfied with
Krishandas. With his greatly enhanced influence at the court of the new
 emperor, Aurangzeb, he apparently obtained sanction for the removal of
the aged Krishandas, and in March 1659 issued a parwdna accusing the
latter of fraud (sdkht) and transferring the custody of Govind-dev temple
to Seo Ram (Shiv Ram) who belonged to the line of successors of Rup
Gosa’in (parwdna of ah 1069: Govind-dev Coll.).
    This change was bound to affect the authority of Krishandas and his
successors (in the line of Jiv Gosa’in) over the estate of Arith. Krishandas
was succeeded by Chand Kunwar, and he by Braj Kunwar: in 1669 Braj
Kunwar had the village headmen, Chandrasen, Jagannath, Bhikya, Siyaram,
Jairam, Hirdairam, etc., summoned to the presence of the jagirdar to give
a pledge that they would not till the land along the ndla feeding the
Radhakund, nor cut down trees in the ground above the pond. They
mentioned in their pledge that the area was in the vicinity of the kunj of
Braj Kunwar Das Gosa’in (RD No. 57). In 1676 Braj Kunwar, along with
Parmanand, both described as Bairagjs, secured a parwdna from Sayyid
Mubariz Khan, officer (in jdglr) of Prince Mu azzam, forbidding anyone
from interfering with their land and gardens (RD No. 95).
    But towards the end of the century Seo Ram s successors (of the line of
Rup Gosa’in) began to make claims on both the ponds and the adjacent
land. By a parwdna of ah 1112/ad 1700, Kahl Singh Rathor, apparently
an officer of the Amber court, directed that Gobind Charan, the successor
to Krishan Charan, who had succeeded Seo Ram, should take over the
custody of the kunjs of Jint (i.e. Jiv) Gosa’in in Vrindavan as well as Arith,
just as Krishan Charan had done (NAI-2671/16). In the hibandma of ah
 1121/ad 1710, by which Gobind Charan transferred all his possessions
and properties to his cousin and designated successor, Jagannath, he listed
among the assets transferred at Arith both the ponds Radhakund and
Krishankund, and the kunjs of Gobindrai (i.e. dwelling of [custodian of?]
Govind-dev temple), Radhakrishan Chakravarti, and Gobind Charan
himself, together with the adjacent wasteland (Govind-dev Coll.).
    This assumption of control over both the ponds was supported by a
new 'invention of tradition’, first recorded among extant documents in
vs 1770/ad 1713. Raghunathdas had, it was now averred, indeed made
the initial acquisitions of land and discovered or created the two ponds,
but he had transferred these to Haridas (not, that is, to Jiv Gosa’in), as
                          From Arith to Radhakund                            171

the custodian (adhikdri) of the Govind-dev temple. Henceforth all the
rights over these properties belonged to the custodian of that temple, the
current incumbent being Jagannath. On that basis, Jagannath could claim
to control all access to Radhakund and to the Vaishnav assemblies (samdj)
for katha-kirtan there. (This declaration was made in March 1713 by all
the zamindars and muqaddams (jamldar-mokadaman) of Arith, four of
whom are named (declaration in Braj: Wright No. 17)). This was repeated
in a bilingual document of vs 1773/ad 1716 (NAI-2671/21).
    The occasion for such assertion was apparently not any challenge
from the line of Jiv Gosa’in, that was at this time torn by a succession
dispute after the death of Braj Kunwar in or before 1700, but from
another important faction of the Chaitanya sect. This group included
Ramchand Ghosh Thakur, Svami Mohandas, Rup Kaviraj, Gobindpriya,
Krishnapriya, Bisvanath Chakarbarti, Manohar Ray, and Anantram, who
had all been expelled, apparently at Jagannath’s orders, but had then been
readmitted to Radhakund, according to the headmens declaration of 1713
(Wright 17). However, by their declaration of 1716, where they recorded
their allegiance to Jagannath once again, the headmen acknowledged
that the same eight persons (including the two women, Gobindpriya and
Krishnapriya) would again be excluded from Radha-kund (NAI-267/21).
The reason for this may well be that Mohandas, the second in the excluded
group, was practically a rival to Jagannath among the Chaitanya mendicants
at Vrindavan: in 1718, when there was some suspicion of maldistribution
in the disposal of alms, the Mughal official Mahyar Khan chose Brajanand
(kinsman and close confidant of Jagannath) and Mohandas to supervise
their distribution among the Gaudiya sect (Mahyar Khan’s parwana,
RY 7 of Farrukhsiyar, collection unspecified).18 Clearly, there must have
been some anxiety that, possessing such high status, Mohandas and his
associates might establish themselves at Arith as well, to the detriment of
Jagannath’s authority there.
   Once Gopi Raman, ofJiv Gosa’ins line, was able to establish his position
as the successor of Braj Kunwar, especially after the death by 1718 of his
rival claimant, Gopinath, he too began to take interest in the property at
Arith. He drew officials’ attention to the fact that one of his ‘ancestors’
(buzargdn) had ‘purchased land within the limits of village Arith, known
as Radhakund, and established a pond and gardens there’ (parwdnas

issued in ry 2 of Muhammad Shah, ad 1720: RD Nos. 27 and 47; MM,
unnumbered: 3 parwanas). Possibly some arrangement had been made
with Jagannath over the two ponds and land attached to them, for Gopi
Ramans complaint was now directed not against Jagannath, but against
Nathu Ram, a Brahman, resident of Mathura, who having seized some plot
within the land inherited by Gopi Raman, wished to set up a compound
 (ahata) there. The parwanas show that Gopi Raman’s complaints were
upheld by the officials (see besides the parwanas cited earlier, the parwanas
of the same year, RD Nos. 213 and 40).19 Apparently, Nathu Ram was
a person of some influence, for a letter survives written to him by an
unnamed official pointing out that Gopi Raman had complained bitterly
to Raja Muhkam Singh, an influential noble, on whose recommendation
orders restraining Nathu Ram had been sent to the headmen and to Nathu
Ram himself. The latter was, therefore, requested to suspend the building
of his kunj until the dispute was settled (RD No. 184).
    The incursions on the land around the sacred tanks however, continued.
Gopi Ramans son and successor Braj Lal, in a Persian statement for public
endorsement, undated but presumably some time after 1725, complained
that three Brahmans (named) had forcibly seized land to the south-west
of Radhakund, which contained mounds of earth dug out when the kund
was excavated, and were now bent on selling it. Braj Lal intended to take
his complaint, when endorsed by a number of leading citizens (panchari)
of Arith, to Sri Maharaja Dhiraj (Savai Jai Singh of Amber) (CU).
    Quite obviously, by this time, and perhaps much earlier, there was
no village wasteland left for the village headmen to sell. So newcomers,
attracted by the villages increasing sanctity, needed either to covet parts
of the wasteland under the control of the Vrindavan divines, especially
such as what lay near the sacred tanks, or purchase land at increasingly
high prices from other owners. In 1667 four villagers, Ghasi, son of
Jaimal, and Khandi, Hirdai, and Ghasi, sons of Jagdev, sold 300 square
diras or 15 biswas of land to Gosa’ins Manikanth and Kamal Nain for as
much as Rs.30. We learn from an additional note in the document that
an equivalent area of land was sold to the two Gosa ins subsequently for a
larger sum (Rs. 37) and that the two purchasers were priests of the devala
Radhakrishan (Bilingual: IVS 190). Thus a new temple of this name had
now been built. In 1710 Kishan Rai, the grandson of Gosain Gopinath,
sold a kunj with 'surrounding walls of brick and mud, and partly roofed
with wood’, situated within two plots of land, the total area being 1244

   19 It is interesting that one of these parwanas to restrain Nathu Ram is issued
by Churaman, the Jat chief, then in Mughal service (RD No. 47).
                         From Arith to Radhakund                          173

square dir a, or just over 3 bighas, 2 biswas, for Rs.60 to Gosa’in Radha
Mohan.
    Jagannath, the custodian of Govind-dev temple, had not apparently
been satisfied with the land he already controlled at Arith. Two interesting
purchases of his are recorded. In ah 1129/1717 he purchased from two
Gauruas Har Rai and Ranjit, with different parents, a ‘garden’ known by
the name Ghasi Ram, comprising 5400 square dir as (13 bighas, 10 biswas)
for the large sum of Rs.250. How the settlement had grown with varied
occupants is shown by the fact that the garden bordered (E) the land of
one Narayan Soja; (W) the wall of the kunj of Lahori Misr, Brahman; (S)
the land ofMathura, shoemaker; and (N) the public road (NAI-2691/11).
In 1728, Jagannath purchased a plot of land (area not mentioned) from
two Chamars, Khunya and Goyasya, for Rs.51. The Chamars’ plot largely
bordered open land: (W) Ballabh Seths garden; (N) the,garden of Govind-
dev temple; (E) Sadarams dressed field (kyari); and (S.) Ballabh Naina’s
barn (kothawdri) and vegetable garden (Wright 23). Clearly, the Chamars’
holding, surrounded by cultivation, was situated considerably away from
the main settlement.
    Religious sentiment could also make one part with ones land. In 1722,
eight zamindars of four families joined together to gift in pun-arth four
bighas of land to Gosa’in Subal Das, adhikdri of the Madan Mohan temple
of Vrindavan. The land bordered dressed plots on two sides and one kunj
each on the two other sides, the kunj of Manu Joshi and that of Daya Ram
 Sahu (MM No. 82). Apparently, an astrologer (joshi) and a moneylender
 (sdhu) had also now come to live (and do business?) in Arith.


                                     IV

We have seen how the settlement at Arith was expanding in area, mainly
because of its increasing attraction as a Vaishnav pilgrim or religious centre,
with its holy tanks and temples. Along with the priests and pilgrims came
shoemakers, astrologers and moneylenders, to cater to the needs of both
the permanent and floating populations—and so their chance mention
in our documents. Money too came in as land prices rose, to meet the
demand from persons with means coming from outside and wishing to set
up kunjs or dwellings, and even temples.
   Such circumstances were bound to alter the conditions of the original
peasant community. In our sixteenth-century documents we find at
Arith seven headmen perhaps representing seven peasant families of the
174                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

 village with little indication of differentiation. But in 1713 and 1716, the
 differentiation is plainly recognized in the formula ‘V, X, Y, Z, and all
 small and big zamindars and muqaddams of village Arith’ (Wright 17; and
 the Braj version of the headmens declaration of 1716 in NAI-2671/21).
 In the Arith documents the term zamindar (jamidar in Braj) now appears
 for the first time, but in a clearly devalued sense of large land-holding
 peasants.
     How this development took place step by step cannot be traced. The
 external influences which now penetrated the village were not wholly
 Vaishnav. Islam came too, and when in 1641 we first meet the headmen
 of the village after Raghunaths time, five are named and the first of
 them is Bari (Khan), the others Chandrasen, Banwari, Guru Sen, Dayal,
 etc. (RD No. 9); in 1642, we have Bari Khan, Chandarsen, and Girdhar
 (bilingual, MM No. 4); and in 1645, Bari, Chandrasen, Banwari, etc. (RD
 No. 102). There is no need to assume that Bari Khan was an outsider;
 he was presumably a convert and retained his position in the Gaurua
 caste. In 1717 we have ‘All Khan, who is mentioned as the father of Go lai
 and grandfather of Har Rai, who himself, along with Jagannath, son of
Jairam, is said to be of the Gaurua Jadon caste (qaum) (NAI-2691/11).
Thus ‘Ali Khan, as the grandfather of one of the two sellers, despite his
 conversion, had remained a recognized member of the Gaurua caste. In
 1722, among the eight 'zamindars’ making the gift in pun-arth (pun-arth,
donated property) of 4 bighas of land, already mentioned, were ‘All Khan’s
son, Golals two sons, Har Rai and Raja Ram, who did so, unmindful of
the faith of their grandfather and, perhaps, father (MM No. 82).
    When in 1669 we have another list of headmen, Bari Khan had probably
died, for Chandarsen now headed the list with Jagannath, Bhikya, Siyaram,
Jairam, Hirdairam, etc., as his colleagues. They were directed by a local
official to refrain from tilling the ground and cutting down trees in the
land around Radhakund (RD No. 57). Since the names of the fathers of
the persons forming the group of headmen are not given, the connection
with the earlier set of muqaddams headed by Bari Khan cannot be traced
except for the continued presence of Chandarsen.
    Two documents of 1713 and 1716 (Wright 17 and NAI-2691/11) are
most valuable in this respect, since they provide greater details about the
individuals composing the set of headmen, though the muqaddams named
are only four. Combining the Braj texts of 1713 and 1716 and the Persian
version of the 1716 text, we obtain the following particulars about the
four men:
                           From Arith to Radhakund                              175

1.   Raja Ram, son of Hridai, of the firqa/thok Trabhangi
2.   Chaju, son of Dayal, of the firqa/thok Das
3.   Pita, son of Gopi, of the firqa/thok Gale/Gali
4.   Ranjlta, son ofJagannath, of the firqa/thok Sanvle

    The word firqa in the Persian version (1716 text) means clan, group,
sect, and here it may mean a set of families with a common ancestor. This
would be supported by the fact that Ranjltas father Jagannath is shown
by a sale-deed of 1667 (IVS-190) to have been the grandson of Sanvle,
by whom his firqa is here designated. Similarly, Gale/Gula was the father
of Maj ha, who appears on the lists of the muqaddams or panch who sold
land to Raghunath Gosa’in in Akbar’s time. Trabhangi and Das could thus
also have been headmen in some earlier generations. While the main text
of the Braj document of 1713 does not use any designation for Trabhangi,
etc., in the ‘signatures’ each headman is identified by his bos (e.g. ‘Ranjit,
Sarnie [Sanvle] ko bas me). Wright, apparently taking the term as a
variant of the word vas, derived it from Sanskrit vansa, and so meaning
‘descendant’.2021Finally, thok, used in lieu offirqa in the 1716 Braj version,
means a body of persons or community, and so can also stand for a group
of families descended from the same ancestor. Now since such groups,
under the so-called bhaiyachara or community arrangements, divided
village lands among themselves, thok also came to mean a subdivision of
the village land, corresponding to pattiF
    It would thus seem that four of these sets of families had by now
become preponderant in the village and possibly carved out large shares of
land for themselves, while there were, as the Braj versions expressly state,
yet other ‘small and big zamindars and muqaddams as well, to whom
must have been left the remainder of the land. A sale-deed of 1717 (NAI-
2691/11) may illustrate how different families shared land lying within the
same portion or thok. Ranjit, son of Jagannath and grandson of Jairam, as
we have seen, was one of the four headmen. Here he sells land (the ‘Ghasi
Ram’ bagh or garden), 5400 square dir'a (13 bighas, 10 biswas) in area,
in concert with Har Rai, the grandson of ‘All Khan, both sellers being
described as Gaurua of the Jadon sept. But there are complexities here.

    20 As has been noted in the introduction to this volume, I was most kindly
given his decipherments and provisional translations of these documents by
Professor J.C. Wright; and from these I have naturally benefited greatly.
    21 Elliot, Memoirs, etc., vol. II, p. 23. For the occurrence of the word thok in
village names in the Mathura District, see F.S. Growse, Mathura-, a District
Memoir (1882), reprint, Delhi, 1979, p. 340.
176                     Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

In 1722 the same Har Rai was to join with Pitambar, son of Gopinath,
surely identical with Pita, son of Gopi of the thok Gale/Gali, and other
‘zamindars1 in gifting land to Gosa’in Subaldas (MM No. 82). Thus the
‘All Khan family had shares in different fields within the thoks of both
Sanwle and Gale.
    The pair of documents of 1713 and 1716 possess yet another element of
importance. The headmen here, as in no other earlier document, declare
their absolute subservience to the custodian of the Govind-dev temple in
all matters relating to access (shud-amad) to Radhakund. Clearly, they
realized what benefits were accruing to them from the growing sanctity
and repute of the pond that drew the Vaishnavite priests and pilgrims to
their village. The gradual change in the village name was another index
of the mounting dominance of Radhakund in their lives. In the extant
original documents (as against copies of the originals made later in the
eighteenth century), the headmen usually described themselves as those
of village Arith, but even in a document as early as 1579 (RD No. 11)
the name of Arith is followed by the words ‘also known as Radhakund.’
While in 1669 the muqaddams still called their village simply Arith (RD
 No. 57), in a parwdna issued in 1676 by Mubariz Khan the village is just
called Radhakund, without mention of Arith (RD No. 95). However,
even in their declarations of allegiance to the Chaitanya sect of 1713 and
 1716 (Wright 17 and NAI-2671/21) the headmen still use the name Arith
 alone, meaning the replacement of the name Arith by Radhakund, though
by now advanced, was still in process until the 1720 s when our documents
on Arith come to a close.22
    The study of the history of Arith is of much interest in showing
that Mughal-period villages were by no means immune to change. So
far as we can judge from our documents, money relations prevailed and
even wasteland was not without its price. A village was by no means an
 isolated republic, for outsiders could, especially by use of money, establish
themselves within it. Nor was it immune from the authority of the
 state, whose officials could intervene and compel headmen to carry out
 their wishes. Enforcement of property rights by the administration was
 generally expected; otherwise one cannot explain the care bestowed on
 the documenting of private transactions in Persian, obviously for use as



   22 By the late 19th century a legend seems to have been invoked to link the
name Arith to the Krishna lore: here Lord Krishna had supposedly killed the
bull Arishtha, and ‘the two famous tanks [were] prepared for Krishna’s expiatory
ablution (Growse, op. cit., pp.82-83).
                        From Arith to Radhakund                        177

evidence of property ownership, to be submitted when required before
authorities outside the village.
   True enough, our documents shed little or no light on other crucial
aspects of agrarian life such as the land tax, rent, and wages. Apparently,
the gosa’ins paid no tax on the ponds or the wastelands, or kunjs in their
possession, and so their documents have nothing to tell us on that score.
For peasants and artisans conditions of life must have been difficult;
but quite naturally their own voice hardly ever comes to us through the
Vrindavan records, important and even unique as they still are.


Addendum

On a fresh reading of the documents an interesting fact came to light.
In the acknowledgement of the purchase of land on behalf of Sitaldas
that Murlidhar, son of Gahgaram, recorded in 1658 (in both Persian and
Braj), while defining the boundaries of the land, the kunj of Nara in Bhatt
(so spelt in Nagari; ‘Narayan Bath’ is the form in Persian) is mentioned
as bordering the plot on the north (IVS-217), Is it possible that we have
here a reference to Narayan Bhatt, the author of Vraja-bhakti-vildsa,
who ‘describes himself as living at Sri-kund, i.e., Radhakund’ (Growse,
Mathura: a District Memoir, p. 89)? The date assigned to him by Growse,
viz., ad 1553, is, of course, far too early, for by that time Radhakund had
hardly been dug. As we have seen, all the extant documents from Arith/
Radhakund, bearing dates prior to 1560, are patently fictitious, and some
later ones, perhaps, as well. So, the Narayan Bhatt of our document could
well have been the author of Vraj-bhakti-vildsa after all.
                             CHAPTER 9



      Land Holdings and Land-Control
             in Village Rajpur
                                Irfan Habib




The Survey of India’s Guide Map of Mathura and Vrindavan, 1989 (scale,
 1:20,000), shows the village of Rajpur (27°34’ N., 77°42’E.)as situated
on the southern outskirts of the expanding township ofVrindavan. From
the Madan Mohan temple, with which Rajpur came to have a special
connection, and which stands on the north-western edge of Vrindavan,
the map shows the village to be at a distance of 1.7 kilometres in a straight
line.
    The earliest extant original document known to us in which the name
of the village Rajpur appears is Raja Todar Mais parwana of Rabf II 992
(12 April-10 May 1584).1 Here not only does the name of the village as
Rajpur occur, but its older name Belu is also mentioned. Since this is an
order issued by Todar Mai to his own official, Udai Chand, about the
assignment in charity (khairdt) of 100 bighas of‘self-cultivated, tilled land
(mazru-khwud-kashtay to Gopaldas, sevak of the Madan Mohan temple,
it is clear that Todar Mai must have then held in his jagir the village of
Rajpur, presumably along with Mathura. That Todar Mai paid special
attention to this village is also shown by the fact that he laid out a garden


    1 RK No. 154. This parwana is also of some interest for its seal, reading:    .
Todar Mai, banda-i dargah, Ram kl pandh (‘Todar Mai, Slave of the (Imperial)
Court, under Protection of Ram’), the last three words forming a Hindi phrase,   .
though pandh is a Persian word. The same legend is found in Todar Mai’s seal      ■
affixed on the back of a farman of Akbar, issued in 1575, for consolidating      ;

 here, which is mentioned in a chaknama of ah 1103/ad 1692 as bordering

 that the name of Rajpur to replace the older one, ‘Belu, was given to the
 village by Raja Todar Mai himself, the first syllable of the name recalling

 ilahi, or 18.26 hectares) already existed within the village. This, as we

 In other words, the grantee, Madho, priest of the Gopinath temple,
 Vrindavan, was expected to settle peasants here or otherwise arrange for

cultivation of the area granted, while enjoying the privilege of having to

   The village saw yet another grant made out of the land situated within it.
In 1598, an imperial farmdn confirmed 500 bighas already given to priests
of the temples in Mathura and Vrindavan and their vicinity, and made an
additional grant to the temples of another 500 bighas. Of the latter, 85
bighas ilahi (20.40 hectares) lying within village Rajpur were granted to the
Madan Mohan temple, besides 35 bighas in Vrindavan granted to the same
temple.6 Land under these grants was to be demarcated half in cultivated
land (and so hitherto revenue-paying) and half in uncultivated land under
the Rule of‘Half’ (bid-munasifa).
   So far as we know, then, by 1600, over 60 hectares of the land within
the village precincts had been made tax-free for the Madan Mohan temple
and its custodians and for another family of priests connected with the
Gopinath temple of Vrindavan. Of these grants, the one created by Todar
Mais orders of 1584 has generated the most documents judging from
what has survived; and two of these, being chakndmas, of 1626 and 1692,
yield some valuable information about the village and its inhabitants as
well.
   Todar Mai’s parwdna, setting aside 100 bighas (before introduction of
the gaz-i ilahi) in grant for the benefit of ‘Gopaldas, sevak of the Madan
Mohan temple’, had no imperial sanction behind it, and it could have
been revoked by any of the jagirdars succeeding him. This did not
apparently happen; but after Gopaldass death, his successor at the temple
as its (chief?) sevak, Srichand, took steps to get the grant converted into
an imperial one, and have himself recorded as the grantee. His efforts
resulted in Jahangirs farmdn of 14 Aban ry 8 (6 November 1613) by
which the land of the grant, computed as equal to 89 bighas, 9 biswas upon
conversion of the old bigha into bigha-i ilahi, became a normal imperial
madad-i ma'dsb grant. By this farmdn the land had to be measured and
demarcated (paimuda wa chak-basta).7 The initial demarcation document

 (chaknama) has not survived, having been allegedly lost during the Jat

     For some reason, Srichand, the grantee, subsequently sought to have the
 area of part of his grant re-demarcated, so as, presumably, to get included
 in it a more productive area from his other khwud-kashta land in order
 to gain more from the grants land-tax exemption. (We should remember
 that in this case, the grant-land, from the beginning, lay entirely in his
predecessors khwud-kdshta holding). In 1626 a chaknama or demarcation
document was prepared, which brought new land of an area of 12 bighas
 18 biswas under the grant, while excluding from it a plot of equal area
 hitherto deemed part of the grant (‘iwaz-i zamln-i chak-i Madan Mohan
jiujj The land so added comprised three plots, bordered, on the east, by
 lands belonging to Madan Mohan and Gopinath temples, on the north,
by the land of the Madan Mohan temple alone, and, in the south, again,
 by land of the Madan Mohan temple plus ‘a tree’. The Madan Mohan
 temple land here probably belonged to the grant of 85 bighas conferred on
 it by Akbar in 1598.
     An interesting entry is the one relating to the western limit of the
 demarcated plots: ‘Land ofthe village ofRajpur, cultivators (karinda): Gadai
 (or Kadai) and Mohan, residents of Dosaich.’ Here we are introduced to
 the category of village-land, which, if untilled or waste, could be disposed
 of by the village headmen. One way of dealing with it was to lease it out
 to paikdsht peasants, i.e. peasants from outside the village.8  9 Gadai and
 Mohan, being residents of a different village, Dosaich (whose site is now
 represented by the mohalla of Dusayat in the town of Vrindavan), had
 only the status of karindas (tillers), and not hereditary peasants. Three
 headmen of the village ofRajpur are named in the document, viz., Bihari,
 Harbans, and Baltok (?), whose consent (qubuliyat) to the demarcation is
duly recorded.
     In 1632, after the death of Srichand, the entire grant of 89 bighas, 9
 biswas, was confirmed by Shahjahan in favour of his ‘heir’, Gosa’indas,
 through a formal^zrman, with the stipulation that the former entitlements
would continue, so that presumably no fresh demarcation (chafe) was called
for.10 From the reign of Shahjahan a set of documents relating to the grant
have survived, issued by officials and jagirdars. The last (of 1657) from

   The curtain falls over the grant for much of the reign of Aurangzeb
(1659-1707), no document relating to it having survived from the first
three decades of his reign. But in 1691, a local official acknowledged
the continued existence of the grant, referring to a parwdna of the local
sadr (officer in charge of grants), where the farmdn of Shahjahan which
confirmed the grant had been cited.11  12 It is possible that the necessity of
such acknowledgement arose because of the Jat insurgency, which had
caused much disturbance in the area.
   Gosaindas, the grant-holder, who apparently lived for a long time,
represented to the amin (revenue-assessment officer) of the pargana of
Mathura that he had lost the original chakndma of the grant, among effects
plundered during ‘the disturbances of the accursed Jat’; thereupon the
amin undertook a fresh demarcation. The document recording it (dated
2 April 1692) has fortunately survived.13 This chakndma (bilingual) fixes
the boundaries of seven plots, all treated as rectangles, with the length
and width multiplied to yield the area. The total area of the plots amounts
to 89 bighas, 10 biswas (as against the official grant area of 89 bighas, 9
biswas). None of the plots correspond to any of the three plots, with a
total area of 12 bighas, 18 biswas, that had been demarcated in 1626, so
that the area under the grant must have had its actual limits considerably
altered in the meantime. It is possible to see that the six plots, Nos. 1 to 6,
were in proximity to each other. Plots Nos. 1 and 3 adjoined Todar Mai’s
garden; Nos. 2 and 3 had on part of their borders the field of Raghunath
Brahman; Nos. 2 and 5 shared boundaries with Lalita Bagh; Nos. 5 and
6 both had the field of Gadai, headman, to their west; and Nos. 2 and 6
were contiguous. The ‘road’ (rah) ran to the east and south of Plot No. 4
(the smallest plot of all, measuring only 11 biswas) and to the west of Plots
1 and 2. Plot 7 was totally separated from the rest, and we will presently
touch on its significance.

    Coming back to the details of the chaknama, it has much more to tell
us about land use and land holdings than the earlier chaknama, dealing
with a much more concentrated area. In describing the boundaries, the
document twice defines plot boundaries by references to the Bagh of
Todar Mai and the Lalita Bagh, as we have just seen. Bagh (bag in Nagari
text) has a broad meaning, covering both flower garden and orchard. It is,
however, unlikely that Raja Todar Mai had laid out a mere orchard to bear
his name; though Lalita Bagh could well have been an orchard. There
were pathways (raty broad enough to set boundaries for the sides of four
of the six plots. The remaining part of the land bordering the grant-area
comprised cultivated fields (kisht), except for two kyaris or prepared beds,
of which one belonged to the grantee, Gosa indas, himself. One piece of
land, possibly untilled, is described simply as ‘land of village Rajpur’.
    The fields bordering Plots 1 to 6 are identified by the names of their
occupants or holders, who number eight in all. As many as three of them
are carefully described as muqaddams (makadam in Nagari) of vill. Rajpur,
one of whom (Kadai or Gadai, who, to judge by the latter form of the
name, could be a Muslim) holds two fields. Raghunath, resident of Rajpur,
was a Brahman and held two fields. Of the remaining four landholders,
three are described as residents of Rajpur and the fourth of Nagla Nagu, a
hamlet that was considered to be situated within Vrindavan.
    The particulars of the seventh plot provided in the chaknama set it
apart from all the other six plots of the grant. First of all, it is placed in
the khadar, the riverine flood-land (here, at Rajpur, to the south-west
of the village at some distance). The khadar is distinguished from the
 upland’ or Bangar, the land beyond the range of river flooding. While the
Khadar may sustain rice cultivation here and there, the floods to which it
is subject at many places tend to remove the alluvium and deposit sand in
its place. It is home, therefore, to scanty and shifting cultivation, while it
does provide grazing for large herds of cattle.14 The latter might have been
the reason why Gosaindas let nearly 10 per cent of the area of his grant be
placed in the khadar.
    His khadar plot had, on its eastern side, the field of one Ganga Ram,
resident of Rajpur. On the west, it had ‘the field(s) of ‘Kunbiwara’. Kunbis
(also called Kurmis) are peasants specializing in intensive agriculture,
especially tending vegetable gardens. The phrase 'kisht of Kunbiwara’
could, therefore, refer to a Kunbi settlement, along with vegetable plots

   14 W. Crooke in The North-Western Provinces of India, London, 1897,
pp. 24-6, provides an interesting description of the ‘khadir tracts in the region
we are concerned with.
184                       Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

cultivated by its members.15 To the north lay ‘the kisht of Chamars (khet
Chamdran in the Braj version) of the village Rajpur’. One infers that
this was land assigned to the Chamars (lit. tanners), for meeting their
subsistence needs while they carried out the lowly work required of them
by the village or the village headmen.16 It will be seen that while land
holders are generally identified by their individual names, no need is felt
for such refinement in the case of Kunbis and Chamars. Either they were
deemed too lowly to be recalled by their names, or they were so numerous
with their small plots that there was not enough space to put in their
names.
   To the south of the Khadar plot lay ‘the kisht of Rahar.’ Rahar cannot
be an individual because in the cases of all the individual owners, the
chaknama mentions the place of residence, which it does not do here. The
Nagari version has "khet Rahar, which confirms the word as read in the
Persian text. It is probable, then, that it too is a caste name and stands for
the Rahwari caste, whose members were quite numerous in the district of
Mathura, to go by the census of 1891, The Rahwaris traditionally tended
camels; and it is probable that they too were allowed use of land in the
khadar to cultivate it for their subsistence.17
   As we have seen, besides the grant originally conferred on the chief
priest of the Madan Mohan temple by Todar Mai,18 the same temple

   16 Crooke, Tribes and Castes, vol. II, pp. 169-94, offers a description of the

            Land Holdings and Land-Control in Village Rajpilr               185

received a grant of 85 bighas in the village of Raj pur by Akbar s farmdn of
 1598. It was confirmed by a parwdna of Asaf Khan in 1633 and by Khalil
Khan in 1651,19 Thereafter the extant documents appear to be silent over
this grant. But in 1732, when Sawai Jai Singh, in whose jdgir the parganas
of Mathura and Sahar now lay, issued an order detailing, village-wise, the
grants held by the adhikaris of the Madan Mohan temple, a total of 174
bighas, 9 biswas, was recorded under the village Rajpur.20 This could only
be made up of 89 bighas, 9 biswas, held under the grant going back to
Todar Mai, and 85 bighas granted by Akbar in 1598, both grants together
yielding, in area, a total of 174 bighas, 9 biswas.
    A parwdna of 5 July 1723 which Raja Gujar Mai, the then jagirdar of
Mathura, issued to revenue officials in charge of village Rajpur survives
in a copy. It refers to this very estate of 174 bighas 9 biswas held in that
village by the Madan Mohan temple by way of madad-i ma‘ash. This
parwdna was obviously issued in the interest of Gusa in Subal Das and
other adhikaris of the temple, since it enjoins the local officials ‘to require
the zamindars to pay the tax (mahsul) on that land to the temple adhikaris’
(Vrindavan Docs., 145). Clearly, this exhortation envisages a situation
where the lands under the madad-i madsh grants were not cultivated
through labourers or even through small rent-paying peasants, but by
substantive tenant-farmers (zamindars), who were expected to pay to the
temple establishment what they would have been obliged to pay to the
government by way of ordinary land-tax had they been cultivating non­
grant land. This is also suggestive of the kinds of arrangements that were
made by holders of substantive areas of grant to get income out of their
estates.
    Besides the grant-lands held by the establishment of the Madan
Mohan temple, there was the early grant received by the priest of the
Gopinath temple of Vrindavan from Emperor Akbar as early as 1568-69,
its area subsequently adjusted to 76 bighas, 2 biswas, in the ilahi scale,
equal to 18.26 hectares. This was confirmed by imperial officials in 1719
and 1722;21 and the land in question therefore, must have remained in

possession of Madho, the original grantee, his successor Jagjivandas, and
then the latter’s successors down to Ramjivan, whose full genealogy is
given in the document issued in 1722. Since these documents have come
from the archives of the Gopinath temple at Vrindavan, it may be assumed
that this family of priests were sevaks of that temple. This may explain
why the chakndma of 1626, for the grant of 89 bighas 9 biswas, then held
by Srichand of Madan Mohan temple, refers to the chak of Gopinath
(temple) lying to the east of the demarcated plots, along with that of
Madan Mohan.
   Over 60 hectares of tax-free land in village Rajpur had thus remained
in the hands of the priests of Madan Mohan and Gopinath temples
throughout the seventeenth century. One may be sure that this would
have given them considerable clout in the village. Other Brahman holders,
like Raghunath, whom we encounter in the chakndma of 1691 as the
possessor of two fields, must also have been encouraged to take their
abode in the village.
   Otherwise, however, the features of the village society found in Arith/
Radhakund [Chapter 8] existed here as well. The chakndma of 1626
names three muqaddams of the village and that of 1692 introduces to us
five, three of them appearing as land-holders. We may infer from these
references that we had here a set of oligarchs, rather than a single village
head, though these are not called panch even in the Braj version of the
chakndma of 1692. It is likely that it was these headmen who determined
who would cultivate the vacant land in the village. As we have noted, we
have found plots of village land being cultivated by two peasants residing in
another village, shown by an entry in the chakndma of 1626; and similarly
an outsider (from Nagla Nagu) used to cultivate a field within the village,
as recorded in the chakndma of 1692. Generally speaking, however, we
learn far less about the village headmen of Rajpur than we do about their
peers in Vrindavan and Radhakund.
GOSA’INS
                           CHAPTER 10



A Documentary History of the Gosa’ins
  of the Chaitanya Sect at Vrindavan,
               to c.1740
                               Irfan Habib




The Vrindavan documents on which we have largely relied in our studies
of aspects of social life and local administration in Braj Bhum during
Mughal times were perhaps largely preserved because their possessors
believed that these were relevant to their claims over properties, priestly
offices, or personal rights. For us, therefore, their contexts become clear
only when we distinguish the interests of the different individuals involved
and the nature and course of such disputes as arose among them. One
way of securing this object has been to reconstruct the biographies of
the individuals concerned from the documents themselves. The present
chapter is the result of such an effort.
   In itself the result can also stand as a modest contribution to the history
of the Chaitanya sect at Vrindavan for some two hundred years. The reader
perusing the appended alphabetically arranged notices of the Gosa’ins and
mendicants of the Chaitanya sect will have to bear with descriptions of
the same facts (and disputes) in the notices of different persons, which,
given this mode of organising our information, cannot be avoided. Yet,
hopefully, the persevering reader may still find many interesting pieces of
information about both the internal history of the sect and about aspects
of local administrative history, land systems, social relations, and the
cultural milieu. A reader looking for traces of a particular individual may
also come across some relevant information here.
    A few further words of explanation seem necessary. The Vrindavan
Goswamis (Gosa’ins) had certain factions based on lines of spiritual
 190                   Braj Bhum in Mughal Times

succession. Of the three major ‘lines’, two were those of Rup and Jiv
Goswami. A Persian document of c.1723 (from collection unspecified),
produced in support of the claims ofJagannath Gosa’in, has helpfully given
us lists of the successors of Rup and Jiv Gosa’in, which are corroborated
by our documents. To these we have added names based on documents
of the post-1723 period. The third line, that of Sanatan, to which the
chief priests of the Madan Mohan temple belonged, given below in the
first column, is reconstructed by us from our documents. The years given
within round brackets are those in which, by our documentary evidence,
the individuals concerned were alive (though not necessarily in occupation
of their holy offices).

because they developed close association with the Amber rulers who in         -

   In the following entries I have tried to give an indication of the
whereabouts of the original documents, especially since sometimes the
provenance may indicate the affiliation of the individual mentioned or warn
us about a possible bias in the document. Thus the Govind-dev documents
generally belong to Rup’s line, and those of the Radha Damodar and
Radha Kund to the line of Jiv Gosa’in. The National Archives documents
appear to have come from a family associated with the Govind-dev temple.
The Madan Mohan temple collection mainly yields documents relating
to the successors of Sanatan Gosain. The IVS collection centres on the
Gopinath temple and its custodians. Unfortunately, the photographs of

of the Amber state even before the year 1707 came to a close.

                  A Documentary History of the Gosa ins                 193

bil.            bilingual (Persian and Nagari/Kaithi)
